Foreword




Communicating Science to the Public: Opportunities and Challenges for the
Asia-Pacific Region is an exciting and very timely contribution that explores
the promotion of the public understanding of science, largely from a regional
perspective. It explores key platforms for communicating science to the public,
shares best practices in science communication from both high-income and lower-
income countries, and underscores the role of science communication initiatives
in fostering national development in low-income countries. The range of platforms
it explores is broad: from conventional science journalism and university-based
public outreach, to the establishment of science centers and the emergence of
new web-based communication, to café scientifiques and science olympiads.
The volume also delves into graduate education in science communication, the
role of learned societies, and the particular problems of science communication
in developing countries. The Pacific Science Association (PSA) is indeed pleased
to have had a small part in encouraging this volume so ably edited by Professor
Leo Tan Wee Hin and Associate Professor R. Subramaniam.
    In the 90+ years since the PSA was founded as an interdisciplinary science
organization with an implicit goal of fostering science communication, the Asia-
Pacific region has undergone dramatic political, demographic, economic, and
environmental change. Science and technology have served as important engines
for many of those transformations. While the Asia-Pacific is in many ways the
most economically dynamic part of the world, it also faces huge challenges in terms
of development, human security, and environmental sustainability. How these
challenges are met will directly impact the entire globe. As the global community
grapples with a new understanding of the relationship between sustainability
and development, a wide range of stakeholders must be engaged in the debate.
Effective science communication is absolutely critical for this effort, and it is for
this reason that the publication of this volume is particularly timely.
    Last year’s Rio+20 Conference on Sustainable Development resulted in
Future Earth – a new 10-year international architecture for global change research
organized under the auspices of the International Council for Science (ICSU) and
the International Social Science Council (ISSC). The target date for the Millennium

                                                                                   v
vi                                                                                   Foreword


Development Goals (MDGs) is also fast approaching, and the United Nations
has released the High-Level Panel report on the Post-2015 Development Agenda
and appointed a Science Advisory Board to the UN Secretary General. That
new report1 explicitly links environmental targets and development goals and
acknowledges the critical role of science and technology. The task is to develop
a set of sustainable development goals (SDGs) that will carry the implementation
of the MDGs forward, especially the overarching goal of poverty eradication,
and address the integration of the three dimensions of sustainable development –
economic, social, and environmental.
   The global community faces a daunting set of challenges with respect
to sustainability. The science and technology community has a critical role to
play in meeting this set of challenges. To do so, scientists must find ways to
communicate more effectively and reach a much broader range of stakeholders.
The chapters in this volume suggest some practical and innovative ways to do
this. Scientific literacy must be enhanced, and science communication can be
used to help those outside the science community to understand how scientific
knowledge can facilitate better decision-making. Science itself must become more
open to fruitful collaborations not just across disciplines and across national
boundaries but also between the science and nonscience communities in an effort
to become truly transdisciplinary: forming an inclusive global partnership with
not only governments but multilateral institutions, businesses, academia, private
philanthropic entities, as well as all of civil society – people living in poverty,
indigenous and local communities, women, persons with disabilities, and other
historically marginalized groups. The lessons contained in this volume suggest
ways to engage these multiple stakeholders.

Honolulu, HI, USA                                                          Nancy D. Lewis

Preface




In recent times, science communication has become an important area of academic
activity. It rides on various platforms to achieve its objectives – for example,
science journalism, science centers, and outreach activities by institutions of higher
learning. While the field of science communication is generally well established
in the developed world, the situation in many developing countries is far from
satisfactory. Given the pervasive influence of science and technology in today’s
society, its influence will only increase in the years to come as the world becomes
more globalized and the economies of countries become more interlinked.
   With a view toward exploring the multifaceted dimensions of science commu-
nication, especially from the point of view of developing countries, this book was
conceptualized. The book thus seeks to address the challenges of communicating
science to the public in the developing world. We have taken a broad interpretation
of the term “science communication,” and thus a multiplicity of approaches for
communicating science to the public is explored. Though the book title has a sub-
line emphasizing the Asia-Pacific region, the issues explored are relevant to devel-
oping countries irrespective of region.
   Chapters were commissioned from scholars working in the areas of communi-
cation of science to the public or who have experience in this area. Each chapter
was subjected to peer review. A total of 17 chapters are presented in this book. They
represent the efforts of 27 authors from eight countries: Australia, Bangladesh,
India, Ireland, New Zealand, the USA, Singapore, and South Africa – a truly
international effort. The diverse perspectives offered from the lens of authors in
different cultural contexts serve to enrich the value of this publication further. For
convenience, the chapters are divided into various sections.
   A book of this nature would not have been possible without the efforts of
various parties. We owe a debt of gratitude to many people. We thank Professor
Nancy Lewis, President of the Pacific Science Association in Hawaii, for her
encouragement and support for us to work on this book project. The folks in
Springer have been especially helpful – particular mention must be made of
acquisitions editor, Ms Bernadette Ohmer, for her patience and encouragement in


                                                                                   vii

the course of our working on this book. We also thank all authors for their
cooperation and support in the course of producing this book.
   It is our hope that the publication of this book will spur more efforts in
communicating science to the public in developing countries.

  Part I
Schools
Promoting Science Literacy via Science
Journalism: Issues and Challenges

Billy McClune and Ruth Jarman




Abstract Science journalism is the source of much of the science an individual
will encounter beyond formal education. Science-based media reports, which might
have been associated with informal education, are increasingly becoming incorpo-
rated into formal school contexts. Unlike science textbooks, the science reported in
the news is often tentative and sometimes contested. It can involve difficult socio-
scientific issues. Descriptors of ‘science literacy’ generally include reading and
responding critically to media reports of science. The challenge of using science-
based news effectively encourages teachers to reassess their knowledge and peda-
gogical practices.
   In addition to creating interest in science and making links beyond the class-
room, news media can be used to introduce pupils to elements of science enquiry
and teachers can promote basic literacy and critical reading skills through system-
atic and imaginative use of media reports with a science component.
   This chapter explores the knowledge, skills and attitudes that underpin the use of
science journalism in the classroom. The unique characteristics and constraints of
science journalism that influence the way science is presented and perceived are
considered, and the importance of media awareness as a foundation for critical
reading of science news is argued. Finally the characteristics of teaching
programmes to support critical engagement with science-based media reports are
outlined and the opportunities for cross-curricular initiatives highlighted.

Keywords Science literacy • Science journalism • Science communication •
Science teaching • News media • Cross curricular teaching • Interdisciplinary
learning • Critical reading




B. McClune (*) • R. Jarman
School of Education, Queen’s University Belfast, Belfast, UK
e-mail: w.mcclune@qub.ac.uk; r.jarman@qub.ac.uk

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,     3
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_1, © Springer Netherlands 2014
4                                                                 B. McClune and R. Jarman


Introduction

When Usain Bolt won ‘gold’ in the 100m and 200m sprints at the Beijing Olympics
in 2008, the media reports of this achievement ensured that he became a sporting
celebrity and his celebration pose is an iconic image recognised across the world.
His attempt in the London 2012 Olympic games to be the first man in history to
repeat this was eagerly anticipated and his achievement reported around the world.
This occasion that had global media appeal may seem an unlikely, but nevertheless
useful, starting point for this chapter.
    Comparing science journalism with the way a major sporting event is reported
can help us appreciate some of the achievements and challenges of reporting
science in the news media. By considering sports journalism and its ability to
focus our attention and shape our expectations, we can make a number of observa-
tions that may provide insights into the topic of this chapter, i.e. ‘science journal-
ism’ and its relationship with ‘science literacy’.
    We will return to this illustration later in the chapter but first a couple of points to
note from the example above. Firstly, of the many events and sporting achieve-
ments of that day, Usain Bolt’s was newsworthy because it was unique, a ‘world
first’, and so it was selected for reporting. Secondly, of the many journalists around
the world who reported on this achievement in local and national media, only some
will be specialist sports reporters, and only a few of these may have been especially
qualified to report on athletics in general or sprinting in particular. Fewer still will
have been in the stadium to witness the event firsthand. It is likely that most reports
were constructed by gathering information from a number of sources to make a
story. In these areas, sports journalism is not unique. All news reporting is correctly
described as a process of selection and construction often carried out by individuals
with journalistic skills but who may lack specialist knowledge in the specific field in
which they are reporting. This is particularly true of science journalism.
    The following section will outline those aspects of science literacy that science
journalism can promote and consider some of the features of science journalism that
the scientifically literate reader should take note of. If science journalism challenges
the audience to make a critical response, then science literacy should equip the
reader to make that response. But first we should briefly address the fundamental
question: Why use newspapers in science education?



Science Journalism in Education

Most journalists, when asked about the purpose of news, would suggest that they
write to inform, interpret, entertain and sometimes persuade their readers. Hansen
(1994), writing about science journalism, notes that newspapers do not set out to
educate the public or promote scientific literacy. However, news reports when used
appropriately can help teachers achieve both of these goals and in addition
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges           5


contribute to the development of general literacy through science. Teachers who
use science-related news (Jarman & McClune, 2002; Kachan, Guilbert & Bisanz,
2006) suggest that it can be used to:
• Help students learn about the role of science in everyday life by providing access
  to topical science issues and so demonstrate relevance
• Make explicit links between school science and the world beyond the classroom
  as a context to apply subject knowledge and so consolidate learning
• Teach about science-related issues with a social dimension and so help students
  become aware of the impact science can have on society
• Develop students’ critical reading skills and so contribute to citizenship educa-
  tion by empowering students to engage with and respond critically to science
  issues that affect them personally.
   Although not intended as a science textbook, science news can also be used to
promote learning of science content knowledge. Many science-related news reports
are based on topics commonly found on the secondary school curriculum. News
reports can be a valuable resource when introducing, applying and consolidating
science content knowledge.
   Increasingly programmes of study in science expect pupils to engage with
controversial issues in science that have local, national or global consequences.
News reports about, e.g. the environmental impact of a wind farm or the ethical
concerns about genetically modified material entering the food chain, provide
excellent contexts for learning.
   Science news can also contribute to the promotion of general literacy. Opportu-
nities for pupils to engage with science journalism in the curriculum can encourage
the development of basic literacy skills such as reading and comprehension,
allowing students to access information. In addition, many teachers value news
reports to develop students’ capability to read critically, make informed decisions
and communicate effectively in tasks that require them to work cooperatively with
others.
   As a resource, news reports are an almost inexhaustible supply of topical
material. Since no one is compelled to read a newspaper, science news needs to
be well written to attract and hold the attention of the reader. These attributes are
equally important in the classroom and teachers can capitalise on them. Further-
more, in using science news in the classroom, teachers recognise that they are
preparing students for life beyond formal education when much of the science they
encounter will be in the news media. Science news reporting is recognised as a
unique genre and students need to appreciate how science is presented in the news if
they are to read it critically. The theme of this chapter is print media and in
particular news reporting.
6                                                                        B. McClune and R. Jarman


Science Literacy, Science Journalism and Media Awareness

Science-related news is widely reported in the media. Science journalism is an
important resource in free-choice or informal science learning. Writing about
science in the popular press, Zimmerman notes that:
    . . .rich sources of information about science are readily available in the popular visual and
    print media, on the Internet, in essays or expository texts written for the general public, and
    in the writing of scientists. (Zimmerman, Bisanz, Bisanz, Klein, & Klein, 2001, p. 55)

    The effect on science learning of increasing access to science journalism has
attracted the interest of researchers focusing on young people and their perceptions
and understandings of issues related to science-based news reports (e.g. Korpan,
Bisanz, Bisanz, & Henderson, 1997; Norris, Phillips, & Korpan, 2003; Phillips &
Norris, 1999; Ratcliffe, 1999). Other studies have focused on teachers and their
perceptions and the use of science-based news reports (e.g. Jarman & McClune,
2002, 2003, 2004; Kachan et al., 2006; McClune & Jarman, 2001).
    This interest in science journalism was fuelled by the ongoing discussion around
the nature of science literacy and, in particular, the increasing support for the view
that there should be a place for science in the media in the curriculum. Hence, as
Zimmerman continued:
    Curricular reform designed to include a focus on learning to read, comprehend, and
    critically evaluate materials from the diverse and well developed genres of scientific
    writing would be of considerable benefit to students who are expected to become effective
    and scientifically literate citizens. (Zimmerman et al., 2001, p. 55)




Science Literacy

Much has been written about science literacy and numerous definitions proposed.
Some focus on science content knowledge, and others emphasise science for
citizenship. Interestingly, across the spectrum of views expressed, the link between
science journalism and science literacy has been a consistent and generally
uncontested element.
    Science in the news media is one of several informal contexts where people
engage with science. This chapter focuses on the reader’s ability to respond
critically to science in this context. The profile of science journalism as an element
in scientific literacy had been raised by recent trends in curricular renewal. Docu-
mentation underpinning science provision in many countries recognises that one
aim of science education is to promote critical engagement with science in the
media. This is exemplified by the newly developed US science education standards
(National Research Council, 2012, p. 73), where it is proposed that, by grade
12, students should be able to ‘read media reports of science or technology in a
critical manner so as to identify their strengths and weaknesses’. Comparable
statements of intent are found in many curricular documents and statements of
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges                         7


national standards. In addition, the inclusion of media-related assessment elements
in international comparison studies such as the Trends in International Mathematics
and Science Study (TIMMS) and the Programme of International Assessment
(PISA) is an indicator of the growing appreciation of the importance of media-
related science.
    This focus on science in the media is an acknowledgement that an education in
science should prepare students to engage with science in the contexts they will
encounter in later life. By promoting the capability and aptitude to make a critical
response to science in the media, individuals are potentially equipped to make a
personal response to science that may have an impact on, e.g. their health or leisure
interests. In addition, as citizens they would be better able to contribute to commu-
nity awareness of, and engagement with, policy or practice in relation to, e.g. the
environment or other science-related issues of the day. A critical capability with
respect to science in the media helps individuals and communities to avoid either
ignorance of science and the consequent inability to scrutinise scientific exploits or
unwarranted caution that stifles valuable and reasonable scientific enterprise.
    We live in news-rich societies where science-related issues are regularly on the
news agenda, so the inclination to engage with, evaluate and respond critically to
science news is a desirable attribute. The capability for critical engagement with
science in the media is recognised as one indicator of science literacy. This poses
two questions for science educators and those involved in science communication.
What knowledge, skills and attitudes underpin the capability to respond critically to
science journalism? And how are these promoted?




Science Journalism

   .. some of the most important science debates of our times have been conducted on the front
   pages and in the headlines of the mainstream news.

This quotation from the UK-based Science and Media Expert Group (SMEG)
(2010, p. 3) highlights what might be called ‘high profile’ science; however, a
great deal of science journalism dealing with health, diet, weather and the environ-
ment (to name a few) along with innovations from all branches of science enquiry
also occupy the less prominent inside pages of the popular press and feature in
broadcast media. Science journalism is a significant source of science information.
For most people beyond the stage of formal education, science-related matters are
mainly encountered through a combination of print media, broadcasting and the
Internet. There is a growing interest among the science education community and
science communicators to use the wide range of science-related news reports to do
more than catch pupils’ attention or demonstrate the relevance of science. They
suggest that students need to learn to read science news with a critical eye. When
viewed critically, news reports often provide a context to look in depth at what are
often controversial socio-scientific issues or provide insights into the nature of
8                                                            B. McClune and R. Jarman


practice and enquiry in the scientific community. This ‘critical capability’ is seen
not only as a way into science in the media but is itself a goal of science education.



A Distinctive Genre

Science journalism is a distinctive type of science writing. In a number of crucial
elements, news reports are unlike the writing in science textbooks that most people
have experienced. Media outlets thrive on breaking news and science researchers
also covet the recognition that comes with being first. Science is increasingly
reported at early stages of a study, and consequently science news is often tentative
and may have a limited evidence base. It is sometimes contested and liable to
change. Anticipated outcomes may be speculative and may not be realised in the
timescale that the reader expects. News reports are intended for a number of
purposes including to attract an audience and entertain readers. Consequently, it
is sometimes prone to sensationalism and unwarranted certainty; it may suggest
improbable applications and extreme implications (Jarman & McClune, 2010). As a
result, the knowledge, skills and approaches that need to be harnessed for critical
reading of this type of text are interdisciplinary in nature and often lie outside the
usual practice of many science teachers more familiar with handling text with a
high degree of certainty where facts are uncontested and there is little room for
opinion.



News Values in Science Journalism

The newsworthiness of a story is often based on the combination of what journalists
recognise as ‘news values’. Among these, ‘timeliness’ is important; if a journalist’s
report is delayed, the story may cease to be news so journalists work to tight
deadlines. Journalists look for a story with a prominent individual or the fascination
factor that can be associated with uniqueness, e.g. of a world first. They also value
stories that contain emotion and human interest. Events that impact a large number
of people or are relevant and of local interest are reported. Finally, it helps if the
story is unambiguous and able to be told clearly.
   Take a moment to list how many of these features come together in the story of
Usain Bolt and consider how a science story might struggle to meet these selection
criteria. Nevertheless, for a science story to be selected and reported, it needs to
match the similar standards of newsworthiness. This can influence the type of
science story chosen and the way it is reported. Hence, all areas of science are
not equally prominent in the news. Reports about health and environment are often
favoured because they are perceived to be relevant to a larger audience. Science is
often presented as a series of discoveries or breakthroughs, and the nature of
research activity is often obscured when there is little sense of the underpinning
incremental work over extended time periods.
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges              9


    It is important to consider why some science-related events qualify for inclusion
in the news. Three examples of science journalism with similar global media appeal
will illustrate the point: the search for the Higgs boson, the tsunami that hit Japan in
2011 and the landing of the Mars Rover Curiosity in 2012.
    The Large Hadron Collider (LHC) had been the focus of several news stories.
The Higgs boson holds a fascination for the public and is often described by
journalists as the ‘God Particle’. In this case, a sense of awe and wonder contributes
to newsworthiness. However, the story is complex and difficult to tell simply.
Science-based reports linked to the tsunami are examples of science journalism
where news values, e.g. relating to ‘human interest’ and ‘impact’, are ‘co-opted’ to
give the science story wider appeal. The human interest and emotion associated
with these events support a science background story that, in the case of the
tsunami, prompted subsequent science-related reports about nuclear power and
plate tectonics. Other news values come into play; the story had impact – it affected
a large number of people.
    Not all stories have global interest. Science journalism may also rely on other
news values such as relevance and local interest. In every case, the embedded
values of the news organisation and its journalists also influence how the story is
told. These often go unnoticed but to the critical reader they are evident in the tone
of the headline or the particular emphasis of the story or the use of emotive and
persuasive language in the text. Is the Mars Rover presented as an unwarranted
expensive adventure or a great technical triumph? Would failure make a bigger
news story than a successful landing?
    It is good practice in relation to this aspect of media awareness to access news
reports of the same topic from different news sources and to compare the presen-
tation of the science story.


Media Limitations

All reports in print media are constrained by column space or in the case of
broadcast media reporting time. This limitation is perhaps less evident in
web-based sources. In print media, the need to have text which can be quickly
reduced by an editor, as priorities change and stories are rescheduled and some-
times resized, has resulted in a writing style which places the conclusion in the
opening lines of the story to allow the final sentences to be cut without loss of
meaning. This limited space can lead to a lack of detail and the omission of
information that may be important to the scientist, science communicator or critical
reader. Here again, it can be enlightening to look for the same story reported in
different news outlets. Taking this approach, news reports often act as starting
points for further study. The news deadline imposes additional constraints on the
journalist, who often depends heavily on a press release. Few reporters are ‘spe-
cialist science correspondents’; many are ‘generalists’ with limited background
knowledge of the topic they are reporting. Even the ‘specialist reporter’ will have
limited expertise across the wide range of science topics. Nevertheless, when
10                                                                 B. McClune and R. Jarman


commenting on the value of specialist reporting, Williams and Clifford (2009) note
differences between the reports from specialists and generalists and observe that
specialists favour scientists over non-scientists as sources and are more likely to use
primary reference to scientific evidence. In addition, news reports are often written
quickly with limited time to consult a range of sources or even check facts. The
pace at which news is produced can sometimes be a source of error.
   It is good practice to encourage students to always ask questions about the
author, the sources and the media context: Who wrote the news story? Are credible
sources used? What newspaper does it appear in? The critical reader should
recognise that it is important to consider who wrote the news and how much
coverage it was given.



Media Awareness

It could be argued, in relation to science in the news media, that there is a tension
between the nature of science, as many people perceive it, and the medium that it is
being used to present it. Hence, in policy documents and curricular materials that
address science in the media, ‘criticality’ or ‘critical reading’ is a recurring theme.
    Most people associate science, particularly as they experienced it in school, with
certainty and established knowledge as presented in science textbooks written by
experts in the subject. Consequently pupils learning science do not expect to be
invited to ‘critically review’ the text. That terminology, it is assumed, belongs in
other curricular areas. Media awareness places science literacy in a cross-curricular
context.
    Media awareness in this context involves bringing together the understanding of
science with knowledge of news, newspapers and journalism, so as to help make an
informed response to science journalism but with the emphasis on media. Gregory
and Miller (1998, p. 106) writing about science communication in the media
emphasise this point when they suggest:
     . . . understanding science-in-the-media has something to do with understanding media
     science, but mostly it is about understanding media.

    In this context, it may be helpful to think of ‘understanding media’ in terms of a
number of media strands: understanding journalistic practices, the nature of news
and the characteristics of newspaper reports.
    Journalists refer to the ‘construction’ of the news report and acknowledge the
constraints that influence this. They highlight how the customs and practice of the
news media guide the use of language and images and determine which sources are
influential and how they are used.
    Awareness of the nature of news itself is crucial to understanding science
journalism. News is conceived when a combination of newsworthy characteristics
catches the attention of a journalist or editor. As it is developed, its substance is
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges             11


dependant on the available sources, and the interests and values of individuals and
organisations who construct it influence its form to varying degrees.
    Finally an understanding of the characteristics of news reports and how these can
influence the reader is important. For example, when, in the interests of balance,
news reports offer alternative views, they may not reflect the relative strength of
these views within the science community, but the reader may give alternative
viewpoints equal weight. Similarly, eye-catching headlines and emotive language
designed to catch the attention of the audience may have the effect of distorting a
news story by simplifying or sensationalising the science.
    It is good practice and an important starting point to encourage pupils to
recognise that news stories are primarily intended to be informative but that they
also interpret reality to entertain and sometimes persuade the reader. The critical
reader should look for evidence of persuasive writing or consider if the author
promotes one side of an argument.




Issues and Challenges

The issues and challenges associated with developing science literacy through
science journalism relate to the key elements of knowledge, skills and attitudes
that underpin the development of critical capability and, in particular, how these
can be identified and promoted within appropriate curricular frameworks. The
following sections will explore in more detail the issues and challenges associated
with promoting science literacy in this context.
    Science journalism, no matter how good it is, may not in itself promote science
literacy but is a valuable resource in achieving this goal. It is by nature a product
intended for ‘free-choice’ learning. The journalist and editor do much to make the
product appealing in the hope of attracting an audience. For most people, to develop
critical reading skills requires a degree of mediation. Most commonly that will be a
teacher working in a context where engagement with science in the media is
encouraged. Consequently, the promotion of science literacy in formal education
will provide the backdrop for the remainder of this chapter.
    When invited to comment on science-related news, teachers often express a
combination of positive and negative views. Figure 1. illustrates some of the words
typically found in teachers’ descriptions and the views they reflect.
    The view that science reports do not provide enough detail or are exaggerated or
unbalanced in some way is often expressed. On the positive side, science news
reports are seen as relevant, able to catch pupils’ attention and engage their interest.
Some teachers find the opportunity to address socio-scientific issues valuable, while
others avoid this. The challenge is to see how each of these features associated with
science news can make a positive contribution to promoting science literacy. The
task is then to develop a pedagogy that is grounded in and supports appropriate
learning intentions.
12                                                                    B. McClune and R. Jarman


        Teachers often describe science in the news as:

                              Most news reports are interesting, relevant and well
         Engaging
                              written


                              Science presented in the media is often uncertain and
         Uncertain
                              sometimes contested


                              Science is sometimes linked to socio-scientific issues
         Controversial
                              which may be controversial and challenging


                              Some science topics and disciplines are covered more
         Limited
                              frequently than others


                              The news report may not provide the reader with
         Inadequate
                              sufficient information about the study or the topic


                              Newspapers may support or campaign for a particular
         Unbalanced
                              viewpoint


                              Access to the news may depend on skills associated
         Interdisciplinary
                              with curricular areas other than science


Fig. 1 Issues associated with science in the news




   Science-related news is intended to be attention grabbing and relevant. The style
of writing, the level of language and the focus on human interest often ensure that
science-related news is engaging. It links science to the world beyond the class-
room. It can demonstrate the relevance and applicability of science in everyday
contexts. Engagement is a first step to promoting science literacy and teachers can
take advantage of these characteristics to encourage young people to engage with
science in the public domain.
   It may be good practice from time to time to design tasks that require students to
seek out science news, e.g. as a class to compile a weekly or monthly record
of news.
   Science presented in the media is often contested, uncertain and open to chal-
lenge. Rather than consider this a disadvantage, science journalism can be seen as
one of the few opportunities teachers have to help pupils engage with current
research presented in language that is accessible. It is also an opportunity to
appreciate the uncertainty inherent in doing science.
   It is good practice to distinguish between media reports and textbook science, so
that pupils can appreciate and handle uncertainty associated with media reports.
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges           13


Pupils need to look for limiting clauses, distinguish between fact and opinion and
take note of reference to ‘the need for further study or research’.
   Science reported in the media is sometimes controversial, often forcing teachers
and pupils to confront difficult questions and socio-scientific issues which take
them beyond science into other areas of human thought and endeavour. In doing so
pupils can become aware of how science impacts society and have first-hand
experience of expressing their views about science issues that affect them or their
local community.
   It is good practice to consider carefully the potential impact of the issues raised
by science. These may affect pupils and teachers at a personal level and need to be
handled sensitively. They often raise issues that overlap with other subject areas
and might benefit from an interdisciplinary approach.
   Teachers who might hope to link every curriculum topic to a science news story
will probably be disappointed. Science journalism may give a distorted impression
of the spectrum of activity in the scientific community. Biological sciences tend to
dominate the news and stories are often reported from human-interest perspective.
Where news reports do link with curriculum topics, teachers and pupils are often
frustrated by a lack of detail. Science news is subject to constraints; consequently,
important information relating to the credibility of the sources, the uncertainty of
the findings or the limitations of study may be omitted due to limited time or word
length. It is important not to expect too much from a news report and to plan to use
even the limitations as teaching points. Appropriate science stories can be a
springboard to further study. Recognising the importance of what has been omitted
from the report is a valuable science literacy skill.
   There is often a perception that science news is biased, contending for or against,
e.g. global warming or the introduction of genetic modifications to the food chain.
While some obvious examples of newspapers adopting a campaigning stance do
exist, the values and viewpoints of the journalists and news organisations are
usually integrated more subtly. In promoting scientific literacy, there is an oppor-
tunity for teachers to illustrate how news, including science news, is not value-free
and it may reflect the perspectives of the news organisation, the journalist and the
media audience. This is good practice.
   Working with science news in the classroom highlights the overlap between
traditionally separate subjects in the curriculum. Science overlaps with language
arts (mother tongue instruction) and the development of literacy providing oppor-
tunities for reading, writing, talking and listening. In addition, very specific capa-
bilities in comprehension and use of language are needed. Generalist teachers
working with intermediate-level pupils may welcome this opportunity. Teachers
more accustomed to subject-specific teaching may lack confidence and competence
and find this subject overlap challenging. It would be good practice to consider how
interdisciplinary aspects of media-related science could form the basis for cross-
curricular collaboration.
14                                                          B. McClune and R. Jarman


Pedagogical Challenges

When reviewing findings of their study of pupils’ responses to media reports, Norris
et al. (2003) suggested that at that time science education did not adequately equip
young people to engage critically with science-related news. Subsequent studies
(McClune & Jarman, 2010, 2011) identified core elements of teaching to support
critical engagement with science-related news. That research would suggest that the
development of critical capability is dependant on teachers:
• Willingness to direct pupils to appropriate examples of science-related news by
  integrating these within programmes of study
• Ability to identify appropriate learning intentions
• Confidence and competence to explore with pupils, media-related issues in
  science contexts through well-designed learning experiences
• Capacity to facilitate interdisciplinary approaches to critical reading through
  cross-curricular collaboration where appropriate.


Rationale

Teachers’ motivation to integrate science-related news into programmes of study
may be influenced by a number of factors. It may be based on positive experiences
of learning stimulated by the use of science-related news in the classroom:
i.e. teachers use science news because it works. Some teachers want to promote
science-related news because of their commitment to the underlying ideology of
citizen empowerment. For others the need to address externally imposed assess-
ment goals may be the main driver. In the absence of an appropriate balance
between these three, the assessment driver has the potential to distort the way
science news is handled.
    The challenge in initial teacher education and professional development is to
provide a rationale for, and experience of, the use of science journalism to enhance
learning. In addition, curriculum planners must seek assessment strategies that
encourage a broad view of critical reading and thinking if the use of science
journalism is to become embedded in the science programme of study and linked
effectively to other relevant curriculum areas.


Programmes of Study

There are many examples of individual teachers using newspapers in interesting
and innovative ways to address specific learning goals. When reviewing teachers
use of science-related news, McClune and Jarman (2012) illustrate some of the
ways teachers have used science-related news to bridge the gap between science
teaching and the world beyond the classroom, to learn about subject-specific
content or science processes and to highlight socio-scientific issues and to support
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges                       15


higher-order thinking. However, they suggest that the majority of teachers who use
science-related news are likely to do so in a casual rather than systematic way,
focusing on a limited number of learning goals with demonstrating the links beyond
the classroom being top of the list. Commenting on studies of school-based use of
news, McClune and Jarman (2012, p. 20) suggested that:
   neither media awareness nor critical reading were strongly embraced as potential learning
   goals.

   This would suggest that one of the challenges for teachers, teacher educators and
those involved in curriculum development is to plan science programmes that adopt
a systematic approach to addressing a comprehensive list of learning intentions that
were teacher generated and evidence based. The key elements of such a programme
should aim to use science journalism to promote:
• Scientific knowledge – the development of content and procedural knowledge
  and an awareness of practice within the science community
• The development of media awareness – understanding of how knowledge of the
  media, and the presentation of science in the media, may equip the audience to
  make judgements and draw conclusions
• The acquisition of literacy skills – recognition of the specific challenges associ-
  ated with science in the media and identification of the necessary literacy skills
• Discerning habits of mind – attitudes which individuals rely on as they encounter
  science-related media influence their interpretation of and responses to it.
   These key themes in programme planning and potential learning experiences are
explored further in a number of media-based resource materials and science
research literature (Jarman & McClune, 2007; McClune & Jarman, 2011, 2012).
Science teacher organisations in the UK and USA (the Association for Science
Education (ASE) and the National Science Teachers Association (NSTA)) have
published helpful resources for teachers. These highlight different approaches to the
use of science media in classroom contexts (Jarman & McClune, 2011; Saul,
Kohnen, Newman, & Pearce, 2012).



Cross-Curricular Collaboration

Science journalism is an authentic context in which the shared interests of specialist
science teachers wishing to promote science literacy and English teachers’ interests
in media awareness and critical reading come together. This is likely to challenge
the confidence and competence of specialist teachers from each discipline and put
them in a position where each has something to learn from the other. Collaborative
working would seem to be an obvious solution but this can take different forms.
   Teachers wishing to retain a high level of independence might look for encour-
agement and support from their colleagues in the form of shared expertise and
insights into pupils’ experiences in different subjects. However, pupils are unlikely
to be aware of this level of collaboration. At the other end of the spectrum is a high
16                                                           B. McClune and R. Jarman


level of interdependence; this might see combined teaching and shared resources
and increased pupil awareness of the collaborative process. Between these extremes
are various approaches that may involve working towards shared learning outcomes
or coordination of separate teaching programmes.
    The issues for generalist teachers are different. For them the challenge may be to
recognise in science journalism the potential for subject-specific development in
areas of science outside their experience. Opportunities for interdisciplinary learn-
ing may be overlooked. Consequently they may benefit from consulting specialist
teachers to ensure that they make appropriate use of the opportunities offered by the
science topics covered in the press and have appropriate subject knowledge to
support the anticipated science learning.
    While some teachers may be reluctant to grasp the interdisciplinary nettle, it is
important to recognise that the view of science literacy being promoted here
expects pupils to bring together skills and knowledge from different curricular
areas. Examples of interdisciplinary approaches to science journalism and some
of the challenges are discussed more fully elsewhere (Alexander, Walsh, Jarman, &
McClune, 2008; McClune, Alexander, & Jarman, 2012.)



Contextualising the Use of Science Journalism

The growth of interest in science in the media is evident in many countries but not
flourishing everywhere equally. Regions and countries with different cultural,
social and economic histories produce different and unique educational contexts.
In recent years, developed economies such as in Europe and North America have
approached the challenge of science literacy by initiating policy and curriculum
renewal, supported by initiatives in initial teacher education and professional
development, which has tapped into well-tried support mechanisms and funding
streams. This level of support will not be universally available. In seeking to
appreciate the contextualised nature of the challenge of engaging with media-
based science, some important context-specific questions might be asked. It
would be important to know what standing science journalism has in the curriculum
and among teachers and to recognise the significant constraints in any education
system.



The Standing of Science Journalism

While many science teachers acknowledge that science journalism may be a
stimulus for learning, it is often regarded as supplementary material rather than a
core resource that is integrated into the curriculum and programmes of study. In
seeking to appreciate why many teachers have a limited view of science journalism,
some points highlighted earlier are important. It was noted that science reported in
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges              17


the media does not fit with the traditional view of science text. Teachers, who are
familiar with textbooks that are factually accurate and present a body of knowledge
with certainty, may be taken aback by the appearance in the classroom of science-
based media text as teaching resources that present science which is often tentative,
sometimes contested, and occasionally inaccurate or at times superficial in its
handling of the supporting science context. Teachers’ view of what is appropriate
in the classroom reflects their view of science knowledge and the purpose of
education in science.
    The key to understanding and valuing science journalism in education in any
context is to appreciate that it is written primarily to entertain not to educate. Hence,
teachers using science journalism are adapting text written for one purpose to
achieve different ends. Teachers sometimes struggle to reconcile uncertainty in
media science with other forms of science writing. Interestingly it is the uncertainty
that makes it a valuable source of learning for science literacy and provides
opportunity to promote the importance of critical reading skills.
    The challenge that media science presents to teachers’ traditional views of
science text can be strongly influenced by the social and cultural context. Therefore,
in a climate of policy change and curriculum renewal where a broad view of science
literacy is promoted, including access to science in the media, the resulting curric-
ulum and professional development create a climate where science journalism may
be viewed positively. The absence of these drivers may hinder or stifle interest in
science journalism.




Constraints

There are a number of issues that constrain teachers’ readiness to incorporate
science journalism into their teaching. The level of teacher awareness and the
degree of system inertia in terms of curriculum and administration are important.
If teachers are to embrace the challenges and opportunities presented by science-
based media, they need to be convinced of the value of the learning outcomes for
their pupils. The education system within which they work must also value and
reward these outcomes and finally there must be access to appropriate resources.
The impact of these constraints may depend on the political and economic context.
    The potential benefits of science journalism in science education and exemplars
of its use are the subject of research in science education; however, the transition
from research to practice is problematic. Teacher awareness of these issues can be
linked to teacher education. The extent to which a broad view of science literacy is a
priority in initial teacher education and ongoing professional development will have
a direct impact on teachers’ level of awareness. The emphasis placed to this aspect
of science education will in turn relate to policy and curriculum priorities. Where
the system is flexible, open to change or in a phase of curricular renewal and where
this is supported by appropriate professional development and underpinned by
18                                                           B. McClune and R. Jarman


consistent policy decisions, the prospects for raising the profile of science journal-
ism are good.
    As noted previously, access to appropriate resources may also be a limiting
factor. While resources and teachers’ guides aimed at illustrating the use of science
media are available, these are intended as exemplars. If the use of science journal-
ism is to be sustained and imbedded in the curriculum, then teachers will need
access to the media and in particular local sources of science news that are relevant
to their context. Print media is a valuable source of science stories and increasingly
these are accessible via the Internet. While this can give the teacher global reach,
more importantly, it can be used to source local and regional science news that
teachers might otherwise find difficult to access. The benefit of regionally signif-
icant news is that it is likely to make a strong connection with the audience and be
relevant to the social and cultural setting. This appreciation of audience is well
illustrated in the publicity for one news website specialising in regional science
news, ‘Reporting about Africa Science with an African Eye’ is used to promote
‘Africa Science News’. Similar websites embrace the same sentiment and provide
online video-, audio- and text-based news in many regions.
    In his discussion paper for the World Bank, ‘Pathways to change’, Verspoor
(1989) focused on the characteristics of successful education programmes in devel-
oping countries. Their observations remain pertinent to this context and the
programmes that might support the implementation of initiatives to promote sci-
ence journalism in the Asia-Pacific and similar regions. They suggested that
successful strategies did not limit their focus to teachers and classrooms. In
addition, they sought to gain support and commitment from administrators and
those responsible for education management. Securing appropriate educational
materials and resources, planning for curriculum development and ensuring rele-
vant teacher education were all essential.



Conclusion

Science news is widely seen as a good resource to make links to science beyond the
classroom and demonstrate relevance of science. Advocates for science journalism
and its place in science education have bigger aims in mind. They see it as a
valuable resource for promoting science literacy by using science-related news
reports to develop in pupils the ability to respond critically to news with a science
component. However, while this aim is supported in curriculum documentation and
policy statements about science education, it is not yet matched by practice that is
fully embedded in the culture of science teaching. There is potential for develop-
ment of pedagogy, and work needs to be done in relation to school-based
programme development, initial teacher education and continuing professional
development.
Promoting Science Literacy via Science Journalism: Issues and Challenges                        19


   Appropriate ongoing professional development will be important to give
teachers confidence and develop their competence to use science-related journalism
systematically as a resource in promoting scientific literacy.
   Promoting critical capacity among students will require individuals to be
exposed to appropriate developmental teaching programmes. These will need to
take account of research that indicates specific learning challenges which individ-
uals face when interpreting science-related news. The development of appropriate
pedagogy will need to be based on evidence from practitioner studies that highlights
effective teaching practices.
   Finally, those involved primarily in science education will need to acknowledge
that the core elements of critical capability are interdisciplinary. This suggests the
need to explore opportunities for cross-curricular teaching and learning if the
potential benefits of using science journalism to promote science literacy are to
be fully achieved.

Science Clubs: An Underutilised Tool
for Promoting Science Communication
Activities in School

M. Shaheed Hartley




Abstract The introduction of science at schools is an important vehicle for science
communication. It is generally understood that the first experiences of science
influence scientific interest. Sustaining this interest through the education pipeline
from primary to secondary to post-school science careers is a challenge faced by the
education system of all countries. It is therefore imperative that suitable vehicles are
chosen to communicate science to learners at all levels to enable them to make
informed decisions about opportunities presented by science, technology, engineer-
ing and mathematics (STEM). This chapter in general reviewed selected reports on
the use of science clubs as a vehicle to promote science activities in various
contexts. It also in particular reported on the outcomes of the implementation of
science clubs in rural South African schools and highlighted the findings of the
experiences of schools participating in a project using science clubs to develop a
culture of science learning. The study revealed particular attributes identified by
teachers involved in the development of science clubs which added to their own
professional growth as science educators and science communicators. The chapter
also identifies some guidelines for teachers to follow in setting up science clubs
especially within developing countries.




Introduction

It is generally acknowledged that there is a need to improve the science and
technology skills and knowledge of learners in order for them to navigate their
way through the world they live in and to successfully access the opportunities

M.S. Hartley (*)
Science Learning Centre for Africa, Faculty of Education, University of the Western Cape,
Cape Town, South Africa
e-mail: shartley@uwc.ac.za, http://www.uwc.ac.za/Faculties/EDU/Pages/Faculty-Community-
Outreach.aspx

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,              21
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_2, © Springer Netherlands 2014
22                                                                        M.S. Hartley


available to them (Afterschool Alliance, 2011). It is also generally understood that
science and technology holds a myriad of employment and economic advancement
pathways to future generations. It is therefore important for learners to be provided
a solid foundation in science so that they can make informed decisions about their
future based on scientific understanding and awareness of the potential that science
holds in shaping that future. Hopefully this knowledge and awareness will encour-
age them to select science subjects and continue into science-based careers. As
technology in the modern world develops and advances, greater demand is created
for an appropriately skilled human resource base to best serve and interact with the
available science and technology as well as to improve and create innovations of
current and new technology.
    There have been reports of a general downward trend of learner participation in
science subjects, and a number of international reports have highlighted various
governments’ concern about the low uptake of science subjects (Pike & Dunne,
2011). In developing countries the challenge is even greater to ensure interest and
enthusiasm for the learning of science at school level, with the numbers of learners
taking up science and mathematics decreasing on an annual basis. International
tests like TIMMS and PISA continually point to a trend of poor achievement in
science and mathematics and highlight the challenges faced by developing coun-
tries. The PISA test (PISA in Focus, 2012) also identified successes of countries
utilising science-related extracurricular activities to communicate and improve
learners’ participation, achievement and enjoyment of science. It is therefore
imperative that the communication of science and all the potential it holds for
future generations be done in a manner that would draw young learners to science
and to sustain their interest and involvement in school science but also in activities
beyond the school curriculum. The challenge to science practitioners, researchers
and other stakeholders is to ensure that appropriate knowledge, skills and opportu-
nities are conveyed to learners in a meaningful way that would allow them to
comprehend, apply and interact within the science and technology field and to add
to the science research pipeline towards higher degrees.
    This chapter reports on science clubs as a vehicle for communicating science to
learners. It will review the application and the effects of science clubs in various
contexts and highlight the author’s experience in South Africa.



Selected Reports of School Science Clubs

The following reports were selected by the author to present the findings of the use
of school science clubs and to highlight similar and varying experiences in the
different contexts. The reports also identified the various attributes and skills
developed by learners as a result of their participation in science clubs.
   In a report by Bennett (1956), evidence was provided of the establishment of
school science clubs in a number (about 100) of British schools as early as the year
1949. These science clubs varied in size from 20 to 200 members, with an average
Science Clubs: An Underutilised Tool for Promoting Science Communication. . .       23


number of 60. The age of participants ranged between 14 and 18, and the majority
were structured in terms of elected committees and participation. Their activities
included visiting local museums, hospitals, industrial premises, field expeditions,
film shows and lectures by visiting speakers. A great number of these clubs joined
up to form societies and associations based on common interests pursued. The
report indicated the educational value added to school learners who changed from
delinquent behaviour before their membership of the science clubs and larger
networks to a mature outlook and responsibility after participation in the organised
activities. The report also identified the participation of learners in, for example,
activities of the Museum of Natural History as well as conferences held by the
different organisations of which membership was extended to school science clubs.
The report also highlighted the country’s rich heritage in science and attempts to
make learners identify with this culture of science learning. Finally the report
pointed out the formation of an organisation called Science Club, which provided
information and support to schools that wanted to form new science clubs. In
general, the report provided positive affective changes in learners towards science
and improved attitudes towards science subjects.
   In a recent study 52 years after the Bennett report, Mannion and Coldwell (2008)
investigated 250 schools in England that were provided with funding to set up and
run After School Science and Engineering Clubs (ASSECs) over 2 years. They used
multiple methods to collect their data, including pupil, staff and club leader
surveys; interviews; case studies; and survey of schools who decided against
running clubs. As with the Bennett (1956) report, their findings showed that the
majority of learners’ views about their involvement in their club became more
positive the longer they had been a member. Most learners thought they had
developed their understanding of what engineers and scientists do, although most
discussions with learners during case study visits revealed a number of misconcep-
tions. A number of learners thought the club had helped their understanding more of
science, design and technology than mathematics. The vast majority of club leaders
and staff saw improvements in practical skills, self-confidence and thinking skills of
learners as well as an improvement of attitudes to and understanding of science,
mathematics and engineering although there were varying degrees of improvement
in actual achievements in these subjects.
   In a single case study reported by a secondary school teacher in Portugal
(Viegas, 2004), she identified much like the reports of Bennett (1956) and Manion
and Coldwell (2008) that having a science club was an important vehicle for
developing scientific interest in schools. Her science club consisting of learners
aged around 14 years proved useful where scientific topics might not be of the
learners’ direct interest. She suggested starting a science club with the initial ideas
generated by learners and that the teacher’s involvement comes in the planning and
directing of the process. Her students indicated that it was important for them to be
in the club, especially because they better understood some science topics which
were taught in class. Similar findings were reported by Twillman (2006), who found
that the greater effect of learner participation in the science club she established at
her school came from a change in attitude. This improved attitude towards science
24                                                                         M.S. Hartley


was also reported by Moore-Hart, Liggit and Daisy (2004). Twillman (2006) found
that because most competitions require teamwork, club members’ communication
and leadership skills visibly improved. She also pointed out that missing school for
an event could assist improve time management skills. She identified challenges
similar to the South African context, namely, that learners experience social
difficulty with achieving and that science enthusiasm, effort and interest were
often frowned upon by their peers. The science club gave members the opportunity
and space to express curiosity, and members often gained a sense of belonging to a
community that they valued.
    A study by Feldman and Pirog (2011) reported on teachers’ and learners’
participation in authentic science research in out-of-school science clubs at ele-
mentary schools in the United States. Four to five teachers worked alongside
practising scientists as part of their research groups. Each teacher facilitated a
science club with 10–15 learners who, by extension, were members of the scien-
tists’ research groups. In their findings, they presented case studies of teachers who
were mentored by a research professor and how they in turn mentored the learners.
They found that in less than one academic year, the teachers were able to gain the
knowledge and skills to facilitate the children’s participation in authentic scientific
research. They also found that the learners gained methodological and intellectual
proficiency needed to contribute useful data and findings to the scientist’s research
programme. The results of this study are also supported by the findings of Hartley
(2010), who used a similar approach of training and mentoring teachers to cascade
their knowledge and skills to learners in science clubs at their schools. The
September issue of Afterschool Alliance (2011) summarised the evaluation reports
from afterschool STEM programmes across the United States and identified com-
mon trends and strengths that afterschool learning brought to STEM education.
Most of the programmes evaluated were specifically designed to provide services to
underrepresented populations in STEM fields and many also focusing on providing
girl learners with exposure to science and female role models. An analysis of the
evaluations yielded STEM-specific benefits that can be categorised under three
broad areas, namely, (1) improved attitudes towards STEM fields and careers,
(2) improved STEM knowledge and skills, and (3) a greater likelihood of gradua-
tion and pursuing a STEM career. These three areas were also highlighted by
Mkwandawire (2009) in his study on physical science activities and skills devel-
opment in the Namibian curriculum. This view was also supported by Shadreck and
Isaac (2012), who elicited learners’ views on teachers’ scientific knowledge in
Zimbabwe. They reported that learners expressed a need for science teachers who
made teaching science fun and interesting so that learners wanted to learn. Similar
trends were found by Hartley & Treagust (2006) with teachers and learners partic-
ipating in science clubs in South Africa. The following section highlights the
findings of an outreach project to establish science clubs at rural schools in
South Africa.
Science Clubs: An Underutilised Tool for Promoting Science Communication. . .     25


A South African Experience of Science Clubs

One of the national priorities in education in South Africa is the effective teaching
and learning of science and mathematics at schools. The achievement of learners in
examinations in these disciplines generally pointed to a systemic challenge that
required novel and innovative solutions. In research conducted by Hartley (2011), it
was found that developing the aptitude and interest of learners in science from an
early age and sustaining that interest throughout their school career increases their
achievement and performance in science. To this end, the Science Learning Centre
for Africa of the University of the Western Cape (UWC-SLCA) in 2005 started a
science development project which involved science clubs for rural schools and in
2009 received national recognition by winning the prestigious National Science and
Technology Forum award as Science Communicator for Public Awareness for its
contribution to science and technology education in this region. The project started
with a total of 40 schools – 20 primary and 20 secondary schools – and currently has
almost 100 science clubs in three provinces in South Africa.
   The project was divided in three phases. The first phase involved training of
science teachers, including specific content knowledge identified by various stake-
holders, including science teachers, practical and experimental skills, pedagogical
strategies and assessment. It was also during this initial phase that the notion of a
science club was introduced to teachers as a vehicle to communicate science and to
get learners hands-on with various science-related activities. The second phase
required the teacher to establish a science club at their schools. Teachers were
guided in this regard, including starting with a small group of learners that they
were comfortable with or one class or one grade level. An internal exhibition/
competition was held at each school for learners participating in the science clubs.
The third phase was a science competition between the various science clubs, and
learners or groups of learners that participated in the internal science competition
were selected to participate in this phase. Competitions were held for both primary
and secondary schools. The establishment of a series of science clubs at both
primary and secondary schools has had a good effect on improving the culture of
science learning amongst learners of participating schools as well as the successful
development of a culture of science teaching with science teachers. Teachers also
reported that discipline in their classes improved, and the popularity of the science
club increased when they did some of their science activities during school assem-
bly and gatherings (Hartley, 2012). The numbers of learners wanting to do science
increased, and improvements in the achievement of learners participating in the
science clubs varied between 5 and 20 %. The range of science activities also
expanded, and the level of the activities in the science competition improved
dramatically. Teachers readily shared their activities amongst each other but only
after the science competition as they challenged each other before the competition.
The overall outreach project also generated a number of smaller research projects
for postgraduate students.
26                                                                          M.S. Hartley


    As part of the research projects of postgraduate students, learners that partici-
pated in the science clubs and science competitions were tracked to follow their
progress from school to post-school study and careers. Current research data
highlighted one story in particular of a group of learners that participated in the
first interschool science competition. Nine learners in this group participated when
they won the high school section of the competition in 2005. In the year 2011, three
of the original nine learners were entrepreneurs owning their own businesses, four
learners were in their final year of engineering, one learner was studying medicine,
and one learner had just completed a science degree. Interviews with these students
illuminated an interesting impression of their participation in the science clubs and
how it influenced their chosen study fields and careers. These learners from humble
rural schools indicated that when they started working in science clubs, they started
to engage with more science literature and discovered the many opportunities that
studies in science held for them. They described their interaction through the
science clubs as “a whole new world opening up”. After winning the science club
competition, they assumed a sense of responsibility as other learners were looking
up to them. Over the years, many learners from the primary schools and secondary
schools have shown great promise and direction in science and engineering.
    The experiences of teachers that participated in the science club project from its
inception and teachers who joined at a later stage were also recorded. A healthy
relationship developed amongst teachers as they supported each other through
innovative and creative science club activities for their learners. A work ethic and
high standard of work developed in this community of practising science teachers,
and new teachers had to join the prevailing work ethic to be part of the group.
Research conducted by students highlighted a professional development journey of
a number of teachers, and the science clubs could be identified as the golden thread
in each one’s story. They identified their role in the science clubs as influencing
their approach, content knowledge, pedagogical style and practical and experimen-
tal skills in terms of science teaching and their professionalism in their subject area.
They also highlighted the important role that the science club activities play in
maintaining discipline in the science classroom. There was also an improvement in
the science achievement of learners that actively participated in the science club.
    In a group interview with science teachers from 40 schools who participated in
the science club project, teachers highlighted the following as important attributes
that were developed during their interactions with the project:
 1. Improved knowledge of science content
 2. Improved practical and experimental skills
 3. Improved pedagogical strategies
 4. Greater confidence in conducting practical work
 5. Better discipline in class
 6. Greater interaction with learners
 7. Development as a professional science educator
 8. Improved management of science classes
 9. Improved organisational skills
Science Clubs: An Underutilised Tool for Promoting Science Communication. . .                                27


 Understanding of contemporary issues in science
