Fig. 1 Graph of attributes versus value addition on a scale of 1–10

10. Development of leadership skills in science department
11. Improved communication with fellow science teachers
12. Improved communication with learners
13. Improved standing amongst science educators in region
14. Ability to relate science content to everyday events
15. Better understanding of contemporary issues in science
    In a pilot study of ten (10) schools to determine teachers’ perceptions of these
attributes, teachers were requested to rate the value addition of the attributes on a
scale ranging from 1 to 10, with 1 being the lowest value and 10 representing the
highest. The results of the pilot study are represented in Fig. 1.
    The data collected for ten schools in the pilot study represented in Fig. 1
indicated an improvement in attributes identified by science teachers. An exami-
nation of the teacher perceptions indicated that most of the attributes were scored
between 5 and 6, indicating that teachers viewed their involvement in the science
club activities to be above average. The scores for the attributes professional
development, confidence in doing practical work, pedagogical strategies, practical
and experimental skills and content knowledge ranged between 8 and 10 in some
schools, indicating greater value addition of these attributes to teachers in these
schools.



Recommendations for Science Club Development

In many schools in developing countries, teachers were required to expand learners’
involvement in science by participating in science weeks, science exhibitions,
science competitions, science excursions or science expos. The extent of their
involvement was either by direct participation in these activities or indirectly by
28                                                                         M.S. Hartley


visiting science centres or a mobile science vehicle visiting their school. The
teacher decided the type of science activity which was mostly determined by
budget allowances. Where schools had no budget for this activity, parents were
requested to contribute, or assistance was sought by the organisers of the activities.
The outcome of their involvement wavered between “learners stimulated to explore
more” and “learners considered it as a day away from school.” The degree to which
teachers inculcated what was experienced in their teaching and classroom activities
in many instances determined the extent of learners’ involvement in science. In
some instances, further support was provided to teachers by the programme orga-
nisers or even higher education institutions. This allowed a support base for
teachers who could then effectively organise and prepare a group of learners to
participate in the various science-related events. It was during these participation
opportunities that the notion of an organised science group or science club became
entrenched at schools. Learners used their participation and competition with
learners from science clubs of other schools as a measure of their own knowledge,
skills and understanding in science. This experience in many instances prepared
them for the following year’s event and the concomitant growth that accompanied
their involvement.
   Learners speak to other learners about their experiences, and soon the science
teacher becomes inundated with requests from many learners to participate in the
science club. It is advisable that teachers who are not confident with their own
scientific knowledge, abilities and skills to seek assistance from science event
organisers or more knowledgeable colleagues in order to instil confidence in
learners. It is also advisable to ensure that teachers understand and abide by safety
aspects for laboratories and experiments before demonstrating or allowing learners
to a hands-on environment. It is imperative to start the science club with elementary
activities and then to move on to more difficult tasks. When activities involve
equipment or substances that could prove hazardous, teachers must conduct these
activities themselves before demonstrating it to learners. There are a number of
science club activities that do not require expensive equipment and make use of
everyday products that are easily obtainable. The following are guidelines (Hartley,
2012) provided to teachers that wanted to start a science club at their schools:
 1. You must be prepared to put in extra time and effort. Commitment and belief in
    what you do is important. If you’re not sure or confident about projects,
    experiments, exhibits or science activities that learners could do in their science
    clubs, make contact with the event organisers, higher education institutions or
    experienced colleagues.
 2. Start with a small group of learners, one class and one grade. Only start with a
    group of learners that you are comfortable with and that you consider
    manageable.
 3. Work with the science club during break or after school. Make sure that you
    have a safe and secure environment/classroom where you can work. Avoid
    working with hazardous compounds in the initial stages of your science club.
    Safety of everyone concerned is paramount.
Science Clubs: An Underutilised Tool for Promoting Science Communication. . .    29


 4. Once you are confident with doing an experiment, show learners in the science
    club how to do it. Stress the fact that we learn more from failed experiments
    than successful ones.
 5. Use curriculum experiments and allow selected learners to practise the exper-
    iments after school, and then conduct/demonstrate it to other learners during
    the actual teaching period.
 6. Read up on small experiments/exhibits (there are a number of booklets avail-
    able on the Internet) that you could quickly demonstrate before starting a
    science lesson. This application has been successfully used by many of the
    participating science teachers as a way to have discipline in their classes. If
    learners are not quiet or paying attention, no new quick experiments will be
    demonstrated to start the next lesson. You will be amazed at how the learners
    respond.
 7. Allow your science club to demonstrate the experiments at school assembly.
    Again, this proved to be successful, and science teachers are inundated with
    requests from learners to be part of the science club. You can use different
    criteria to manage the growth of the science club, but remember safety cannot
    be compromised. Find out if there are any courses on laboratory safety that you
    could attend.
 8. Make contact with other science clubs and become part of the community of
    practice in establishing and expanding the notion that a healthy culture of
    science learning adds great value to learner performance.
 9. Become of part of science club competitions or exhibitions at a local level to
    allow your learners (and yourself) to gain confidence in what they do. You can
    move from there to national competitions.
10. Learners must demonstrate an understanding of the activities that they are
    involved in and must be able to explain what the science activity is all about.
    Communication of the science is important. Initially use a designated speaker
    (s) that would confidently do the necessary explanation. Encourage learners to
    read up more about the science activities that they are involved in. They can
    read up more in the library, Internet or various science promotion magazines
    and booklets. Make sure that you plan these activities with the necessary
    information about the content and safety aspects of the science activity.
   Funding is always mentioned as a barrier for the development of science clubs.
This need not be the case as many science activities require a creative approach and
an understanding that elementary products can be used as effectively as expensive
equipment. Indeed in one school, learners built their own model of a vehicle and
raced it against an expensive one bought in a shop. Their vehicle, put together from
cardboard, elastic, straws and a balloon that provided air pressure as power source,
provided greater speed and accuracy than the expensive vehicle. This type of
activity spurred on learners to create their own designs and models from elementary
products. Teachers were amazed of the extent of learning that took place as learners
tweaked and adjusted their creations to obtain maximum efficiency. This, in turn,
30                                                                                   M.S. Hartley


provided a learning opportunity for the science teacher to exploit and expose
learners to curriculum content in their own constructions and designs.



Conclusion and Recommendations

The reports from various countries and contexts identified the use of science clubs
as a positive addition to the learning of science. It was also considered as an
important vehicle through which science and science-related activities and careers
could be communicated. One of the threads that was clearly visible throughout the
various reports was the positive attitude towards science that learners develop.
There was a greater sense of understanding and appreciation for scientific endeav-
ours, including improvement in discipline. There were indications of limited
success in examination and test achievement in science and mathematics but, on
the whole, appear to be an upward curve. The South African experience with rural
schools was a useful revelation since it brought about a culture of science teaching
amongst teachers along with a pride for their subject. This situation allowed
teachers as professionals to form a community of practice and setting indicators
of good practice for others to follow. The development of science clubs brought
about a culture of science learning as more and more learners wanted to do science.
This served to put pressure on teachers to develop the science clubs even further.
The science club can be an important vehicle for teachers entering the profession as
it can establish them as respected educators amongst learners and their fellow
teachers. Science clubs can also be used as the medium through which the dissem-
ination of various science and science-related activities can be communicated to
learners, teachers and parents.
   From the above reports science clubs can indeed be recommended as an effec-
tive tool not only to promote science communication but also as a vehicle through
which the teaching and learning of science subjects can be improved to increase the
human resource pipeline of science students, researchers and careers.

Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging
in Science in Everyday Life: Ideas for Science
Educators

Tang Wee Teo and Kim Yong Lim




Abstract Scientific literacy may be developed by foregrounding the applications
of science in everyday life. In this chapter, we describe two sets of science activities
that draw upon ideas from everyday life to achieve two goals: (a) support students
in understanding science so that they may appreciate its national and global
significance and (b) to develop citizens who are scientifically literate. Drawing
upon our knowledge about science and former teaching experience, we designed
two extended sets of science activities that progressively build upon simple core
ideas to include more sophisticated ideas to address the above two goals. Through
this effort, we hope to offer some ideas to teachers and catalyze some change to the
way science is taught in schools.

An Excerpt from an Online Blog
                         An Excerpt From an Online Blog

     Steven: My wife gave me some red dragonfruit yesterday night. This morning I
     saw that my bowel movement was red. I was horrified and quickly flushed it
     down. The second time I went to the toilet to urinate, I saw that it was also red.
     Is there something wrong with me? I’m still young……



            “Dr Quack”: Red dragonfruit is genetically modified from the more common
            white dragonfruit by injecting chemicals that emit red colour to the naked eye.
            The red waste is a sign of kidney failure. You may want to see a doctor as soon as
            possible!


            Jane: I asked my mum and she said eat red dragonfruit is like dat one……dun
            panic.



           Peter: Drink more water to flush it out of your body.


                 “Dr Wise”: Not really…especially if it is organic-based.


              Steven: Thank you all for the suggestions. I’ll see a doctor
              immediately to do an endoscopy.


   The above excerpt was reconstructed from an online blog initiated by Steven,1
who had eaten red dragonfruit2 and became extremely worried when he saw red
bowel movements the next day. Similar topics had been initiated in various online
blogs and discussions were rich as different people conjectured about the possible
side effects of the red coloring and offered advice on remedies. “Dr Quack,” for
example, played on Steven’s fear to suggest that he should see a doctor as he could
be suffering from kidney failure as a result of eating red dragonfruit. He borrowed
the word “genetically modified”—a jargon popularly used but poorly understood—
to make his point more convincing. Jane’s proposal was based on hearsay from her
mother who represented a symbolic character of wisdom and experience, and
hence, what she said could be believable. Peter’s ideas were based on the assump-
tion that the red colorant was water soluble, and hence, it could be “flushed out” of
the body. “Dr Wise” contradicted Peter, again, throwing in another jargon


1
 Pseudonyms are used in this chapter.
2
 Red “dragonfruit” is a vernacular name of pitaya, a fruit of cactus species. This fruit is popularly
consumed as it is known to contain antioxidant properties.
Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging in Science in Everyday. . .         35


“organic” which the public often associate with being “healthy.” Eventually, Steven
decided that he would go for an endoscopy to scan his stomach, a decision which
deviated from all those suggested. If you were Steven, or had a similar experience
when you ate a red dragonfruit, whose suggestion would/did you take and why?
   Scientific literacy entails informed decision-making based upon knowledge and
understanding of science including its assumptions, uses, and limitations. As
science educators in teacher education and higher education, we believe that
scientific literacy can be developed from trying to find scientifically based expla-
nations of observed phenomena in our everyday life. This chapter is a conceptual
piece providing science educators with ideas on activities that relate to our every-
day life so that students may become empowered to employ accurate and relevant
knowledge of science to develop public understanding of science.



Scientific Literacy

Scientific literacy is a term which is increasingly mentioned in various national
science education documents and science education literature (see, e.g., Bybee,
1997, 2009; Laughksch, 2000). Organizations and government agencies around the
world have provided definitions of scientific literacy in various documents to
underscore the broader impact science education should have on its citizens.
According to the US National Science Education Standards, “Scientific literacy
is the knowledge and understanding of scientific concepts and processes required
for personal decision making, participation in civic and cultural affairs, and eco-
nomic productivity” (National Research Council, 1996, p. 22). Below is what a
scientifically literate person should have the capacity to do (National Research
Council, 1996, p. 22):
• Ask, find, or determine answers to questions derived from curiosity about
  everyday experiences.
• Describe, explain, and predict natural phenomena.
• Read with understanding articles about science in the popular press and engage
  in social conversation about the validity of the conclusions.
• Identify scientific issues underlying national and local decisions and express
  positions that are scientifically and technologically informed.
• Evaluate the quality of scientific information on the basis of its source and the
  methods used to generate it.
• Pose and evaluate arguments based on evidence and apply conclusions from
  such arguments appropriately.
   Two levels of understanding in scientific literacy are embedded in the above
statements—the development of knowledge about the norms of science practices
and key science constructs, and the knowledge that policy changes will inevitably
emerge, accompanying better knowledge about the impact of science and technol-
ogy (Miller, 1983).
36                                                             T.W. Teo and K.Y. Lim


   However, we question if students are able to develop scientific literacy when the
canons of (propositional) knowledge continue to take the primary role, and appli-
cations to real-life examples play second fiddle in a science curriculum. The
development of a scientific disciplined mind has traditionally and historically
focused on the “transfusion” of propositional knowledge representing canons of
scientific knowledge. This approach is contrary to teaching science: (a) by observ-
ing and finding an interest in understanding phenomena occurring in our everyday
lives, (b) drawing upon scientific knowledge to explain observations, and then
(c) make informed decisions as consumers of products and processes developed
from advancements in science and technology. Critics, however, may argue that
students need to first have a conceptual bank of knowledge before they can apply
concepts to explain the observed phenomena and engage in deep conversations.



Our Stance

Science activities interconnected to everyday life can actively engage students,
develop intellectual achievement, incite personal change, form new self-identities,
and provoke practical or social action (Aikenhead, 2007). The science activities
described in this chapter are designed in a way such that students engage in things
commonly found, used, and observed in our everyday life. Simple core ideas are
progressively expanded upon to include sophisticated ideas so as to make science
learning more palatable for students. In this way, we cater to the needs of diverse
learners of varying abilities so that they may be motivated and curious to
learn more.
   Through engaging with science in everyday life, we aim to achieve the two main
purposes of science education: First, to support students in understanding science
well enough to appreciate its national and global significance. We do this by turning
the traditional approach to science teaching on its head to suggest that science
should be learned from things used and observed in our everyday life; this serves as
the starting point to science teaching and learning.
   The second goal is to develop citizens who are literate enough to understand and
critique what is communicated by scientific experts so as to inform their decision-
making (Aikenhead, 2007). Cross and Price (1999) argued that the public should be
knowledgeable and critical enough to question, interrogate the “rhetoric of conclu-
sions” presented by “experts” and fed through the media, and make judgment on
controversial scientific issues. Else, the rise of expertocracy may threaten democ-
racy (Chomsky, 1992) when information fed by individuals with vested economic
and political interest distorts the public’s knowledge and ability to make wise
decisions. Students should be more cognizant and critical of what they observe,
read, and listen and apply relevant scientific knowledge to make sound personal
decisions. Returning to the opening blog, Steven believed that endoscopy results
would yield him some useful information, but he probably had little knowledge
about the purpose and limitations of endoscopy. This blog presents a good storyline
Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging in Science in Everyday. . .                           37


and case study to initiate inquiry about the chemical composition in red dragonfruit
and how our human body works so as to better understand why this fruit is healthy
to consume, what commercial properties the natural red colorants have, and the
functions of the human organs.
   In what follows, we describe two extended science activities that can be used to
develop students’ scientific literacy beginning from everyday life encounters. Each
activity begins with a case scenario to help students recall similar real-life encoun-
ters followed by suggested questions to invoke inquiry and extensions to the
activity.




Activity 1: Food Additives

The first activity is about the study of colorings, which are food additives. The food
we eat are mostly processed and colorings are added to it to enhance its appearance.
However, the type and quantity of colorings allowed in processed food are con-
trolled in many countries. In Singapore, the Food Testing Laboratory in the Health
Sciences Authority of Singapore, which is a government statutory board, verifies
some of the information about the food ingredients. Using a constructed story, we
want to invoke inquiry about food colorings, food coloring testing, and knowledge
about the control and testing of food in Singapore. Below is a vignette which can
be used to initiate a class discussion:
   “If only I had known.”
       Conrad Lee had recently ventured into an import and export business of local produce in
   Southeast Asia. As a wholesaler, he would travel around several places to search for goods
   produced from factories and export them to places that have a demand for the produce.
   Recently, he was sourcing for Belacan, a shrimp paste which is a common ingredient used
   in Southeast Asian and South Asian cuisine. It is made from fermented ground shrimp
   mixed with salt, sun-dried and cut into fist-sized rectangular blocks. Belacan is pungent and
   vary in their appearance from pale liquid sauces to solid brown-coloured blocks.
       Conrad met up with several suppliers and decided to purchase a bulk that looked
   brighter red in colour because it might look more attractive to consumers. To his surprise,
   he was informed that samples of the Belacan was sampled for food testing at the Singapore
   customs and later on, confiscated. He was surprised to learn that the Belacan he exported
   had contained artificial food colourings that were not approved and other approved
   colourings had exceeded the threshold amount. He lamented on his foolishness to be
   attracted to the colour of the sample and also wondered how the authorities had found
   out about the food colouring. He lamented, “If only I had known.”

   The above story can be used to excite students to conduct inquiry on the nature
(types and health effects) and regulations on food colorings in the local context and
other countries. Such knowledge will enhance their capacities as consumers to
make informed decisions when purchasing and consuming food products. In the
informal and formal science curriculum, students can craft their own questions or
use the ones below to conduct an inquiry about this topic and provide their synthesis
and critique on the information:
38                                                               T.W. Teo and K.Y. Lim


• Why are food colorings used?
• What are the different types of food colorings?
• Which government agency regulates the use of food colorings in your country?
  What are some laws governing the use of food colorings? What other food
  additives are being regulated?
• Which are the approved and unapproved food colorings? Why are certain food
  colorings approved or not approved?
• Can food additives cause hyperactivity in children?
• Why is there a limit on the amount of food colorings used in food?
• How are some food colorings tested in food testing laboratories?
• What are some possible food colorings used in Belacan? How can they be
  tested?
• Are there replacements for artificial food colorings?
    Based on the above questions, students will explore and discover that food
colorings are added to influence perceptions about the food (e.g., red is perceived
as spicy), replace color loss due to processing and storage to make it more
attractive, and make the food fun to consume (e.g., bright colors in sweets). Various
bodies in different countries are responsible for regulating food additives, and there
are different regulations on the approved food colorings and daily intake limits.
Currently, there is no common consensus on the type and daily intake limits for
each food coloring. In the United States, FD&C numbers are assigned to FDA3
(Food and Drug Administration)-approved synthetic food colorings, while in
Europe, “E” numbers are assigned to approved natural and synthetic food colorings.
In Singapore, the Agri-Food and Veterinary Authority of Singapore regulates food
colorings used as food additives. Other than the list of synthetic organic colorings
listed in the “Fifth Schedule,”4 no one is allowed to sell, import, advertise, deliver,
or consign any food that contains the food coloring in food intended for consump-
tion. When the goods are detained at the customs, they will be tested in the Food
Laboratory at the Health Sciences Authority.
    Sudan red is a common coloring added to food but it is banned in Singapore
because of its known carcinogenic effects. In the United States, FDA requires
tartrazine (FD&C Yellow No. 5) to be identified in the ingredient line as it is
known to cause hives in fewer than 1 out of 10,000 people.5 In the 1970s, there were
hypotheses about food additives causing hyperactivity in children. The Center for
Science in Public Interest, started in 1971, is an organized voice for the American
public on nutrition, food safety, health, and other issues. In a forum page on its
website, parents had expressed their views and hypothesis that food colorings have
on their children’s behaviors.6 On the same webpage, links were provided to

3
  www.fda.gov/Food/FoodIngredientsPackaging/ucm094211.htm#coloradd
4
  www.ava.gov.sg/NR/rdonlyres/0CA18578-7610-4917-BB67-C7DF4B96504B/23650/2web_SOF_
FoodRegulations_15Apr2011.pdf
5
  www.fda.gov/Food/FoodIngredientsPackaging/ucm094211.htm#qayellow5
6
  See http://www.cspinet.org/fooddyes/testimony.html
Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging in Science in Everyday. . .           39


documents such as “Food Dyes: A Rainbow of Risks” and “Petition to Ban Food
Dyes” to argue for artificial food colorings to be banned in food. However, based on
several reported studies, including one commissioned to the UK Food Standards
Agency to investigate whether certain food colors cause hyperactivity in children,
both FDA and the European Food Safety Authority reviewed the results and
concluded that no substantial correlations between the food color additives and
behavioral effects were found. These contradicting narratives present an interesting
platform for students to gather evidences, make and support their claims on the use
of food colorings as consumers, and have a public voice. At the same time, they will
learn about the limitations of science and instrumentation that can in turn limit
human ability to accurately determine and understand human behaviors and cog-
nition. This brings across the idea that scientific knowledge is not canons of
knowledge but socially constructed from arguments and consensus and substanti-
ated by limited evidences available (Kuhn, 1962).
    In food testing laboratories, the identity and quantity of food colorings can be
analyzed using gas chromatography-mass spectrometry to separate the contents.
However, without the presence of sophisticated instruments, qualitative analysis
can still be done to identify known colorants. Students may be challenged to suggest
methods for such an analysis that is efficient and cost saving. For example, they
may suggest using thin-layer chromatography to separate substances with different
polarities. They may visit a supermarket and select food substances for analysis
such as M&M, Skittles, and Belacan as they are coated with different colors. They
can select a reasonable quantity of the food substance that will yield them sufficient
colorant extract for analysis. They will have to do some literature search on the
types of food colorings used (e.g., soluble or insoluble in water, polar or nonpolar)
and decide on the solvent, procedures, apparatus, cost, and time needed for the
completion of the analysis. They can learn to write a laboratory report and do a
presentation to showcase their work and findings. Thereafter, they can critically
examine and compare how their analytical methods and results compare with the
ones done by professional testing laboratories and propose reasons why attaining
accurate findings are important. Based on this experience, students will learn the
importance of scientific accuracy in helping law makers pass fair legal judgments in
courts and to convince policy makers that certain laws and regulations need to be
passed and enforced to protect the interest of consumers. Students will learn that
scientific research is not only “high end” or theory producing but can be applied to
improve the quality of human life.
    With stronger consumer public knowledge and demands for healthy food, there
is a growing demand for natural food colorings. Returning to the opening excerpt
about red dragonfruits, students may be prompted to suggest using natural rather
than synthetic food colorings. For example, red beetroots are a source of natural
food coloring. As such, the red coloring in red dragonfruits may be extracted as a
natural food coloring as well. However, are the red colorings the same and what is
the advantage of using one over the other? The red pigment in red dragonfruit
(pitayas) contains betacyanins, which is a class of water-soluble pigment that can
pass out of the body easily. Currently, red beetroots are the main source of
40                                                                           T.W. Teo and K.Y. Lim


betacyanins but they contain geosmin and pyrazines that cause the unpleasant
peatiness of the crop. There are also high nitrate concentrations associated with
the formation of carcinogenic nitrosamines (Phebe, Chew, Suraini, Lai, and Janna,
2009). Red dragonfruit is an alternative as it does not give the negative sensorial
impact and can be harvested relatively fast. Already, the red coloring extracted
from red dragonfruit has been used to make jellies, cakes, and other food products.
Students may investigate ways to extract and concentrate the red pigment in red
dragonfruits and use it to make their own food products and write a position paper
arguing for the replacement of synthetic food dyes with natural ones. Such an
activity will make students more aware of the alternative sources of safer food and
additives that can be found and used in everyday life. The scientific knowledge they
apply to help themselves and others make informed decisions about the consumer
choices in their everyday life are a step toward developing greater scientific literacy
that benefit self and others.
   It is important to have educators realize that a single topic (in this case, even a
single substance) can lend itself across different disciplines and can be used to
engage students with varying interests and aptitudes. For example, elementary
school teachers may teach students to make observations of color changes using
the red dragonfruit extract but not teach them the concept of pH.7 High school
teachers may teach students the concept of pH and equilibrium constant which
shows the relative concentration of species responsible for the observed colors.
Higher-order and more sophisticated ideas about science may be introduced at
higher-grade levels and for higher ability students who may wish to do science
research. In this instance, red dragonfruit dye can be tested as a viable alternative
candidate to dyes used in dye-sensitized solar cells (DSSC). The DSSC is a
low-cost solar cell invented by Michael Gratzel in 1991. Interested students can
be encouraged to undertake a simple research project exploring the potential of this
dye in DSSC. Some overheads cost may be required in the form of purchasing
indium tin oxide (ITO)-coated glass, titanium dioxide (TiO2) powder, iodine
crystals, and potassium iodide in addition to some standard lab equipment. With
the exception of ITO glass (which can be bought from several vendors), the other
items required can be easily acquired. In the simplest setup, the anode is an ITO
glass coated with a layer of TiO2 paste (TiO2 powder mixed with detergent and
vinegar) and then immersed in the extracted dye, allowing TiO2 to absorb the dye.
Another piece of ITO glass can be used as the cathode with an iodine/triiodide
solution as the electrolyte. This setup can be connected to a voltmeter to measure
the voltage output of the DSSC under various light conditions. The research can be
extended to include various parameter searches on the concentration of the various
chemicals and reagents (given the same area and light source) to optimize the
voltage output of the DSSC. If resources permit, different light sources with
maximum intensities at different wavelengths can be employed for further


7
  Due to the intense color and high water solubility of the red dragonfruit extract, one dragonfruit is
sufficient for an activity involving a class of 30 students.
Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging in Science in Everyday. . .                             41


exploration. Red dragonfruit dye extract can also be tested against other fruit dye
extracts. If several groups are involved, this can even evolve into a long-term
project/competition.
   Through this project and its extensions, students can learn a myriad of topics
across the disciplines including light properties, circuit properties, semiconductor
properties, redox chemistry, electrochemistry, and fruit juice chemistry. In a report,
students can then explain how and why such a solar cell can work and how to make
it work best as well as the limitations of using dragonfruit dye and/or other fruit
dyes as dye alternatives in DSSCs. Students can also be asked to look at the
relevance and do simple calculations on the cost-effectiveness of the DSSC in
actual usage. A more complete picture of the importance of solar cells in today’s
energy-hungry geopolitical environment can also feature in a more holistic aca-
demic discussion. While the full implementation of the suggestions can be a senior
or high-ability student’s task, the project can foreseeably be broken down into small
parts to be dished out at different points of a student’s learning journey.



Activity 2: Materials in Our Everyday Life

Science subjects of today are broadly and traditionally classified into the study of
physics, chemistry, and biology with artificially defined knowledge domains. In the
real world, however, decisions are constantly made and knowledge synthesized
from a myriad of subject areas and content knowledge. In our efforts to cultivate
responsible scientific literacy, it is imperative that students inculcate skills and
attitudes that will help them connect information from various sources and knowl-
edge domains. Equally important, we can stress the importance of how some
common underlying principles in science lend themselves to become explanations
of various phenomena. One such underlying principle is the understanding of
substances as being made up of atoms and molecules. It is possible to identify
several topics in daily life which lend themselves to such an approach. One of them
is in the field of materials science.
    Here, we present a series of activities where students can learn about materials,
what they are, how and why they behave in certain manners, and ways to improve
on these materials. We can start off with excerpts from online articles on a related
topic. In what follows, we present two excerpts from online articles on related
topics.
   Article 1
       What’s So Special about iPhone 4’s Aluminosilicate Glass
       What’s in a piece of glass? A lot, apparently, if you’re talking about new mobile phones.
       What’s in a piece of glass? A lot, apparently. Recent devices have made a big deal out of
   something called Gorilla glass, with the Dell Streak most famously teasing its indestruc-
   tibility to great fanfare. Manufacturer Corning claims the glass can withstand nearly
   anything us puny humans can throw at it, and electronics makers are flocking to the screens
   in droves. . . . In other words, if you’re to drop a device manufactured in the last few years,
   its screen will probably be the first thing to break. You don’t need to travel much farther
42                                                                          T.W. Teo and K.Y. Lim

     than your local Apple Store to find a swath of people mourning the untimely death of their
     iPhones, marred by a four-foot drop onto cold, hard concrete. Because we’re unlikely to
     become less clumsy anytime soon, Corning has taken an alternate route and tried to
     improve the performance of the screens instead—and that’s where Gorilla glass comes
     in. . .
          Source: http://www.tested.com/news/news/429-whats-so-special-about-iphone-4s-alu
     minosilicate-glass/ (Retrieved on October 14, 2012)

     Article 2
         Glass Works: How Corning© Created the Ultrathin, Ultrastrong Material of the
     Future
         Don Stookey knew he had botched the experiment. One day in 1952, the Corning Glass
     Works chemist placed a sample of photosensitive glass inside a furnace and set the
     temperature to 600 degrees Celsius. At some point during the run, a faulty controller let
     the temperature climb to 900 degrees C. Expecting a melted blob of glass and a ruined
     furnace, Stookey opened the door to discover that, weirdly, his lithium silicate had
     transformed into a milky white plate. When he tried to remove it, the sample slipped from
     the tongs and crashed to the floor. Instead of shattering, it bounced.
         The future National Inventors Hall of Fame inductee didn’t know it, but he had just
     invented the first synthetic glass-ceramic, a material Corning would later dub Pyroceram.
     Lighter than aluminum, harder than high-carbon steel, and many times stronger than
     regular soda-lime glass, Pyroceram eventually found its way into everything from missile
     nose cones to chemistry labs. It could also be used in microwave ovens, and in 1959
     Pyroceram debuted as a line of space-age serving dishes: Corningware. Yet while there
     was plenty of initial interest, sales were slow. . .
         . . .After some market research, executives believed the company could even earn a little
     money off this specialty product. The project was codenamed Gorilla Glass. . .
         By the time the call from (Steve) Jobs came in February 2007, these initial forays hadn’t
     gotten very far. Apple was suddenly demanding massive amounts of a “1.3-mm, chemically
     strengthened glass.”
         Source:         http://www.wired.com/wiredscience/2012/09/ff-corning-gorilla-glass/all/
     (Retrieved on October 14, 2012)

   The above two news articles show how an iPhone’s glass screen, an ubiquitous
modernity, can have an interesting history and be used as an avenue to start a
meaningful conversation topic about materials science. There are several ways to
go about this. Students may of course be required to use the articles as a launch pad
for inquiry by checking the authenticity of online sources through cross-referencing
with other reliable sources in the form of books and journal articles. In addition,
they can be inducted into the subject through a string of questions:
1. Why do things, e.g., glass, break easily?
2. Do some things break easier when they are pressed or stretched slowly as
   compared to when they are knocked?
3. Why can some materials stretch? How are they stretched?
4. What are the factors that affect how a material behaves?
5. How do we get materials to become stronger? How can we change the elasticity
   of materials?
   However, for a beginning science student without experience in materials
testing, it may be a difficult task to appreciate the finer details of the subject
Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging in Science in Everyday. . .               43


and/or link up what can seem as disparate subdisciplines. Here, we string up a
coherent series of activities to guide students into what could otherwise be an
ambitious foray into the study of materials science.
   While directly experimenting with breaking and broken glass can be hazardous,
we can illustrate to students how and why a composite material may have more
desirable properties than its individual components using a demonstration that
employs an analog of the composite’s components. The demonstration entails
breaking ice with a hammer versus breaking an aggregate of ice and tissue paper
with a hammer. The latter is made by freezing tissue that is soaked in water. In this
simple exercise, it will become apparent that the tissue paper, or ice composite, is
much stronger than ice. Tissue paper is of course by itself soft. As cracks form and
spread easily in ice along defects that are inherent during freezing, ice is fragile
(though hard). The addition of tissue paper stops the cracks from spreading easily,
hence, strengthening the ice-tissue paper composite.8 At this juncture, the demon-
strator or teacher may wish to highlight the types of defects (interstitials and
vacancies, misalignment, etc.) that can exist in a crystal structure.
   Another common example of composite material is concrete, quite visibly the
building material of choice in our urban environment. It is used in almost all
modern buildings as a staple material. Concrete is actually a composite material
comprised of aggregate (sand and/or gravel), cement, and water. Within the mix-
ture, the hydration of water and cement, itself comprised of tricalcium silicate,
dicalcium silicate, tricalcium aluminate and tetraaluminoferrite, determines the
binding quality of the final paste. The resulting calcium silicate hydrate form
dense aggregations between crystalline phases/cement grains and helps to adhere
sand grains and the coarser aggregates.
   While the qualitative effects of having composites are clear in the ice-tissue
paper composite demonstration, it will be better that this be followed up by an
experiment that highlights the importance of a quantitative assessment of the
strength of the materials. Concrete can thus be used as a case study. Concrete can
be fabricated by mixing different ratios of sand, cement, and water, after which the
mixtures are allowed to set. The various pieces from the different ratios can then be
individually tested for strength using a clamp and force plate by measuring the
maximum force required to break them. From this exercise, we can get students to
discuss about the strength of materials relative to their compositions as well as
provide an extended discussion on strategies to strengthen materials such as intro-
ducing impurities or adjusting microstructures, the underpinning principle being
that of impeding crack propagation. Students can also do a literature search on how
concrete compositions can be adjusted for different portions of buildings (e.g.,
pillar vs. wall), how temperature can play a part in concrete formation, and so on.



8
  This demonstration was introduced by Assoc Prof Sow Chorng Haur, Department of Physics,
National University of Singapore and has been a constant feature in the NUS Science Demon-
stration Laboratory demonstration sessions ever since.
44                                                              T.W. Teo and K.Y. Lim


   Another experiment that students can perform is how the mixture compositions
can be adjusted to allow for the workability of the cement-aggregate mixture. The
physical properties such as density and strength of concrete are partly determined
by the proportions of cement, water, and aggregate (usually sand and gravel). To
determine which cement-water-aggregate ratio is optimal for workability, a “slump
test” is usually done in which the initial and final height of the concrete mix is
measured after it is packed into a plastic cone (can use a plastic cup), inverted, and
removed. In real construction sites, slump tests are done to compare with “stan-
dards” to ensure that the “right” concrete mixture is used for the building of
different structures such as walls, footings, beams, columns, pavements, and
slabs. Additionally, students can measure the change in the temperature of the
concrete mixture and learn that the changes observed are not physical (powder to
slurry) but chemical as the calcium silicates are hydrated to form calcium hydroxide
and hydrated calcium silicates, hence forming an exothermic reaction.
   Another follow-up question that can be answered through experimentation is
that of how materials stretch and break. In this experiment, students clamp a thin
copper wire to one end and stretch the wire by means of a spring scale at the other
end. Measurements of the tensile force applied versus the extension of the wire are
made, and a graph reflecting the stress and strain of the wire can be drawn. Students
can be asked to calculate the Young’s modulus for comparison against theoretical
values. Through this exercise, students are introduced to the basic terminology in
fracture mechanics and will see and learn to take measurements of the actual
physical phenomenon of fracture. Students can be led to identify the different
regions of the stress-strain curve and be asked to postulate what have happened in
terms of arrangement of atoms in each of these regions. A good theoretical guess
will entail having to match these mental models with experimental trends. While
such an exercise can be extended to other materials, we find that a thin copper wire
works best, considering the simplicity of the equipment used and because of the
parameters involved. Scientific traits of observation, measurement and analyses can
again also be cultivated through such an exercise. To cater to an elementary school
audience, the experiment can be modified into a qualitative demonstration where
the phenomenon of wire stretching is demonstrated. The point of necking can also
be visibly examined using a simple magnifying glass or microscope. This observa-
tion again lends itself to a discussion on how materials can undergo stretching and,
eventually, fracture.
   The above combination of activities can culminate in a consistent messaging of
how materials are formed and how they behave under pressure and tension in the
context of their chemical bonding and physical arrangement of atoms and mole-
cules. Students can be asked to compare and contrast how different materials break.
Specifically, brittle materials can break by cleaving and crack propagation while
malleable materials can deform and break if sufficient forces are applied. Again,
strategies as well as similarities and differences to strengthen different materials
can be discussed. To round up this series of discussions, students can be led back to
the case of the iPhone glass screens to understand what has been spectacular about
the advances made on having stronger but thinner glass and how this success has
Developing Scientific Literacy from Engaging in Science in Everyday. . .                        45


come about with a better and deeper grasp of the scientific terminology and
fundamental concepts. With sufficient interest and proper background, a review
paper from students in this direction should be a less arduous and, hopefully, a more
fruitful requirement and experience.



Concluding Remarks

In this book chapter, we advocate for science to be learned using ideas taken from
everyday life to develop scientific literacy. Using two everyday, commonplace
items (topics)—red dragonfruit (food colorants) and iPhone screen (materials)—
we show how a range of related activities, concepts, and ideas may be incorporated
into science teaching and learning. The students then construct an understanding
about science and develop scientific sense through exploring everyday phenomena.
Nonetheless, the suggested activities are only illustrative of the types of opportu-
nities that may be provided to students. The activities are not meant to be prescrip-
tive to teachers as we understand contextual factors such as costs and time may
limit the extent to which the activities, concepts, and ideas are fully developed and
discussed in class. In fact, some of the activities such as the pH indicator test using
red dragonfruit are relatively simple to prepare; the materials are relatively low cost
and readily available.
   Further, we acknowledge that engaging students with scientific ideas from
everyday life can be challenging for some teachers. According to Cajas (1999),
schoolteachers may not have the kind of “practical knowledge” needed to help
students with authentic tasks. By “practical knowledge,” we mean the knowledge to
troubleshoot and respond when an activity does not work as expected. Also, such
approaches may not rank highly in teachers’ priorities compared to, for example,
completing the syllabus on time or helping students to score well in exams. The
practical knowledge, often viewed as low status as compared to high status of
discipline knowledge, is seldom integrated into the formal curriculum (Goodson,
1994; Layton, 1973). While we acknowledge that these are real concerns and
factors that may limit the teaching of science relevant to everyday living, the
ideas in this chapter—which we have tried out in our own classrooms—can
hopefully offer some concrete ideas to interested science teachers.9




9
  As more information about the described activities is readily available in public sources such as
the Internet, we have avoided providing technical details. This would also encourage teachers to
read up and implement the activities in their classrooms.

The Nature and Role of Science Kits
in Affecting Change in Public Attitude
Toward Understanding of Science

Daniel L. Dickerson, Craig O. Stewart, Stephanie Hathcock,
and William McConnell




Abstract Science kits have tremendous potential as effective instructional tools
for changing the ways people think about science in both formal and informal
contexts. However, they can also be a colossal waste of resources if design,
implementation, and support are not appropriate. This chapter addresses science
kits by first describing what a science kit is. Both commercial and noncommercial
types of kits are discussed, including what they do and do not contain. Secondly, the
chapter addresses the rationales for the use of kits. These rationales are based in the
literature and focus on the efficacy of kits in the teaching and learning of science
among diverse populations. Next, we will discuss two example cases of kit imple-
mentation. For each example, we will describe the context in which the kit was used
and the total cost associated with implementation (both the cost of the kits and
support structures including professional development), as well as discuss how the
kit was incorporated into the current curriculum structure. Lastly, the chapter wraps
up by exploring trends in kit development.




Brossard and Lewenstein (2010) identify four general models of public understand-
ing of science: the Deficit Model, the Contextual Model, the Lay Expertise Model,
and the Public Engagement Model. They categorize the first two of these as being

D.L. Dickerson (*)

VA, USA

C.O. Stewart

e-mail: Craig.Stewart@memphis.edu
S. Hathcock • W. McConnell
Department of Teaching and Learning, Old Dominion University, Norfolk, VA, USA

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,             47
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_4, © Springer Netherlands 2014
48                                                                D.L. Dickerson et al.


focused on delivering information to a generally ill-informed public, highlighting
either general deficiencies in science knowledge (in the Deficit Model) or the
psychological and social contextual influences on the public’s (lack of) understand-
ing of science (in the Contextual Model). They categorize the latter two as focused
on engaging the public, highlighting the knowledge that various publics already
possess (in the Lay Expertise Model), or the active participation of citizens in
socioscientific decision-making (in the Public Engagement Model). While science
communication research often conceptualizes information delivery and public
engagement models as running on parallel tracks, Brossard and Lewenstein find
that, in practice, efforts to communicate science to the public will blend assump-
tions and goals from across these models.
   In this chapter, we describe science kits as a method of communicating science
to the public that illustrates the overlaps between these theoretical models, as
described by Brossard and Lewenstein (2010). That is, science kits offer, in both
formal and informal settings, a method for transmitting science concepts to lay
audiences, in ways that are sensitive to local contexts and individual differences,
while illustrating the interaction between expert and local knowledge and engaging
members of the public in socioscientific decision-making. While not all use of
science kits will draw on all of these models in the same way or in equal measure,
they demonstrate the problematic and artificial divisions between theoretical
models of the public understanding of science when applied to the practice of
science communication. We begin by describing what a science kit is. We then
address the rationales for the use of kits. Next we discuss two example cases of kit
implementation. We conclude by exploring trends in kit development.




What Is a Science Kit?

A science kit is a collection of materials and instructions packaged together and
used as an instructional tool to enhance science understanding. Science kits exist as
both commercial products and noncommercial instructional tools. Commercially
developed science kits can be purchased from a variety of vendors. For example, a
simple Google search using the phrase “science kits,” in quotations, produces
1,630,000 results (0.20 s, http://www.google.com/search?client¼safari&rls¼en&
q¼%22science+kits%22&ie¼UTF-8&oe¼UTF-8, February 1, 2012). A very small
sample of a variety of science kits is provided in Table 1. These kits can be used in
any context, but the market information is provided to describe where they are
usually found. All prices are approximate, subject to change, and listed in US
Dollars. As one can see, there is considerable range in cost of commercial kits,
from a few dollars to hundreds of dollars. For many science teachers in developing
countries, such costs likely put commercial kits out of reach. Later in this section,
we discuss the characteristics of noncommercial kits that can be created for
substantially less cost than many commercial kits. Later in the chapter, we elaborate

50                                                                 D.L. Dickerson et al.


on the construction and implementation of one such kit, the Great Build-A-Buoy
Challenge.
    Commercial kits can be purchased from school supply vendors as well as
common, large, chain retail stores. Often, however, there are some significant
differences in the types of kits offered by these different vendors. While all are
usually identified as being educational, most of the kits sold by school supply
vendors are geared more toward use with large groups in a structured environment.
This means that they often include materials for more than one person and instruc-
tions that generally meet the needs of someone working with a group of people
during a structured, educational experience. It is particularly important to note that
it also means that the consumable supplies included in the kit can usually be
replenished easily through the same vendor from which you purchased the kit.
Retail stores typically sell science kits designed for use by an individual, and
usually it is not nearly as easy to replenish consumables. The quality of instruction
in these types of kits varies as well. Most kits sold by school supply vendors have
had some degree of vetting by professional educators before the vendor agrees to
carry the product. This is not to say that there are no high-quality science kits
carried by retail stores, but the generalizations described exist based in part on the
various vendors’ primary target consumer and the desires of those consumers.
    Regardless of vendor, science kits address a wide range of science content within
every major science domain (i.e., life, earth, space, physical). Science kits carried
by school supply vendors often are aligned with national and/or local standards
documents and frameworks. This type of alignment makes it much easier for
teachers and those working within a formal education environment to select and
implement an educational activity that directly addresses a particular standard.
Some kits are even designed to address multiple science domains and include
lessons and materials for a specific grade level or age range.
    Costs vary as much as content focus, and the cost of consumable supplies after
the initial purchase of a kit can sometimes almost equal the cost of the original kit.
One method of holding down costs is to develop or assemble your own kit. This is
of particular importance for those whose budgets are already strained both in
developed and developing countries. Often these kits carry one of three character-
istics: (1) Kits are constructed of reused materials – example of reused materials
would be the Build-A-Buoy Kit described later in this chapter, which is often
constructed of scrap PVC pipe from area construction jobs. (Care must be taken
to insure that the PVC is safe for children and adults and that it was not previously
used in hazardous conditions.) (2) Kits contain few if any consumable materials –
the fewer consumables that are included in the kit, the lower the long-term cost of
the kit. (3) Kits have a practical purpose beyond their educational value, for
example, kits have been developed that serve a practical purpose in the home,
such as cooking (e.g., solar oven) or providing power (e.g., building a small
generator) while teaching about the science-related concepts. In this way, the cost
of the kit may help reduce other household costs in the long term.
    Noncommercial kits are ones that science educators develop or assemble from
materials they select in order to address the content they identify. Often these
The Nature and Role of Science Kits in Affecting Change in Public Attitude. . .   51


materials may be items that are being reused or donated from businesses, which can
decrease costs. They often do not carry as polished a look as a commercial product;
however, they do typically contain the same or similar elements (e.g., materials,
instructions, curriculum, standards alignment, etc.). There are some significant
advantages and disadvantages of noncommercial kits. Cost is certainly an advan-
tage as noncommercial kits are typically less expensive to obtain. Another advan-
tage is that they have the capacity to be place-based on a local scale and use local
contextual elements, including examples, dialects, and cultural traditions. One
disadvantage to noncommercial kits is that the quality is in large part dependent
on the capacity of the individual or group that is putting the kit together. For
example, if no one in the group understands curriculum development or standards
alignment, then the kit is likely going to be inferior to a commercially developed
kit. The companies that develop kits commercially often hire experienced science
educators to develop those elements for them.
    In general, whether kits are commercially or noncommercially produced, they
may provide an advantage over other forms of curriculum and instructional tools in
that they are a self-contained learning experience, complete with all the materials,
curriculum, and instructions necessary to support independent or guided learning
(when appropriately implemented). They are typically packaged to be highly
portable and when replenished can usually be used over and over again. There is
also tremendous variety and choice in terms of instructional content, targeted age
range, and price. There are of course disadvantages relative to other forms of
curriculum and instructional tools such as the need for space to store the kits
when they are not being used and cost of kits and consumables, and while they
may contain detailed, well-written instructions and curriculum, they are not
immune to user error. Science education researchers have studied the use of kits
in various contexts and provide the community with additional and related findings
regarding advantages and disadvantages to the use of kits in science teaching and
learning.




Rationales for the Use of Kits in Teaching, Learning,
and Communicating Science

This section of the chapter focuses on the historic rationales and current literature
regarding the efficacy of kits in the teaching and learning of science among diverse
populations. Both commercial and noncommercial science kits have been in use for
decades (Hone, 1970; Lopez & Schultz, 2001). The reasons provided for use of kits
by teachers and administrators are many and include the need to move toward a
more inquiry-based, hands-on learning environment and to compensate for lack of
teacher content knowledge (National Research Council, 2000; Perisi, 1975).
Research has demonstrated that science kits can be effective in enhancing student
achievement as measured by content-based, multiple-choice tests (Dickerson,
52                                                                  D.L. Dickerson et al.


Clark, Dawkins, & Horne, 2006). Studies also provide evidence that science kits
have positive impacts on both science teachers’ and students’ science knowledge
and attitudes (Houston, Fraser, & Ledbetter, 2008; Morrison & Estes, 2007).
Specifically, Jones, Robertson, Gardner, Dotger, and Blanchard (2011) found that
teachers reported increased inquiry-based instruction and reform-based assessment
practices. Additionally, they found that teachers who reported using kits most often
were significantly more likely to report that their students engaged in the design and
implementation of science investigations. Such findings occur in countries around
the globe (Kriek & Grayson, 2009). While many of these studies occur in the
context of formal education, similar findings exist when implemented by nonformal
educators and in informal contexts (McEntire & Rollins, 2007; Smith & Enfield,
2002). These informal, kit-based learning opportunities span the spectrum of STEM
content but are usually more product oriented. For example, remote control cars are
often offered in kit form, and while one will likely learn much from the process of
building the vehicle (e.g., concepts related to electricity), learning science content
is not the primary focus – producing a working vehicle is. This is an important
point, however, because the context of informal learning often means that the
learner has considerable motivation and interest in the topic, which increases the
likelihood of meaningful learning occurring.
    One of the biggest criticisms of kit-based science instruction is with implemen-
tation (Olguin, 1995; Saul & Reardon, 1996). However, many researchers consider
the benefits of kit-based instruction to be considerable despite the potential pitfalls
of inappropriate implementation. Some specific examples include empowering the
disenfranchised, enhancing teacher content knowledge and pedagogy, enhancing
student attitude toward learning science, and enhancing classroom environment
(Gennaro & Lawrenz, 1992; Houston et al. 2008; Monhardt, Spotted-Elk, Bigman,
Valentine, & Dee, 2002; NRC, 2000; Ward, 1993). Furthermore, some researchers
working with populations in developing countries have identified benefits that are
specific to those environments. For instance, Nganunu (1988) found that the use of
science kits tied to contexts that were culturally and geographically relevant leads
to more meaningful learning for students. Overall, while there are legitimate
concerns regarding the appropriate implementation of kits in both formal and
nonformal environments, a considerable amount of research documents the efficacy
of kit-based science instruction in a wide range of contexts. The use of kits appears
to have grown over the last 100 years as gauged by the increasing number of
commercial kits available and their presence in research studies within the science
education literature. Since the reasons cited for the use are not expected to diminish,
it is likely that science kits will continue to be a prominent tool in the teaching and
learning of science.
The Nature and Role of Science Kits in Affecting Change in Public Attitude. . .     53


Implementation of Science Kits

This section of the chapter focuses on cases in which science kits were used to teach
science content and impact attitudes about science for children and adults. Specific
examples are provided of kits and associated program structures that have led to
successful implementation. For each example, we describe the context in which the
kit was used and the total cost associated with implementation (both the cost of the
kits and support structures including professional development, etc.) and discuss
how the kit was incorporated into the current curriculum structure. The first
example involves the use of a high-cost commercial kit with preservice teachers.
The second example involves the use of a low-cost noncommercial kit with both
children and adults.




Preservice Elementary Science Teacher Education

One way science kits are used is to teach preservice teachers how to teach science.
The basic idea is that you model for university students how to provide effective
science instruction to children by using the same instructional tools and strategies
that best practice research suggests they should use with their students. The
expectation of course is that once these preservice teachers graduate and begin
their teaching careers, they will have access to similar or identical kits in their
schools. While access to resources varies dramatically across individual schools
and school systems in the United States, most schools have access to some form of
science kit (usually a commercial product).
   At Old Dominion University, we have purchased and use FOSS (Full Option
Science System) [http://fossweb.schoolspecialty.com/] kits with our preservice
elementary science methods students. There are a large number of different kits
that address different grade levels and science content (e.g., landforms, levers and
pulleys, solids and liquids, trees). Each has a different cost, is designed for a
different number of activities, and contains different materials. An individual kit
costs approximately US$1,000 and contains teacher instructions and support mate-
rials, notebooks for students (approximately 8/kit), short textbooks for students
directly related to the content (approximately 8/kit), and materials for approxi-
mately four different activities. Consumable costs range dramatically from approx-
imately US$10 to 100 or more. Typically, the kits are used with students working in
groups of two to four students due to the quantity of materials in the kits. Additional
materials can be purchased without buying another full kit if the instructor did not
want students working in groups.
   We have our university students complete an activity using the kits just as the
children would. After the students get a feel for what it is like to complete a
kit-based activity (which is a hands-on, materials-rich activity with associated
curriculum materials), we then have them use the instructor materials (both hard
54                                                                 D.L. Dickerson et al.


copy and web based that are included in the kit price) and develop a kit-based lesson
for their classroom peers. This is part of their instruction regarding lesson planning
and best practice strategies for teaching science. During practicum experiences and
student teaching, we also encourage our preservice teachers to use kits when
available to teach their students science. Due to the cost, many schools cannot
afford to purchase all the kits available. They may decide to only purchase kits
related to content with which test data show their students regularly have difficulty
year after year. Often these teachers will share these kits and so we also work with
the preservice teachers on co-planning with other teachers. In addition to commer-
cial kits (e.g., FOSS), we also have preservice teachers work with ones that faculty
developed as part of grant-funded projects or collaborations with government
agencies or nongovernmental organizations (NGOs). One such kit is called the
Great Build-A-Buoy Challenge, and it has been used with children and adults in
many different contexts.



Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM)
Education for Children and Adults

The Great Build-A-Buoy Challenge is an excellent example of how kits can be
highly portable and appropriate for diverse populations. This kit was developed
from an activity originated by Dr. Doug Levin, who worked for the National
Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA). Through grant funding from
NOAA (Project SEARCH: #NA09NMF4570008), Dickerson and colleagues mod-
ified the activity, added a supporting curriculum, and produced kits. These kits have
been used with adults in preservice and in-service elementary and secondary
teacher education programs and with parents of school-aged children during family
science nights at schools. They have also been used with adults who are nonformal
science educators during trainings at local, national, and international conferences.
The kits have also been used with children in kindergarten through high school (i.e.,
6 years old through 18 years old). The Great Build-A-Buoy Challenge is also a good
example of how, with appropriate curriculum integration, a kit can serve as a
foundational element in a truly integrated STEM-based lesson that is inquiry
based and helps to demonstrate elements of the nature of science and science
process skills. The basic idea is that the child or adult works to construct a
PVC-pipe buoy that will support as many golf balls as possible while floating
freely.



The Great Build-a-Buoy Challenge Kit

Dependent on the age of the user and the specific content targeted, there is a wide
range of science, technology, engineering, and mathematics concepts that can be
The Nature and Role of Science Kits in Affecting Change in Public Attitude. . .       55


addressed using the kit. Some of the science concepts typically targeted include
density, buoyancy, and center of gravity. Technology and engineering concepts can
include combining technologies and design principles. Mathematics concepts can
easily include measuring and ratio and proportion. In order for this kit to be used
widely, we had to identify and align the kit-based content with national standards
documents (see Table 2). These topics and standards are just a few of the many
possibilities that exist.


Implementing the Kit-Based Curriculum

The Great Build-A-Buoy Challenge Kit (and associated curriculum) is intended to
be led by an informed instructor that understands the STEM content and appropriate
inquiry-based pedagogy. An example of an inquiry-based STEM lesson plan using
the kit can be found in Science and Children published by the National Science
Teachers Association (NSTA) (Dickerson, Hathcock, Stonier, & Levin, 2012). It is
possible to use the kit as a stand-alone activity that is self-directed, but the outcomes
of such use are inconsistent in a fashion typical with other self-directed kits.
   We start by telling the participants that we have recently come across a little
known world record – the world record for the number of golf balls a small buoy
can hold – and that we intend to capture the record! The record number, we tell
them, is 21. (Of course there is no world record and we let them know at the end that
this was a story for their entertainment.) There are only two rules that must be
followed in building the buoy: (1) they can only use the materials provided in the
kits, and (2) the buoy must support the golf balls such that no part of the buoy or the
golf balls touch the sides or bottom of the test tank.
   Before students get their materials we address safety issues. The materials in the
kits stay fairly consistent across age ranges, although some substitutes are made for
very small children for safety reasons. For example, usually real tools are included
that can pinch fingers and cable ties (zip ties) that if placed on students’ bodies
could be pulled tight and cut off circulation. When working with populations that
may not be able to safely use these materials, we simply remove the tools and use
yarn or rope instead of cable ties.
   Each group or individual is provided with a kit complete with the materials they
will need to build their buoy. A laminated visual materials list is included. The list
can be found at [http://www.odu.edu/~ddickers/grant_noaa_search.htm]. The total
cost of each kit, minus the tools or test tank, is approximately US$15 and most of
that cost is the container. When we begin, we usually place a partially constructed
“decoy” buoy where everyone can see it. The decoy, however, is of very poor
design (see Fig. 1). Most people initially try to copy the design and their buoys are
not able to hold very many golf balls. As students begin building, sometimes they
will ask questions about whether their design will work. To make the lesson inquiry
based, it is important that the facilitator say something like, “That’s interesting, why
don’t you test that idea out?” People usually apply a trial-and-error approach to
increasing their buoy’s payload capacity. In the kits we also place a toy with a very
