                among technologies and the connections between technology and other fields of
                study
             (A) The study of technology uses many of the same ideas and skills as other subjects
             (B) Technologies are often combined
             (C) Various relationships exist between technology and other fields of study (ITEA,
                2007)
Engineering ITEA Standard 8: students will develop an understanding of the attributes of design
             (A) Everyone can develop solutions to a problem
             (B) Design is a creative process
             (C) The design process is a purposeful method of planning practical solutions to
                problems
             (D) Requirements for a design include such factors as the desired elements and
                features of a product or system or the limits that are placed on the design (ITEA,
                2007)
Mathematics NCTM Measurement Standard: understand measurable attributes of objects and the
                units, systems, and processes of measurement
             (A) Understand such attributes as length, area, weight, volume, and size of angle
                and select the appropriate type of unit for measuring each attribute
             (B) Understand the need for measuring with standard units and become familiar
                with standard units in the customary and metric systems
             (C) Carry out simple unit conversions, such as from centimeters to meters, within a
                system of measurement
             (D) Understand that measurements are approximations and how differences in units
                affect precision
             (E) Explore what happens to measurements of a two-dimensional shape such as its
                perimeter and area when the shape is changed in some way (NCTM, 2000)


low center of gravity called a Weeble (Fig. 2). We have them compare the behavior
of their buoy to the behavior of the Weeble. After making and recording their
observations on behavior, they hold the Weeble and describe it. This usually results
in people making the connection between stability and location of mass. At this
point, people usually move the Frisbee lower in their buoy design if they are using it
to hold golf balls. Sometimes people even abandon the Frisbee altogether and use
cable ties to make nets to hold golf balls below the water line. An example of a
stable buoy is provided in Fig. 3.
The Nature and Role of Science Kits in Affecting Change in Public Attitude. . .        57


    When working with students, we use the kits to get them excited about buoys.
From there we show them how to create observation buoys by placing a thermom-
eter on it and recording temperatures. Eventually, we have students build larger
buoys that hold high-end instruments capable of collecting tier-one data. These
instruments are not educational quality but instead are the same quality as the ones
government agencies use. We currently have multiple buoys deployed in the
Chesapeake Bay measuring various water quality parameters (e.g., temperature,
conductivity, dissolved oxygen, concentrations of environmental toxicants; see
Figs. 4 and 5). These data, with proper quality assurance/quality control (QAQC)
protocols, can be used by government agencies in policy-making decisions. Data
collected in accordance with QAQC protocols accepted by the Virginia Department
of Environment and Natural Resources (VA DENR) can be included in state reports
sent to Congress and would include the students’ names as the collectors of the data.
    So while the kit itself is a critically important element, it is less potent when used
in isolation. By using the kit in combination with other instructional strategies and
tools, we were able to capitalize on the educational momentum generated by the kit
resulting in the creation and application of authentic inquiry tools. Without the kits,
however, students would not have had the foundation to extend this learning
experience to the point where they could conduct authentic science and communi-
cate their findings to others.



Conclusion: Trends in the Development of Science Kits

The conclusion of this chapter focuses on future directions of kit development and
use. While a number of trends exist, we will focus on two of the most prominent: the
importance of STEM Education and socioscientific issues.



STEM Education

The integrated nature of science, technology, engineering, and mathematics is
addressed in a wide range of ways around the globe. Those ways are rooted in
culture and tradition, political policy, and educational norms. In the United States,
there has been a growing emphasis on increased integration among the four
disciplines, particularly in formal education contexts. One example of this can be
seen in A Framework for K-12 Science Education recently published by the
National Research Council (2011). This is the document that many look to as an
updated version of the National Science Education Standards (NRC, 1996) – the
document on which most states base their own standards. While the NSES and other
standards documents and frameworks have long included technology, design (engi-
neering), and mathematics, recent efforts provide greater attention to the integration
of these other disciplines. As STEM content becomes a larger part of formal
58                                                                  D.L. Dickerson et al.


Fig. 1 Buoy decoy




Fig. 2 Weeble toy




education across the United States, teachers and administrators will look for
instructional tools and curricula that can meet their needs. Since the vast majority
of practicing teachers in public school environments have not had training regard-
ing STEM instruction, particularly with regard to the technology and engineering
components, it is likely they will look for instructional tools that contain a high
degree of content support. Therefore, they will likely consider implementing
science kits. While research calls for professional development support with the
use of kits (Dickerson et al., 2006), in practice, that may not happen for a variety of
reasons, including little or no funding to support professional development, com-
peting professional development needs, or the belief by administrators that the
teachers do not need professional development to use the kits effectively. As such
the kits themselves, because of their comprehensive nature, may be used as a
solution to the problem of lack of teacher knowledge regarding STEM content
The Nature and Role of Science Kits in Affecting Change in Public Attitude. . .   59




Fig. 3 Stable buoy design




Fig. 4 Large buoy with tier-one instrumentation and telemetry
60                                                                  D.L. Dickerson et al.


Fig. 5 Environmental
toxicology buoy




and STEM pedagogy. Kit designers need to be aware of this possibility and take
steps to ensure, or at least increase the likelihood, that the users of their products
will get the professional development support they need to effectively implement
kit-based instruction. While on-site professional development may be cost-
prohibitive for many, the use of web-based content may provide a potential solution
for some. Interactive webinars, along with static web-based content (FAQ pages,
STEM content explanations, videos of teachers effectively using the kits, etc.), may
be able to offer sufficient support for teachers. Additionally, video-based pedagog-
ical instruction could be included in the kits on DVDs if Internet access is an issue.



Socioscientific Issues

Socioscientific issues are another major trend of which kit developers and users
should be aware. Socioscientific issues are those that deal with the intersection of
science and society. Examples include topics such as stem cell research, global
climate change, relationships between science and religious faith, etc. These issues
typically have no clear solutions and require people to consider scientific informa-
tion, cultural values, societal norms, political climate, and any other influences that
may impact both personal and communal decisions. Government agencies, NGOs,
and a wide range of nonformal educators often deal with socioscientific issues on a
regular basis when communicating with the public. For example, park rangers may
need to teach visitors about logging issues related to a national park. The visitors are
typically representative of the general public at large and will often hold views
toward logging that vary from one extreme to the other and just about everything in
between. Providing instruction regarding the science behind logging relative to
habitat destruction and creation, the economic benefits and liabilities, current
applicable laws, environmental justice issues, etc., can be very challenging partic-
ularly if the intent is for the instructor to stay neutral on the topic. While it is
arguable whether one should remain neutral, there are numerous situations in which
The Nature and Role of Science Kits in Affecting Change in Public Attitude. . .                61


the park rangers are required to remain neutral in their responses to visitor questions
regarding politically sensitive issues related to the park. Additionally, there may be
other situations in which the park rangers are worried about getting into arguments
or long debates with visitors about an issue. In such situations, these nonformal
educators may rely on instructional tools that deemphasize the role of the park
ranger in the instructional activity and create a more shared learning experience
among the visitors, while the park ranger serves as more of a facilitator. Kits have
been used for this purpose and further allow visitors to interact with exhibits and
resources, kit materials, and other visitors. Due to the contextual nature of the
content, many such kits are noncommercial and are created at the informal educa-
tion institution or by the governing agency.
   When developing or reviewing kits to be used to teach the public about
socioscientific issues, it is important to remember to review the most current
research regarding the teaching and learning of the topic to inform your selection.
For example, when teaching about climate change or other environmental chal-
lenges, the research suggests staying away from instructional materials that use
fear, tragedy, or doomsday-type messages, particularly when working with children
(ICLEI, 2011). Instead it is suggested that it is more effective when materials focus
on personal behaviors and actions and offer messages of realistic hope (Kelsey &
Armstrong, 2012). Since socioscientific issues can emerge quickly and the related
scientific and public perspectives remain dynamic, information about effective
instruction regarding the particular topic should be sought and revisited regularly.
What we know about effective instruction regarding climate change has changed
considerably since the concept first entered the public domain. That knowledge will
continue to change. Staying informed about effective pedagogy regarding
socioscientific issues will help insure that the kits used will be consistent with
best practices in science education and will effectively provide current and mean-
ingful information to the public.

Field Trips to Industrial Establishments:
Infinite Opportunities for Popularising
Science

Irene Tan and Charles Chew




Abstract This chapter focuses on the edutainment value of field trips to industrial
establishments as a means of popularising science to students. It traces the impor-
tance of field trips as a teaching pedagogy for active learning to bridge the gap
between science lessons in the classroom and science in the real world. By means of
an exemplar of a successful field trip to Jurong Island (Singapore’s chemical hub),
the usefulness of field trips to industrial establishments in the fostering of scientific
literacy for twenty-first century’s teaching and learning is highlighted.

Keywords Field trips • Edutainment • Industrial establishments • Scientific
literacy


Introduction

In the learning of science, it is always valuable to start teaching and learning by
engaging students’ prior knowledge and daily experiences that are situated in the
real world. As the students progress to higher levels, the scientific concepts are not
as evident in the daily life. For example, while a student can recall personal
experiences for separation techniques such as filtration and sublimation, it is
often challenging to expect student to have knowledge of oil refineries, as these
are not commonly seen. In order to make science and technology relevant and
contextualised for the students, it is worthwhile for teachers to plan field trips to
industrial establishments which are accessible to schools.
   Industry focuses on the production of goods and services within an economy and
can be classified in two ways – by sectors and by products. In terms of sectors, there
are three types, namely, agriculture, manufacturing and services. When classified in


I. Tan (*) • C. Chew
Academy of Singapore Teachers, Ministry of Education, Singapore, Singapore
e-mail: Irene_TAN@moe.gov.sg; Charles_CHEW@moe.gov.sg

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,        63
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_5, © Springer Netherlands 2014
64                                                                    I. Tan and C. Chew


terms of products, there are many categories, such as chemical, petroleum, auto-
motive, electronic, hospitality, food, software, paper and semiconductor.
    By visiting industrial establishments, students can gain first-hand knowledge and
understand the applications of science and technology in the various industrial pro-
cesses. These visits will help them contextualise the content taught in the science
curriculum and realise the importance and relevance of science in their daily life. At a
macro level, students will be able to better understand how science impacts a country’s
economy as well as the quality of life and well-being of the people.
    In the late twentieth century and beginning of twenty-first century, Singapore
has witnessed unprecedented rapid economic growth due to advances in technology
and globalisation. This has led to rapid changes in the way businesses are
conducted. Some changes include outsourcing and offshoring. In a “flattened”
world (Friedman, 2006), never before has a nation’s economic development
become so dependent on advances in science and technology, such as ours. This
has created a demand for a workforce that is technically and scientifically literate
(Liu, 2009). It is against this backdrop that 1997 can be viewed as a watershed year
in the history of the education system in Singapore when the Ministry of Education
launched the framework of “Thinking Schools Learning Nation” as well as the
Master Plan for Information and Communication Technology (ICT).
    To prepare the young for the twenty-first century with its knowledge-based
economy driven by the twin forces of globalisation and the breakneck speed of
advancements in ICT, three major educational initiatives, namely, information
technology, national education and thinking skills were launched. Field trips by
Singapore schools to places of interests and national institutions (which include
industrial establishments) in the form of “learning journeys” were situated in the
wider context of the national education initiative as well as non-formal science
education.
    In Singapore, “learning journeys” are field trips organised by schools to foster
experiential learning outside the classroom as an integral part of the educational
process. Learning journeys may include public industrial establishments such as
Jurong Town Corporation, Maritime Port Authority of Singapore, Singapore
Power, as well as private industries such as GlaxoSmithKline, Everbloom Biotech
and Hewlett Packard. Besides enhancing the students’ quality of learning, these
journeys help to enlighten students on the role and importance of these industries in
Singapore’s development, instil a sense of pride in the country and be confident of
Singapore’s continued prosperity to grow and sustain her economy.
    For the purpose of this chapter, we will explore a few questions: In what ways do
field trips to these establishments in the form of learning journeys help to enhance
the students’ quality of learning science? What are some major considerations that
teachers need to be cognizant of in planning successful field trips? While these
questions are valid, two more important and fundamental questions are: What does
research say about field trips? Is there any research study on the use of field trip as
a teaching pedagogy for active learning?
Field Trips to Industrial Establishments: Infinite Opportunities for. . .                        65


What Does Research Say About Field Trips?

In general, there is a dearth of research on the efficacy of using field trips to
industrial establishments as a teaching pedagogy for active learning. A literature
scan has not located any research studies done in the area of field trips to industrial
establishments within the Singapore context. Our research references are drawn
from a collection of field trips to museums and science centres abroad.
   Research has surfaced the edutainment value of field trips. In terms of educa-
tional value, researchers who examined the elements of effective science field trips
found that students tended to remember trips in which they had high mental
engagement and actual physical engagement with exhibits and objects. (Wolins,
Jensen, & Ulzheimer, 1992). Besides the rich educational value, field trips engage
and even entertain students, helping to make educational experience more relevant,
memorable and meaningful (Scarce, 1997).
   Non-formal science field trips enable students to bridge the gap between science
lessons in the classroom and science in the real world (Bartley, Mayhew &
Finkelstein, 2009). The researchers also found that non-formal science activities,
which include field trips, also have “positive effect on science content knowledge
and attitudes by serving as an intermediary space between formal science educa-
tion” (Bartley et al., 2009).
   As a teaching pedagogy, field trips are lived learning in which students learn
actively through hands-on experiences. The interaction between the experts and
students, amongst the students as well as between teachers and students, brings into
focus the application of science in the real world, making the curriculum alive
(Coughlin, 2010).
   Field trips to industrial establishments with clear goals and careful planning can
bring about powerful learning experiences where students construct new knowl-
edge out of the experiences they encounter. The fostering of students’ understand-
ing and appreciation of science and technology in society through these learning
experiences at industrial establishments are essential aims of science education,
which are supported by PISA’s definition of competencies in scientific literacy for
learners.
   PISA defined competencies for scientific literacy for all children including but
not limited to (a) students’ ability to identify scientific issues, (b) explain phenom-
ena scientifically and (c) use scientific evidence. PISA argued that students should
be competent to acquire scientific knowledge and use that
   . . .knowledge to identify questions, to acquire new knowledge, to explain scientific phe-
   nomenon, to draw evidence-based conclusions about science-related issues. Understanding
   of the characteristic features of science as a form of human knowledge and. . . how science
   and technology shape our material, intellectual, and cultural environments. (OECD, 2009)

   A well thought-out field trip would help intrigue the students and bring relevance
to concepts they learn in the classroom. This helps them make use of the scientific
knowledge learnt to the identified areas of scientific interest. During the field trip,
students seek answers through iterations of inquiry that allow them to act as
66                                                                  I. Tan and C. Chew


“scientists”, such as Charles Darwin who contributed to science through the long
and rigorous field trips to gather scientific evidences and data. The use of inquiry
methods by scientists such as Charles Darwin in knowledge construction from field
trips are certainly in line with the nature of science as a hybrid arena which blends
aspects of various social studies of science (including the history, sociology and
philosophy of science) and research from the cognitive sciences (such as psychol-
ogy) into a rich description of what science is, how it works, how scientists operate
as a social group and how society itself both directs and reacts to scientific
endeavours (McComas, Clough, & Almazoa, 1998, p. 4).
    There are five arguments on the importance of nature of science (NOS) as an
educational goal, and these are provided by Driver, Leach, Millar and Scott (1996).
Their arguments are as follows:
• Utilitarian: it is necessary to make sense of science and manage the technolog-
  ical objects and processes in everyday life
• Democratic: it is necessary for informed decision-making on socioscientific
  issues
• Cultural: it is necessary to appreciate the value of science as part of contempo-
  rary culture
• Moral: it helps develop an understanding of the norms of the scientific commu-
  nity that embody moral commitments that are of general value to society
• Science learning: it facilitates the learning of science subject matter
   Having established the value of field trips from research findings, how does one
go about planning successful field trips with a strong theory-practice nexus?




Planning Successful Field Trips

To conduct successful field trips that are relevant and exciting for the students,
teachers need to build links to the curriculum to factor in the affective, behavioural
and cognitive domains. Researchers concluded that the strength of the field trip
experience was clearly impacted by whether or not the teacher was able to create a
context for the trip. It is important to note that activities by themselves do not
constitute meaningful learning. For students to truly benefit from the learning
experience, they must be able to make a clear connection between background
knowledge and the present activity. Under such conditions, “doing becomes a
trying; an experiment with the world to find out what it is like; the undergoing
becomes instruction – discovery of the connection of things” (Dewey, 1934).
   Melber (2008) opined that planning for a successful field trip should include the
following:
Field Trips to Industrial Establishments: Infinite Opportunities for. . .            67


•   Location selection
•   Curricular connection
•   Transport arrangement
•   Developmental Appropriateness
    The selection of ideal location for field trips is of utmost importance as teachers
need to consider the connection between the location and curriculum with respect to
schemes of work. Besides logistical considerations, a successful field trip includes a
previsit briefing and post-visit activities that make specific links to the experiences
of the field trip. In addition, it is important for teachers to mitigate potential risks
which may arise from such trips as no natural environment or journey is ever
completely secure. Safety is of paramount importance and teachers have to ensure
safe practice procedures with due consideration for well-qualified, experienced
specialists and first aid or emergency procedure in place.
    It is essential for teachers to consider the overall learning goals, safety param-
eters and resources available when planning for field trips to make learning
meaningful for students. The teacher needs to consider the relevance in terms of
content, the skills which the students will learn in the field trips and the attitudes
that the teacher hopes that the students will acquire. Some possible questions to
frame the planning are listed in Fig. 1 (Scrlbnor-MacLean & Kennedy, 2007).
    There are evidences to support that post field trip reflection as part of reflective
learning is crucial to extend the benefits of such outings. One possible strategy is to
use Bloom’s Taxonomy to guide and frame post field trip discussions with students
(Table 1).
    Field trips can be used together with research-based pedagogies to enhance
student learning. For instance, a field trip can be part of an inquiry process of the
BSCS 5E Instructional Model (Table 2). Teachers can use field trip as part of the
Engage phase to enable increased level of interest amongst the students or Explore
phase such that the students can be given the same experience visiting the same site,
observe, collect information and record in a meaningful manner. These information
can then become part of their discussions in the follow-up lessons to aid under-
standing of science concepts.
    Likewise, there is potential for field trips to be included as part of a problem-
based learning (PBL) cycle since teachers can innovatively weave in the selected
sites as part of the problem scenario for students to investigate (Table 3). Alterna-
tively, the selected sites can be part of the Discovery phase in PBL where students
can visit the sites as part of learning to find out about how to solve the problem
described in the scenario.
68                                                                       I. Tan and C. Chew




Fig. 1 Questions guiding the planning of field trips (Scrlbnor-MacLean & Kennedy, 2007)
Field Trips to Industrial Establishments: Infinite Opportunities for. . .                        69

Table 1 Use of Bloom’s Taxonomy to foster reflective thinking
 Bloom’s Taxonomy         Questions for Reflection

 Creating                 What should I do next? What is my plan? How can I adapt this

                          content or skill to make a difference?

 Evaluating               What can I do to improve on this? Is what we learn important and

                          why?

 Analysing                Was there a pattern or relationship? What were the main ideas?

 Applying                 Where can I apply this? Do I see connections of what I learn in this

                          field trip to what I learn in class? Where could I use this my life?

 Understanding            Why was it important? What is the impact?

 Remembering              Where did I go? What did I do? What did I observe?




Table 2 BSCS 5E instructional model

              Phase          Purpose

              Engage         Understanding learners, excite, generate interests,

              Explore        Unfold, finding out more, doing things, touching

              Explain        Describe, telling in their own words

              Elaborate      Extend, applying knowledge

              Evaluate       Assess, applying knowledge




Some Suitable Industrial Establishments in Singapore
for Field Trips

Some suitable industrial establishments in Singapore for field trips include Jurong
Island (petrochemical industry), GlaxoSmithKline (Pte) Ltd. (pharmaceutical
industry), Firmenich (Asia) Ltd. (chemical industry), Singapore Flyer (tourism
70                                                                   I. Tan and C. Chew

Table 3 Problem-based learning

 Stages of PBL                              Type of Instruction

 Meeting the problem                        Teacher-directed Learning

 Problem analysis and learning issues       Self-directed Learning

 Discovery and reporting                    Self-directed Learning

 Solution presentation and reflection       Self-directed Learning

 Overview, integration and evaluation       Teacher-directed Learning




industry), supermarkets (retail and food industries), Singapore Changi Airport
(transportation industry) and Bosch Singapore (Pte) Ltd. (automotive, industrial,
consumer goods and building technologies). This chapter will describe in detail one
industrial establishment for teachers to plan for a highly educational field trip: the
Jurong Island.



Field Trip to Jurong Island

Target audience: secondary/junior college students
Type of industry: petrochemical industry
Objective(s):
     (a) To foster scientific literacy through experiential learning
     (b) To appreciate the economic importance of Jurong Island’s petrochemical
         industry
Key Learning Points
1. Jurong Island’s Past and Present
   Jurong Island is Singapore’s chemical hub. This man-made island is situated
offshore on the southwest of the main island of Singapore. The island is made up of
seven smaller islands amalgamated by land reclamation. The seven are Pulau
Seraya, Pulau Merbau, Pulau Sakra, Pulau Pesek, Pulau Merlimau and Pulau
Ayer Chawan. The massive reclamation started in the early 1990s and completed
in 2009. With a total area of approximately 32 sq. km, this makes Jurong island the
largest outlying island of Singapore.
   Started as an oil refinery in the 1970s on Pulau Ayer Chawan, Jurong Island, it is
now home to more than 100 leading petroleum and petrochemical-related
Field Trips to Industrial Establishments: Infinite Opportunities for. . .           71


companies from all over the world. Jurong Island is one of the most innovative and
strategic projects of our island nation. It represents the nation’s vision and will to
succeed – transforming small islands and coral reefs into the world’s third largest
petrochemical facility.
2. Preparations Before the Field Trip
   The island is only accessible via a link bridge from Jurong Port Road and a prior
booking has to be carried out by teachers. Teachers also have to furnish the names
and identity card numbers of all visitors, including the bus driver, 2 weeks before
the slated visit. One important point to note is that there are to be no additions or
replacements after the original list of names have been submitted. As Jurong Island
is a gazetted protected place, no recording equipment such as cameras, tape
recorders, etc. are to be brought in. The security clearance to Jurong Island is
very tight due to the sensitive nature of the industry.
3. Highlights of Jurong Island
   Upon arrival, the view is rather breathtaking as the area near Visitors’ Centre is
quite scenic. A bus will bring the students and teachers around the island while an
experienced guide on board explains the history and composition of the island.
Here, the students observe first-hand how an oil refinery looks like instead of staring
at a picture from their textbooks or from websites. The industrial structure is rather
spectacular – beyond buildings are rows of cylindrical oil tanks for liquid petroleum
products and spherical containers for gaseous products, along with countless
brightly coloured pipes that are labelled with the names of the raw materials.
   Students who visit the island can observe the well-established infrastructure on
the island and how different companies are integrated and interlinked by the
common pipeline service based on the products the company specialises
in. Going through the island, the students will understand why companies prefer
to set up their operation here as the integration makes the manufacturing process
seamless, with one company producing the raw feed for the others-all with the
convenience of pipelines that run hundreds of kilometres across the island.
   The guide also explains how chemical feedstock and products are supplied and
dispatched from one plant to the next via the network of pipelines, as the output of
one plant is the input of another as a result of the cluster manufacturing arrange-
ment. All these minimise the requirement for storage facilities or long-haul trans-
port and distribution.
   The trip ends with a visit to the Chem Gallery. Stuffed with exhibits and
interactive displays about crude oil and how it is separated and leads to the products
we see and use every day, the Gallery allows students to learn how a country like
Singapore, which has no natural deposits of oil, has managed to develop the
petrochemical industry into one of the key pillars of its economy. It also boasts a
giant periodic table and organic chemistry charts from where they can learn the
chemistry behind the processes that are happening on the island. Part of the Gallery
also houses exhibits for students to learn about the origins of oil and gas, their
transformation into valuable petrochemical and specialty chemical products and
72                                                                   I. Tan and C. Chew


their extensive applications in everyday life. The products from the island include
the following:
•    Adhesives and sealants
•    Agricultural chemicals
•    Construction chemicals
•    Cosmetics raw materials
•    Food additives
•    Cleaning and maintenance chemicals
•    Plastics, polyethylene, packaging materials, bottles, containers, etc.
•    Specialty chemicals related to flavours and fragrances
   To facilitate students’ meaning making of their rich experiences, a customised
Chem Gallery worksheet (Annex A) is provided to engage students in their cogni-
tion during the exploration and as a post-trip assessment for learning tool to
consolidate learning. As observed in the Annex, the suitable topics for chemistry
(SEAB, 2013) are:
• Fuels and crude oil
     – Name natural gas, mainly methane, and petroleum as sources of energy.
     – Describe petroleum as a mixture of hydrocarbons and its separation into
       useful fractions by fractional distillation.
     – State that the naphtha fraction from crude oil is the main source of hydrocar-
       bons used as the feedstock for the production of a wide range of organic
       compounds.
     – Describe the issues relating to the competing uses of oil as an energy source
       and as a chemical feedstock.
• Alkenes
     – Describe the manufacture of alkenes and hydrogen by cracking hydrocarbons
       and recognise that cracking is essential to match the demand for fractions
       containing smaller molecules from the refinery process.
• Alcohols and carboxylic acids
     – Describe the reaction of a carboxylic acid with an alcohol to form an ester,
       e.g. ethyl ethanoate.
     – State some commercial uses of esters, e.g. perfumes, flavourings and solvents
• Macromolecules
     – State some uses of poly(ethene) as a typical plastic, e.g. plastic bags and
       cling film.
     – State some typical uses of man-made fibres such as nylon and Terylene,
       e.g. clothing, curtain materials, fishing line, parachutes and sleeping bags.
   A field trip to Jurong Island exemplifies the spirit of the chemistry syllabus for
Singapore (SEAB, 2013) which aims to draw students’ attention to:
Field Trips to Industrial Establishments: Infinite Opportunities for. . .            73


• The finite life of the world’s resources and hence the need for recycling and
  conservation
• Economic considerations in the chemical industry, such as the availability and
  cost of raw materials and energy
• The social, environmental, health and safety issues relating to the chemical
  industry
• The importance of chemicals in industry and in everyday life




Conclusion

This chapter has presented the benefits of field trips to industrial establishments and
how they offer numerous opportunities for popularising and learning science. Field
trips that are meaningfully fun and engage the cognition can certainly go a long way
in inspiring learners to enjoy learning science and appreciate its relevance to their
daily lives. Twenty-first-century students are immersed in an environment that is
rich in media and technology, and field trips can help to make them see the impact
of science on their daily life. The widespread use of products of science and
technology for greater comfort and convenience means students are in constant
contact with applications of science and technology. In order to make science more
relevant to students in schools, the use of context-based science has assumed a
greater importance for effective teaching and meaningful learning. This approach
of learning science is supported by a growing literature on the significance of
students learning science beyond the boundary of the school (Falk, 2001). Besides
igniting a passion and interest in science, situating the learning of science in a real-
world context can inspire them in their educational pursuit as well as making
science as a choice career. This is especially important as at a national level,
Singapore’s knowledge-based economy is highly dependent on science and tech-
nology. This dependence necessitates having a large workforce that is highly
literate in science and technology. In addition, the knowledge of science is also
vital for the public to make responsible decisions to preserve the natural environ-
ment and understands the impact of their decisions and actions.
    It is also pertinent to note that Singapore being small in size has its advantages
for field trips as many of these industrial establishments are easily accessible by
simple modes of transport such as public/chartered bus and Mass Rapid Transit
railway system. In addition, most industrial establishments are within one hour
travel time. As such, the illustrations and descriptions in this chapter serve as points
of reference and broad guiding principles for consideration in planning such trips.
For any location beyond 20–25 km from the school locale, it is advisable to take
into consideration other key factors beyond transport and safety. These will include
overnight accommodation, adult chaperones to participants’ ratio and others
described in the section on planning successful field trips.
