         Part II
Science Centers
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating
Science: A Personal View

Graham Durant




Abstract This chapter outlines how science centres are useful, relevant and effec-
tive places for communicating science to a broad range of audiences.
   Many countries, cities and regions that do not yet have science centres are
considering setting one up. This chapter outlines a range of science centre models
that could be adopted by different countries seeking to establish science centres,
taking into account different economic and social imperatives. It offers a brief
history of the evolution of science centres and considers potential future directions
so that science centres around the globe can continue to offer lifelong learning
opportunities outside of the classroom.
   Multiple science communication means are detailed including hands-on inter-
active exhibits, travelling portable exhibits, science shows, large format film pro-
ductions and theatre spaces. The way in which these science communication styles
are utilised within science centres, as part of outdoor exhibit spaces, in outreach
activities, and via digital communication methods is investigated.
   The chapter also offers expertise, guidance and direction in establishing and
sustainably operating a science centre. The establishment of the Glasgow Science
Centre and Questacon, Australia’s National Science and Technology Centre, are
offered as case studies.




G. Durant (*)
Questacon: Australia’s National Science and Technology Centre, PO Box 5322, Kingston,
ACT 2604, Australia
e-mail: graham.durant@questacon.edu.au

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,          81
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_6, © Springer Netherlands 2014
82                                                                            G. Durant


What Are Science Centres?

Science centres are wonderful places for communicating science. Well-run science
centres are lively, experience-rich places full of excited children and families
enjoying a good mixture of hands-on exhibits and science demonstrations. Typi-
cally they occupy their own buildings and operate as stand-alone visitor attractions.
Science centres are places that people want to go for a mix of education and
entertainment and they go in large numbers.
   There are many different types of science centres and many different science
centre programmes. Some are small and others are very big. Some science centres
occupy large purpose-built buildings designed by famous architects. Others occupy
older buildings that have found a new lease of life. Some science centres are sited in
shopping malls and others alongside academic institutions. Some science centres
have no permanent home but travel around setting up temporary facilities in
community buildings or even tents.
   In short, there is a science centre model that could fit any country, city and town.
Just as places have museums, art galleries, swimming pools and libraries, access to
science centres or science centre activities should be part of the local cultural mix.
   There are now hundreds of hands-on science centres around the world, and they
exist in countries irrespective of economy, geography, race, religion and politics.
Many countries, cities or regions that do not yet have science centres are consi-
dering setting one up. There are an estimated 2,400 science centres or related
organisations worldwide attracting some 300 million visitors each year. There are
many different types of science discovery centres, so a precise determination is
problematical.
   Science centre visitors are mainly children and families, teachers and school
groups, young adults, parents and grandparents. As such, science centres are
forward-looking places with a future focus. Most science centres started locally
serving their town or city. Some branched out with various types of outreach
programmes to take on regional responsibilities and to become national facilities.
Now science centres work together cooperatively around the world connecting
people across all sorts of boundaries, be they geographical, political or language
barriers.
   The different types of science centres share a common hands-on philosophy
offering active engagement with exhibits rather than passive observation, and
learning by doing using hands and brains together. Science centres typically offer
access to bright young science communicators who can facilitate learning and
enhance the experience for visitors. Such science communicators can help explain
exhibits, present demonstrations of scientific phenomena or deliver science shows
in theatre settings.
   Science centres are not science museums. Science museums typically have a
historical focus and collect and use the artefacts of science and technology. Science
centres have a focus on phenomena, experiences and stories. However, there are
many institutions that combine a science museum with a science centre approach.
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View               83


Children’s discovery museums share much in common with science centres, but
these tend to have a much broader multidisciplinary focus.
    Science centres operate in many business sectors. They operate as education,
tourism, retail and economic development businesses. Some science centres have
commercial operations building exhibits, running consultancies or film-making.
Most science centres operate in the service sector delivering different types of
educational experience on a fee-for-service basis. Some science centres run schools
and deliver curriculum support activities for the region, and a few even offer
teacher training as an alternative route into the teaching profession. Primarily,
science centres are in the motivation business, but they strongly support education
and informal learning.
    Through the excitement and engagement of the various experiences on offer,
science centres can inspire and motivate individuals and groups. This inspiration
and motivation can lead to changing attitudes or behaviours. It can lead to extra
interest and extra effort in the classroom. It is through the encouragement of such
extra interest and effort that there is a gain in productivity for individuals and for
society. Science centres excite children, but importantly, science centres can
influence their parents and teachers, those who will have a longer-lasting impact
on children’s interests and attitudes. Science centres can, and do, change lives,
helping to set people on a path through life that may lead to them becoming
professional scientists, engineers or innovators. Science centre experiences can
help people better understand issues of science.



A Brief History of Science Centres

Hands-on science centres were established in North America, with the Pacific
Science Center in Seattle, the Exploratorium in San Francisco and the Ontario
Science Centre in Toronto being the forerunners. A complete history will identify
pioneering work with galleries in some science museums and science educational
establishments, but most people will credit the developments in North America as
the foundation for the expansion of science centres around the world.
   The Pacific Science Center (1962) and the Exploratorium (1969) occupied
vacant expo buildings, while the Ontario Science Centre building was specifically
designed as a science centre (1969). The Ontario Science Centre was the first of
many purpose-built leading architect-designed science centre buildings. The
Science Centre Singapore (1977); Science North in Sudbury, Canada (1984);
Questacon in Canberra, Australia (1988); and Heureka in Vantaa, Finland (1989)
were some of the early purpose-built buildings. More recently, Nemo in Amster-
dam, the Netherlands (originally New Metropolis, opened 1997); the Glasgow
Science Centre in Scotland (2001); the City of Arts and Sciences in Valencia,
Spain (2000); and Phaeno in Wolfsburg, Germany (2005) provide some examples
of architect-designed science centres in Europe. In Asia, the National Science
Museum in Bangkok, Thailand (2000); Miraikan in Tokyo, Japan (2001); the
84                                                                            G. Durant


Guangdong Science Centre in Guangzhou, China (2008); and the Macao Science
Centre, Macao, China (2009) all occupy buildings of architectural significance.
   There are many more in other parts of the world. There are many fine purpose-
built science museums that also deliver hands-on science centre exhibits and
activities. The Hong Kong Science Museum (1991); the National Science and
Technology Museum in Kaohsiung, Chinese Taipei (1997); and the China Science
and Technology Museum in Beijing (originally opened in 1988 and in a new facility
from 2010) provide some examples. There are many more.
   Not all science centres are housed in such new buildings. There are excellent
science centres occupying old railway stations (the Science Museum of Virginia,
Richmond, USA), old industrial buildings (Experimentarium, Copenhagen, Den-
mark) and even a former slaughterhouse (La Cite des Sciences, Paris, France, now
Universcience). Some science centres occupy buildings in shopping malls (Scitech,
Perth, Australia, and Petrosains, Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia).
   For towns and cities that do not have science centres, travelling science centres
can offer valuable hands-on experiences in tents, trucks and village halls. The
Discovery Dome operated by Science Projects in the UK was a forerunner for
such mobile science centres, touring from 1986 with some 50 portable hands-on
exhibits and a science show stage in linked tents.
   Some science centres such as the Clore Garden of Science in Rehovot, Israel,
exist outdoors using a garden setting alongside a university to offer visitors a
chance to explore purpose-built exhibits that exploit the natural phenomena of
sunlight, wind and water. It is not unusual to find university professors helping to
explain the exhibits to visiting school groups.
   The association of a science centre with a university can be very positive, and
some science centres have been set up by universities or by university academics.
Universum in Bremen, Germany, and Universum in Mexico City are examples of
science centres built on university campuses. The Bristol Exploratory, Bristol,
England (1980), and Techniquest in Cardiff, Wales (1990), are examples of science
centres set up through the personal passion and drive of university academics,
Professor Richard Gregory and Professor John Beetlestone respectively.
   Science centres are established for reasons other than for a passion in communi-
cating science. Although science communication is at the core of what is on offer,
the driving force behind the establishment of different science centres has been for
educational support, as a catalyst for urban regeneration, as a tourist attraction or as
a statement of national or civic pride.




Communicating Science in Science Centres

Science centres exist to communicate science. It is not the buildings that make a
science centre-it is the content of those buildings, the activities and the enthusiastic
people who operate and deliver them that make the science center.
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View                 85


   There are many different visitors to science centres, and the exhibits and
activities have to cater for a wide range of interests and abilities. The best exhibits
and demonstrations offer interest and meaning for young and old alike.
   Science centres communicate science using a range of different media and
techniques.



Hands-on Exhibits

Exhibits and exhibitions typically provide the core experience for visitors to science
centres. Numbers of exhibits vary with the size of the science centre but can range
between 50 and 500 exhibits per institution, the latter making it virtually impossible
to experience every exhibit on a single visit. Larger venues will have multiple
galleries that can be themed or which can house topical exhibitions. Most science
centres will have some significant ‘hero’ exhibits that are a ‘must-see’ part of the
experience of visiting that centre. The fire tornado in Science Centre Singapore, the
‘Asimo’ robot in Miraikan and the ‘Gravitram’ at Questacon are examples of such
exhibits.
   Good hands-on exhibits are attractive and intuitive. Good hands-on exhibits
need little or no labelling. Ideally, exhibits should have only minimal labelling as
most visitors will not read long labels. A typical label hierarchy will have an exhibit
name, a brief instruction, some reinforcement of the observation or experience and
a link to the phenomenon in a real-world application. Additional content or
explanation can be delivered through gallery explainers, through mobile devices
or via a website.
   The exhibits need to be well designed and well engineered to withstand or
endure the enthusiastic attention of hundreds of visitors each day. A good main-
tenance regime needs to be in place to change exhibit components that wear out
through repeated usage and to undertake minor repairs of exhibits in the gallery.
Visitors tend to notice exhibits that are not working since other visitors are not using
them and they are consequently free to use. It is for this reason that well-run science
centres remove exhibits from the floor for mending. Although staff may notice that
a particular exhibit is not in the gallery, visitors rarely do.
   Each exhibit needs to work as a stand-alone experience (a conceptual island)
although groups of exhibits can be clustered to develop a theme or topic
(a conceptual archipelago). A themed cluster of exhibits can be tied together with
graphics to create a richer experience and to convey more contextual knowledge.
   It is instructive to observe visitor behaviour in science centres. After the initial
arrival and orientation, younger visitors in particular will often move quickly in an
excited manner between exhibits, soaking up experiences on a superficial level.
After a while, a pattern of calmer behaviour tends to emerge, and visitors will spend
a bit longer with some of the exhibits, getting a deeper experience and extracting
some deeper meaning from the exhibits. Repeat visitors will spend longer with
exhibits having had the initial superficial experience on a previous visit.
86                                                                            G. Durant


   Visitors tend to move randomly between exhibits since many are already
occupied by other visitors and are bypassed. Although the structure of the science
centre building and arrangement of galleries may impose some order on a visit, it is
generally not possible to have strong linear content. The museum or art gallery
shuffle where visitors move in a line from exhibit to exhibit is not often observed in
science centres. There is generally no beginning or end to a science centre exhibit
experience. They are designed for repeat visits rather than one-off experiences.
In order to attract repeat visits, science centres have to continually renew their
exhibitions and programmes. They typically have to balance the need to retain the
‘old favourite’ exhibits that repeat visitors seek out and the creation of new exhibits
and experiences.
   Certain types of exhibit encourage group activity, and these can be very enjoy-
able for participants and watchers alike. Mechanical/physical exhibits generally
seem to be more popular than computer screen-based exhibits, although there are
some excellent large-scale exhibits that successfully exploit computers to create
enjoyable interactive experiences. Kinetic sculptures and art-science exhibits can
introduce elements of wonderment into science centres. Different science centres
will have their own unique offerings although visitors to many different centres will
observe a broadly similar collection of exhibits. However, it is only the well-
travelled science centre professionals who tend to notice this.
   In designing galleries for science centres, it is perhaps helpful to consider
exhibits as loose furniture in a room. The advantage of this is that the exhibits
can be moved around to refresh a gallery. Broken or tired exhibits can easily be
removed for maintenance, and new exhibits can be easily inserted. Galleries
designed more cohesively can be likened to a fitted kitchen. Such galleries typically
have a strong design and visual appearance, but it is harder to make changes, to
remove or replace exhibits or associated graphics.



Exhibitions

Exhibitions tend to focus on a particular topic, such as climate change or robotics.
They will typically be a mix of graphics, set work and exhibits, sometimes with real
objects. They will be tied together with strong design elements. Many such exhi-
bitions will be designed to travel to different venues, while others will be designed
for a single venue.
   Commercially produced large-scale blockbuster exhibits are frequently intro-
duced into the larger centres to drive visitation and revenue. Dinosaurs, plastinated
human bodies and the machines of Leonardo da Vinci are examples of exhibitions
that prove to be popular with visitors, drawing large crowds. An exhibition such as
that of plastinated human bodies needs to be handled very sensitively but, if done
so, can provide a meaningful educational experience, particularly for an older
audience demographic. Photographic exhibitions, particularly those featuring
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View                87


wildlife or microscopic images, are usually popular with visitors. As more such
images appear on the Web, it is possible that their novelty is becoming less.



Travelling Exhibitions

Science centres often rely on the use of temporary exhibitions to attract repeat
visits, and there is an active world market for such travelling exhibitions. Small-
scale, ultraportable exhibits play an important role as outreach components,
allowing hands-on phenomena to be exhibited and enjoyed in community and
school venues. These are particularly useful for smaller towns and even villages
where there are no suitable venues for larger touring exhibitions.



Outdoor Exhibits

Many science centres use the area outside the building for a range of outdoor
exhibits. These are typically more robust having to withstand the elements as
well as the attention of sometimes overenthusiastic visitors. There are many
advantages to having exhibits outdoors since natural phenomena such as wind
and sunlight can be used. Water exhibits are better outside as are large-scale
geological exhibits. Plants can be used outside to enhance the visitor experience.
It is essential that outdoor exhibits be safely designed for unsupervised use even if
within a closed off area. Legislation governing the safe operation of playgrounds
becomes relevant and must be adhered to outside a building.



Science Shows

Science shows can be spectacular if done well. The essence of an excellent science
show is some good science demonstrations, a lively presenter and a good audience
rapport.
   Science demonstrations and shows have to appeal to a range of audiences with a
range of ability and attention span. Good science communicators can adjust their
level of delivery to suit the nature of the audience. A show for a weekend family
audience will typically be delivered in a different way to that for a school group in
which there is a broadly similar grouping of ability.
   Science shows need good content with real demonstrations of an appropriate
scale to be visible to the entire audience. Many science centres employ science
communicators who can successfully use theatrical techniques to increase the level
of engagement. Dressing up as a character, the use of humour, storytelling, singing,
puppetry and juggling are all skills that have been used to good effect in the delivery
88                                                                             G. Durant


of science shows. Some science centres employ actors to deliver shows, but it is
important that such actors understand the scientific concepts that they are trying to
communicate to avoid introducing misconceptions.
    Science shows are often delivered in specially designed theatres that allow good
visibility of demonstrations, good audience interaction and ease of set-up and
delivery. Controls for lighting and multimedia must be well situated to allow for
ease of operations in what is typically a one-person show. An adjacent preparation
area and storage for demonstration apparatus greatly enhances the ease of delivery
for presenters.
    The safety of staff and visitors is of paramount importance, and only well-trained
science communicators should be allowed to use hazardous materials or apparatus.
Exploding hydrogen balloons, freezing items in liquid nitrogen, electricity and laser
demonstrations can all be memorable experiences but ones that require great care
since they carry risks for staff and visitors alike. Safe storage and handling of
chemicals and gases is essential.
    A good science show will have a number of authentic demonstrations or
experiences linked together with a general narrative. Each demonstration should
be clear and work every time although some demonstrations may be a bit temper-
amental and require more practice.
    A good demonstration should be something that is exciting, curious, puzzling or
delightful. Surprise is a technique used successfully by good presenters. Science
centres should seek to demonstrate things that cannot be experienced in everyday
life as well as familiar things put into a different context. Encouraging visitors to try
something at home is a potentially good outcome for certain things.
    Science centres encourage audience interaction, and most will draw volunteers
from the audience to help with demonstrations. The sea of hands when a presenter
asks for a volunteer from the audience is a typical reaction seen in science centres
worldwide.
    Technological developments allow more integration of multimedia into shows
although its use should be balanced. Leisure-time visitors are unlikely to come in
droves to power-point presentations no matter how engaging the presenter may
be. However, sensible and clever use of images, film clips and close-up cameras can
greatly enhance science shows. Some science centres now use real-time high-
definition slow-motion filming to closely examine phenomena by playing back
demonstrations.



Large Format Film Theatres

Large science centres in metropolitan areas will often have a large format film
theatre as part of the science centre’s product offering. Such large-screen experi-
ences are often memorable because of the quality and scale of the imagery. Many
films offer a 3-D experience to visitors. Often such large-screen film theatre
facilities are a commercial operation with separate charging taking advantage of
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View                 89


the fact that visitors to science centres also enjoy such films, particularly those with
a strong nature content.



Space Theatres

The older planetarium has evolved into the modern space theatre where, often under
a domed roof, the night sky can be projected onto the ceiling. Using optical or
digital projectors, backed up with some sophisticated multimedia applications,
space theatres can take visitors on voyages of exploration of the solar system and
beyond. Digital technology now allows other topics to be delivered within domed
theatres.



Object Theatres

Object theatres use mixed media to tell a story that is automated through the use of
audiovisual experiences, lighting control and various special effects. Although
set-up costs may be significant, once built such theatres can be operated simply
many times without presenter fatigue.




Enhancing the Visitor Experience

Science centres will often add more depth to the exhibition experience through
programmes of events, hands-on workshop activities, gallery talks and science
busking. Most centres will have a team of volunteers, often students or retired
scientists and engineers who will also interact with visitors to demonstrate things or
to help with certain exhibits. However, it is the trained science communicator who
can turn a good visit into a great experience.
   After hours, science centres frequently serve as venues for a range of community
programmes. Café Scientifique events that facilitate dialogue between scientists
and the public are a popular part of the mix for adult audiences. Talks, after school
clubs, sleepovers, themed evening and many other activities enhance the core
product of a typical science centre. Looking around the websites of leading science
centres reveals a wealth of creative ideas and events as the science centre works
with its local community.
90                                                                            G. Durant


Communicating Science Outside Science Centres

Outreach

Many science centres operate outreach programmes extending the science centre
experience to local, regional and national communities.
   The Shell Questacon Science Circus is probably the world’s best known and
most extensively travelled science centre outreach programme, and as such it
provides a good example of the successful elements of an outreach programme.
   The Shell Questacon Science Circus has now been operating for 28 years as a
partnership between a science centre, a university and a corporate supporter.
Questacon, Australia’s National Science and Technology Centre; the Australian
National University; and the Shell Company in Australia have been working
together since 1985 to ensure that communities in regional and remote parts of
Australia get a hands-on science experience. The science circus delivers a mobile
science centre comprising around 50 ultraportable hands-on exhibits, science shows
and a small shop. The science circus is delivered by postgraduate students from the
Australian National University’s Centre for the Public Awareness of Science who
operate the science circus as part of their master’s degree in science communi-
cation. The exhibits arrive at their regional destination in a large highly visible
truck, and the exhibits are typically set up in a community hall or school gym-
nasium. The students then travel to perform science shows in all of the schools
around the venue. School students and families visit the exhibition in the commu-
nity venue where more science shows are delivered. The science circus remains in
one location for a few days before travelling to the next location. A normal science
circus tour lasts for 4 weeks with the students running 4 such tours each year as part
of their coursework.
   The Questacon science circus model has led to many innovations in the design of
ultraportable exhibits and show delivery in geographically isolated areas. In
28 years, nearly 400 postgraduate students have operated the circus in cohorts of
around 15 students per year. It has proved to be an excellent training for the students
who have gone on to a wide variety of science communication roles worldwide.
Other science centres now operate similar outreach programmes, building long-
lasting relationships with the different remote schools and indigenous communities.
The science circus model works particularly well in a large country such as
Australia.
   The extent and impact of such outreach programmes depend upon the local
context for the science centre. Other models of science centre outreach are needed
in metropolitan areas where local communities can benefit from science shows and
workshops. In such settings, it is possible to set up longer-term programmes with
different communities and to set up events in the centre itself. Science centres in
such settings often run programmes for socially disadvantaged young people that
can help them onto good career paths through association with the science centre.
The New York Hall of Science has been doing exemplary work in this regard for
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View               91


many years helping young people from different ethnic backgrounds develop skills
by volunteering and then working in the science centre.



Using the Web

Science centres successfully use the Internet to convey visitor information, corpo-
rate information and educational materials. Good interactive experiences online
will ensure a high level of visitation and access to an international audience.
Science centres are often quick to exploit new opportunities on the Web and to
experiment with different software. The Exploratorium’s use of Second Life to
create a virtual science centre experience has attracted interest from around the
globe. It is now important that websites become accessible via mobile devices as
they are becoming the principal means of accessing the Internet for many people.
Applications such as Wikipedia, YouTube, Facebook, Twitter, Instagram and
Pinterest allow science centres to convey information and deliver experiences to
a much larger audience than those able to visit the centre. Social media can be a
significant marketing tool as well as a means for having online conversations about
science. Nowadays the visitors to science centres are creating their own content
around visits to science centres, sharing their images and experiences with friends
and the broader online community.



Video Conferencing

Increased broadband capability means that science centres are increasingly using
video conferencing to deliver educational output, to link scientists with the public
and to link science centres to each other. There is significant potential for science
centres to play a role in increased people to people international connectivity in the
future, particularly for those science centres within similar time zones.



Running a Science Centre

Establishing a science centre is a significant challenge. Keeping one going is often a
much harder undertaking. Science centres can be expensive to build and expensive
to run. When considering establishing a centre, it is critically important to consider
how much the particular country, city, region or town can afford to support the
centre on an ongoing basis. If funds are limited, it may be worth considering
running a science festival instead of establishing a science centre.
    Making the case for building a science centre requires a deep understanding of
the educational, economic and social impacts of science centres. The relative
92                                                                             G. Durant


importance of these will vary from place to place and influence arguments in favour
of establishment as well as defining the mission for a particular organisation. A
centre built primarily as a leisure attraction will have a different focus on enter-
tainment relative to education influencing the nature of exhibits, shows, the design
of the facility and its marketing. A centre built primarily for educational purposes
will have other priorities and will require different facilities such as laboratories for
school classes, for example.
   The publication ‘Before the Blueprint’ written by Peter Anderson and published
by the Association of Science and Technology Centers (ASTC) is a valuable
resource for those considering building a science centre.
   Anyone considering establishing a science centre should spend time doing some
research on the Internet, visiting different types of centres and talking to as many
CEOs and senior staff from existing science centres. There is much hard-won
practical experience available, and there are many people happy to share such
experience.



Operating Models

Science centres have various business models. Some are wholly independent
businesses and others are agencies of government. Some have boards that have
legal and financial responsibility for the centre and which appoint the CEO. Other
centres have advisory boards whose principal role is advocacy and partnership
building, with the legal and financial responsibility remaining with the government
agency that appoints the CEO.
    The CEO appointment is critical to the successful operation of science centres,
and such people come from many different backgrounds, from academia, from
government bureaucracies, from business or from politics. Whatever their back-
ground, today’s science centre leaders have to be multi-skilled focussing on the
‘what’, the ‘how’ and the ‘why’ of running a centre. Scientific knowledge, business
skills, communication ability, social stamina and passion are necessary prerequi-
sites. Science centre CEOs have to lead teams with many different skills and
manage many different types of stakeholders. They need to develop resources to
deliver their institutional missions. These resources are both human and financial.



Financial Resources

Like many cultural organisations, science centres have to work hard to balance
income and expenditure. They have to raise capital for buildings and exhibitions.
They have to meet their expenditure needs through earned income and from
government or third-party support. Science centres typically raise income from a
mix of sources such as by charging admission, through shop sales, through venue
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View                  93


hire, through sales of educational services, through exhibition hire, through exhi-
bition design and fabrication, through consultancy services and through sponsor-
ship. The expenditure of running science centres has to cover staff costs, facility
costs, marketing, IT systems and support, governance, legal costs, consultancy
support, exhibition and programme development, exhibition maintenance, events
and often much more. Most science centres receive some direct grant support from
government or local authorities.
   Although the ratio of government support to earned income varies, a 60:40
government support to earned income ratio is a useful indicative figure for planning
purposes. However, the costs of running centres and the ability to earn income will
vary significantly from country to country or city to city.
   Science centres are often difficult to classify in terms of their government
support. Science centres are sometimes administered by a Department of Arts and
Culture as tourist attractions or national institutions, sometimes they are admini-
stered by a Department of Education, and sometimes they are administered by a
Department of Science and Industry. There is no ideal situation.




Human Resources

Science centres are typically run by people who have a passion for science and a
passion for communicating science. The larger science centres can have several
hundred staff on the payroll with many different skills. Smaller science centres may
have less than ten, and those staff will have to be multi-skilled to cover off all of the
activities.




Staff and Training

Science centres are run by people for people. The creativity, skills and passion of
the staff are a critical component of what science centres offer. These front-of-
house activities, which tend to be in the limelight, have to be supported by strong
corporate expertise, good marketing, good retail ability, good IT systems, evalu-
ation expertise, good health and safety regime and ongoing training opportunities.
The creation of good exhibits and exhibitions requires strong design and technical
skills. Good websites and use of social media require appropriate expertise. Science
shows require informed staff able to develop good science content, undertake risk
analysis and deliver with theatrical ability.
   Most science centres will have a core of ongoing staff backed up by casual and
short-term appointments that help with balancing workloads. Most science centres
will have a strong cohort of volunteers who can add value to the work of the
organisation. The science centre needs to have a strong vision and values supported
94                                                                            G. Durant


by cooperative staff behaviour. Good planning, product, promotion, people, poli-
cies and partnerships have to be driven with passion.



Partnerships

Science centres need to work with many different partners to deliver their mission.
Enabling partners or sponsors are critical to providing financial resources. Knowl-
edge partners are critical in providing content. Partnerships with other cultural
institutions or tourist attractions can be important for marketing, and media partner-
ships are vital to promote awareness of the science centre and its activities.



Networks

Science centres exist as members of different networks locally, regionally, nation-
ally and internationally. There are now strong regional science centre networks
around the world such as the ASPAC, Asia Pacific Network of Science and
Technology, or ECSITE, European Network of Science Centres and Museums.
ASTC, the US-based Association of Science-Technology Centers, is the network
for North America that also serves as a worldwide network for the sector. Other
networks cover Latin America, North Africa and the Middle East, Southern Africa
and the Indian subcontinent. Most countries have national networks that pick up
other smaller science centres as well as the larger organisations that can afford to be
members of the international networks. Annual network conferences allow science
centre personnel to gather together, share expertise and discuss key issues. Atten-
dance at such a conference is a good way to make contacts and find sources of
support for start-up projects. Even if it is not possible to attend a conference, study
of the conference programmes online can be a useful source of information and
ideas. Science centres also may be parts of academic or educational networks,
business networks or networks of tourism attractions, depending upon their local or
national situation.



Visitors to Science Centres

Who comes to science centres? The audiences for science centres vary, but ideally
science centres should appeal to all sectors of society. The principal categories of
visitors are family groups with children and school students attending with teachers.
Families typically attend at weekends and during school holiday periods. School
visits are during term time. School visits are usually from upper primary to lower
secondary classes although younger and older school students also attend. Adult
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View              95


audiences tend to come for special events although there is a trend for more senior
citizen general visits. Many science centres offer early childhood programmes for
very young children. In common with many leisure attractions, science centres will
be very busy on public holidays and quieter when schools resume after holiday
periods. The patterns of visitation matter to science centres as they have to manage
the peak loads of public holidays without compromising the quality of a visit. They
also have to be able to manage staffing levels to match the peaks and troughs of
visitation patterns.



The Impact of Science Centres

What are the performance indicators for science centres? Visitor numbers alone
cannot judge the success of a science centre. Science centres have educational
impacts, economic impacts and societal impacts. Some of these impacts can be
measured although the cost of the necessary research is often prohibitive. How do
you assess the effectiveness of science communication in different science centre
programmes? How do you assess motivational impact? How do you assess the
overall satisfaction of visitors? How do you assess the impact of a centre’s outreach
programmes or their website? How do you assess the value of the partnerships?
How do you assess a centre’s value in terms of international engagement and
cultural diplomacy? There are many indicators of the success or otherwise of
science centres, but it is important to be able to demonstrate efficiency and
effectiveness to staff and stakeholders alike. Science centres need to develop an
effective evaluation strategy to gather the evidence for continual improvement and
advocacy.



The Future of Science Centres

The Evolving Science Centre

The first science centres had a focus on hands-on exhibits of physical phenomena,
maths and basic engineering with simple science demonstrations such as electrical
phenomena or chemistry. As science centres grew and matured, the range of topics
broadened. Exhibits developed into themed exhibitions. Science demonstrations
developed into science shows through additional narrative and through the intro-
duction of theatrical techniques. Science centres reached beyond their walls with
outreach programmes and outdoor exhibits. The Internet offered new possibilities,
and science centres were quick to exploit the opportunities for offering more
science content online as well as key visitor information. Social media and broad-
band Internet are opening up many additional possibilities for science centres to
96                                                                            G. Durant


exploit and help deliver their core mission and build audiences. As they mature,
science centres typically broaden their audience base demographic, offering spaces
and activities for early childhood learning as well as opportunities for adult
audiences.
   Science centres must continually adapt and evolve. They must remain relevant
and useful. They typically do not have collections to fall back upon as the core
reason for a visit, so they have to remain fresh, relevant and up to date. They must
offer meaningful authentic experiences in novel settings. They have to offer quality
experiences since they are competing for people’s leisure time and money. They
have to offer value for cost and they have to have impact.



A Future for Science Centres?

Good science centres are continually searching for relevance and looking to the
future. Mature science centres are able to offer much in the way of leadership for
the broader science communication sector or contribute to policy development.
Questacon, for example, is leading the Australian Government’s Inspiring Australia
national science communication strategy that involves running the annual National
Science Week and the Prime Minister’s Prizes for Science as well as administering
a grants programme for science communication activities. Questacon led the
consultations and produced the initial Inspiring Australia Report (2010) that led
to a budget response by the Australian Government. Questacon has established six
expert working groups to look at aspects of improving science in the media, sharing
indigenous knowledge and improving interagency cooperation in communicating
aspects of marine science research to Australians. Other science centres around the
world are also stepping up to take on additional roles and responsibilities such as
teacher training, science communication training and international aid develop-
ment. Many science centres continue to develop commercial activities such as
exhibit building, film-making, curriculum support and publications.
   The science centre sector is dynamic and growing. The expectation is that it will
continue to grow as more countries and cities develop science centres. Many
science centres are already working together across international borders and
connecting young people in different countries to discuss issues of common interest
such as that of water management, one of the topics chosen for the 2011 ASTC
SCEnaRioS project that involved the Guangdong, Singapore and Questacon science
centres as well as other science centres in different regions. This project is just one
example of how science centres cooperating globally can work with organisations
such as the United Nations, in this case in the context of the Rio + 20 Summit.
   As science centre regional networks strengthen, science centres, with their focus
on science, young people and the future, will play a role in connecting communities
in our increasingly globalised world. Even in a world where knowledge is at
everyone’s fingertips, the authentic experiences in novel settings that science
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View                  97


centres offer will remain of value to families wishing to learn in a social setting and
to school students seeking exciting but relevant experiences.



Thinking About Setting Up a Science Centre

Many countries, people, cities or organisations would like to set up a science centre.
As director of one science centre, I regularly receive calls from people who would
like to set up a new centre. Sometimes the aim is to support education, sometimes it
is to support tourism, and sometimes it is for an entirely different reason. Museums
frequently want to include more hands-on activities to capitalise on engagement
through interactivity. Sometimes the enquiry will come from government officials,
sometimes from academics and sometimes from consulting organisations. Wher-
ever the source of the enquiry and whatever the reason for trying to establish a
centre, there are some basic steps to follow.
    In the early days of science centres, it was often the vision, passion and drive of
individuals that led to the establishment of a particular centre. Frank Oppenheimer
and the establishment of the Exploratorium, Richard Gregory and the establishment
of the Bristol Exploratory, John Beetlestone and the establishment of Techniquest,
Mike Gore and the establishment of Questacon all provide examples of this
approach. In each of these cases, the pattern seems to have been to get started on
a small scale and grow. None of these centres had an easy birth, and all have
important lessons for new start-up projects. Setting up something from nothing is
not easy.
    Other start-up projects emerge from political drive, from a decision of govern-
ments or cities to establish a science centre. Starting a science centre requires a
venue, exhibits, people and funding. It requires commitment and a lot of hard work.
It can be very rewarding and very frustrating.



Venues

The ideal venue for a new science centre requires good public access, one or more
large open spaces as exhibition areas, some smaller rooms for educational acti-
vities, a theatre space, rest rooms, a café, a shop and offices. The ASTC publication
‘Before the Blueprint’ itemises the balance of spaces needed for these various
elements.
   Start-up projects typically find space in unused buildings, but such buildings can
be expensive to convert or maintain. Industrial units or vacant spaces in shopping
centres can be useful start-up venues.
   The location is critical. A poorly located centre will struggle to attract visitors no
matter how good the content. Co-locating with other attractions or alongside an
academic institution can be beneficial in different ways.
98                                                                               G. Durant


Exhibits

With basic woodworking and mechanical workshop facilities, it is possible to make
a good start-up set of exhibits. The Exploratorium’s exhibit ‘cookbooks’ are a very
useful resource to get started in hands-on exhibit building. The exhibits use simple,
easily sourced materials to display intriguing phenomena. A workshop and techni-
cally skilled staff to build and maintain exhibits is a critical requirement for science
centres. This style of hands-on exhibit allows great flexibility of operation. Exhibits
can be easily moved on and off the exhibition floor, maintenance is relatively
straightforward, exhibits are safe and visitors seem to enjoy the simplicity. Each
exhibit operates as a single experience that doesn’t require visitors to move through
the centre in a particular order. Themes can be clustered. Dark spaces can be created
for optics exhibits. Costs are kept low.
    Bigger budgets allow more advanced exhibits to be purchased or developed and
thematic exhibitions can be considered. This step-up brings in a requirement for
new skills such as exhibition design or contract management. This is an area where
it is important to get good advice from more experienced institutions.



People

Science centres are run by people for people. Building a team of the right people
with the right skills is critical. Passion for science and enthusiasm for the project
can take you a long way but professionalism is needed. Recruiting, training and
retaining staff is a significant challenge. The role of the project leader is to articulate
the vision, build the partnerships, generate the resources needed and build the team
for the project and subsequent operation. As the project grows, the leader will need
to let go of many of the tasks and trust others to deliver. Competent managers have
to emerge to develop the exhibition product, deliver science communication acti-
vities and events and operate the facility. An effective organisational structure and
strong processes have to be developed.




Partnerships

Partnerships are critical. Enabling partners include funders, sponsors, philanthro-
pists, tourism associations and schools organisations. Universities or colleges may
be able to build exhibits or provide some core elements that can be turned into
exhibits. Knowledge partnerships provide expertise in science or engineering for
activities, demonstrations or talks.
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View                 99


Governance

Science centres have to be set up properly. Decisions have to be made about the
business structure and its governance. Most science centres operate as not-for-profit
organisations rather than commercial businesses. A board will need to be
established to have the legal and fiscal responsibility and appoint a CEO. The
relationship between board chairman and CEO is critical. Other science centres
are set up within a government structure with a government department, such as
education, culture or science, appointing the science centre CEO with science
centre staff appointed as public servants. An advisory board will provide advice
and advocacy but not have the legal and fiscal responsibility that remains a
responsibility of the government department. Some science centres are set up as
part of an academic institution with staff on a university payroll.



Outreach

An operating science centre has the opportunity to take exhibits and demonstrations
out from the centre to create opportunities for disadvantaged communities who may
not be able to visit the centre. In cities there are typically areas of socioeconomic
disadvantage or areas with large immigrant communities. Programmes targeting
disadvantaged and/or minority communities should be sensitive to cultural nuances.
Rural areas are often too far from the centre to visit easily. This is where the science
centre can make a difference through touring programmes or exhibitions. Such
outreach programmes with their wider geographic coverage focus on overcoming
disadvantage, and higher visibility can be attractive to sponsors.



Events

Special events are a good way for a science centre to attract visitors; support
national and international events, e.g. World Water Day; work with local partners;
and get some media coverage.



Setting Up the Glasgow Science Centre: A Personal Case Study

In 1988 the travelling Discovery Dome was set up at the University of Glasgow.
Partially funded by industrial sponsorship, this 2-week travelling science centre in
tents allowed the public and university administration to experience the excitement
of hands-on activities. This in turn led to the establishment of the Glasgow Dome of
100                                                                            G. Durant


Discovery with further support from the industrial partner and the university.
Operating for 25 months from 1990 on a shoestring budget in a redundant building
with low-cost exhibits, the Glasgow Dome of Discovery offered good proof of
concept for a science centre in Glasgow, building public and institutional support.
The Glasgow Dome of Discovery needed to grow, and larger unused buildings were
being examined for a second next phase when the establishment of a UK National
Lottery to fund good causes led to a call for projects to celebrate the millennium.
    A project led by the city development agency was set up, and after much work to
make the case and build support, lottery funding allowed the creation of the
purpose-built Glasgow Science Centre that opened in 2001. As cofounder of the
Glasgow Dome of Discovery and Director of Science and Exhibits for the Glasgow
Science Centre project, I learned much about the establishment and operation of
both small and large science centres.
    The scales and budgets were very different, but the principles are the same. Visit
existing centres, attend sector conferences, study science centre websites, seek
advice, build a team, generate resources, start small, plan for growth, know the
market, find someone to bankroll the project, work hard, communicate well, learn to
walk before you run, and develop a sound business plan.
    It is critically important to develop an appropriate business model that includes
both capital start-up costs and a realistic assessment of ongoing financial support for
the operation and growth of any new science centre. It is also important to recognise
that there are different phases to a start-up project that require different skill sets.
    Creating a science centre is essentially a project that works to a finite deadline,
the opening of the centre. Operating a science centre is a long-term open-ended
commitment with a different set of stakeholders and relationships with the com-
munity. Managing the transition from the project phase to the operating phase has to
be handled well. The project team is generally exhausted by the push to completion.
A fresh team is needed to greet visitors from day one.
    Typically an amount of capital is acquired for the start-up project. This can be a
mix of funding from governments, academic and university partners and from
philanthropic sources. This funding comes into the start-up organisation in different
ways with different commitments for accounting for the funds. A good banker is
needed. This funding is expended on creating and fitting out a venue, developing
exhibits, developing marketing and educational resources, creating a website,
staffing and much more. The funding progressively runs down as the bills are
paid. There is then a critical period when the available funds may run out before
the opening day. Any delays in the project can have major financial impact at this
stage. Project staff will still need to be paid. Operational staff are now on the payroll
and undergoing training. Building operating costs start generating bills. Preopening
marketing costs may be significant. Shop stock has to be purchased.
    When the new centre opens, visitor revenue can start accumulating, helping to
balance the budget for operating costs. A business plan will predict a certain level of
revenue, but the exact amounts and the exact costs of running a centre are not really
known until 2 or 3 years into the operation. Visitor numbers are typically high in the
first year of operation of a new facility and then dip. The income-expenditure model
A Role for Science Centres in Communicating Science: A Personal View               101


has to account for this. The annual patterns of visitation can be predicted, but
typically there are major peaks and troughs. The centre will have to cope with very
busy days and very quiet days. It is critically important to be able to adjust staffing
levels to cope with these scenarios. A pool of casual staff and volunteers can
support a core workforce on such occasions. The busy days, usually holiday
weekends, are when the visitor services staff, the facility and the exhibits really
get tested. The centre has to be able to deal with these peak loads. All visitors have
to be warmly greeted, exhibits have to be kept in good working order, the café has to
be able to cope and the facility has to be kept clean. At the end of the day, staff are
exhausted and possibly exhilarated. They have to do the same again the next day.
    The centre has to operate as both an educational facility and a tourist attraction.
The school market and the tourism markets are different but often complementary
with schools typically attending during school term times and tourists at weekends
and during holiday periods. In order to attract repeat visits, the science centre will
need to offer a regular programme of new experiences such as different exhibits,
special events and science demonstrations. Good partnerships with local univer-
sities can help with such programming.
    Once a science centre is up and running, it will need access to capital injections
for new exhibits, new experiences or facility growth. Seeking additional sources of
funding becomes a major task for the organisation alongside the day-to-day
operations.
    In the early days, a new science centre will often have a small, very enthusiastic
team with good communication and a strong sense of purpose. As the centre grows
larger, it may require a different organisational structure and different processes for
communication, training, etc. The enthusiasm may still be there, but the back-of-
house functions looking after human and financial resources, internal and external
communications, legal matters and much more need to be stronger as the organ-
isation grows.
    All of these factors are important in setting up a science centre. In the case of
Glasgow, a travelling hands-on exhibition led to a small year-round operation
which in turn paved the way for a larger newly built facility when the opportunity
emerged. A similar growth pattern occurred in the case of the establishment of
Questacon in Canberra, Australia.
    The centre started at the university through the enthusiasm and drive of a lecturer
in physics. A small centre was then set up in an old school building with a range of
exhibits that had been built or donated. An outreach programme began touring
exhibits and demonstrations to various locations across the country. The organ-
isation was ready to seize the opportunity for growth when it came along. In 1988
Questacon became Australia’s National Science and Technology Centre in its
purpose-built new building alongside other national cultural institutions. Questacon
continues to grow.
    I have been privileged to work in the science centre sector for over 25 years.
I have learned much and continue to do so. I have made good friends who lead
science centres around the world, friends who share a passion for science, for
science communication and for science centres. Collectively we and our many
102                                                                                    G. Durant


science centre colleagues, supporters and partners have already made a difference
to many lives. Science centres will continue to make a difference to many more
lives and, as the sector grows, create new opportunities for those who do not yet
have easy access to the experiences that science centres offer. Countries that do not
have science centres should be helped and that help is available. The signatories of
the 2008 Toronto and 2011 Cape Town Declarations from the 5th and 6th Science
Centre World Congresses, respectively, have committed to offer such help. Work-
ing together, the world science centre community can help make the world a better
place, offer hope and provide solutions thinking on a global scale. Lifelong learning
outside the classroom is the new paradigm, and science centres are well placed to
play a major role in this regard in countries around the world.



A Short Bibliography

There is an extensive literature for science centres, and investing time surfing the Web will
    uncover a great deal of useful information. These publications are a good place to start if
    considering setting up a science centre.
Anderson, P. (1991). Before the blueprint: Science center buildings. Washington, DC:
    Association of Science and Technology Centers.
Grinell, S. (2003). A place for learning science starting a science center and keeping it running.
    Washington, DC: Association of Science and Technology Centers.

    Springer.
