Universities
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science
Communication: Educating and Training
Science Communicators to Work
with Communities

Nancy Longnecker and Mzamose Gondwe




Abstract Graduate degree programmes in science communication have a role to
play in developing the professional capacity of both scientists and science commu-
nicators. University-based science communication programmes build local capac-
ity that is theoretically grounded to ensure that scientific research can be
communicated effectively with those who need it. Science communication
programmes make contributions to the development of evidence-based policy as
well as to assist in public involvement and use of science. And so it is particularly
important for people in developing countries to develop understanding, skills and
expertise in science communication. Collaboration between scientists, academics
and professional communicators in programme development can lead to authentic
educational experiences with immediate and long-term positive outcomes for all
parties. Curriculum should be theoretically grounded while also providing oppor-
tunities for students to think like a professional science communicator, to hone
skills needed in their careers, to develop collaborative networks and to create
communication resources to add to their portfolio. Products from science commu-
nication programmes can include media releases, consultancy evaluation reports,
communication strategies, displays, science interviews and stories. These should be
useful to professional science communicators in kick-starting their careers as well
as to the organisations for whom the products are created. This chapter describes an
educational philosophy that can be used in programme development, a checklist to
help determine programme priorities and examples that have been tried and tested.




N. Longnecker (*)
Centre for Science Communication, The University of Otago, Dunedin, New Zealand
e-mail: nancy.longnecker@otago.ac.nz
M. Gondwe
School of Animal Biology, The University of Western Australia, Crawley, WA, Australia
e-mail: mzamose.gondwe@uwa.edu.au

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,          141
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_9, © Springer Netherlands 2014
142                                                      N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


Introduction

This chapter provides information for those who are developing or supporting
university science communication programmes. We outline types of science com-
munication and communicators and core knowledge for professional science com-
municators. Considerations for setting priorities in curriculum development are
summarised, and a few examples of existing education programmes for science
communication are given to illustrate diversity in focus. While general guidelines
for curriculum development are presented, specific examples are used to illustrate
key points. The tension between providing a theoretical background and real-world
experience is discussed. We then conclude with strategies to facilitate students
learning the theoretical underpinning of the discipline while applying their learning
in authentic, professional practice.
   Our experience is in Australia which has a dynamic and vibrant community of
professional science communicators who are generous in sharing their experiences
and expertise. It is more challenging to develop authentic education opportunities
when professional science communicators are a scarce resource. Nonetheless, it is
important to identify potential collaborators and contributors from the variety of
professionals, including those with expertise in journalism, informal education,
agricultural extension and health promotion.
   Guiding principles that we recommend to use in developing a university science
communication programme are that: (1) developers should identify key priorities
that address local needs and respect and utilise local knowledge when determining
focus of any new science communication programme; (2) content should reflect
current best practice of professional science communicators; (3) students should be
provided real-world experiences and interact with professionals as much as is
practicable; (4) assignments are generally better than examinations as a mechanism
of assessment for motivating students, providing useful learning experiences and
determining communication learning; (5) free choice of topics for assignments
allows students to apply what they are learning in more personally relevant and
useful ways; and (6) authentic assessments motivate students and can be used to
integrate theory and practice.



Types of Science Communication and Communicators

Science communication is a multidisciplinary field that is underpinned by grounded
theoretical frameworks and practitioner strategies integrated from a wide range of
disciplines, including communication studies, journalism, public health, marketing
and advertising, education, public relations, museology, psychology, social sci-
ences and humanities.
   Science communicators from all disciplines present scientific content in a
culturally relevant context, fulfilling the role of ‘science interpreters’ who promote
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .       143


interactions between scientist/science and the target associates and users of the
science. Straight interpretation is an imperfect analogy in that science communica-
tors can also participate in advising and devising strategy in order to improve the
use of scientific information and evidence in personal and policy decision making.
    The diversity of science communicators is seen in the array of participants at the
biennial Public Communication of Science and Technology international confer-
ences. The conferences attract journalists, educators, museologists, communication
officers, evaluators, researchers, social scientists, bloggers, artists, librarians, public
health officers, scientists, musicians, performers, animators, environmentalists,
filmmakers and authors. These professionals are affiliated with different types of
organisations including research institutions, government bodies, environmental
agencies, nongovernment organisations, museums and science centres, media
houses, institutions of learning and design studios.
    As illustrated in this book, a diversity of work comes under the broad umbrella of
science communication, usually falling into one of three categories: (1) informal
education, (2) corporate communication and (3) science media. It is important to
acknowledge that the three categories can overlap and, in some workplaces, one
person can service more than one category. We acknowledge that the terms used to
describe these categories are imperfect and so provide a brief description of how we
use each term.
    Firstly, we use the term informal science education to refer to learning science in
out-of-school contexts (Rennie, 2007). A diverse range of sites, contexts and life
experiences can be considered informal learning environments: everyday experi-
ences; interactions with media through gaming, television, radio and the Internet;
visits to nature parks, zoos, science centres and museums; and participation in
citizen science programmes. Informal science educators can be responsible for
developing resources for these settings, e.g. games, shows, exhibits, interpretative
signage and/or walks and educational outreach programmes.
    Secondly, we use the term of corporate science communication for the broad
range of activities that include developing and managing internal and external
communication strategies and articulating the application of science to practice or
policy. This can involve working directly with end users of research results from the
employing organisation (which may be a government agency), media relations such
as responding to media inquiries, public relations, developing the corporate image,
training scientists to engage with the media, crisis communication, managing
misinformation and working with policy makers (Bauer & Gregory, 2007).
    Thirdly, we use the term science and media to refer to science journalism (Blum,
Knudson, & Marantz Henig, 2006), the production of various media, e.g. books,
films, news articles and websites, as well as use of social media (Bauer & Gregory,
2007). Science communicators create resources for publication in print, broadcast
and electronic media. They may be science journalists, authors or documentary
makers. Science journalism work requires specific skills taught in specialised
courses with input from professionals, while other science media can be learnt in
more general courses.
144                                                      N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


    In spite of the differences between these three categories of science communi-
cation, at the heart of all science communication is a respectful awareness about the
needs of the relevant community, willingness to collaborate with the community in
the development of greater scientific awareness and understanding, conceptua-
lisation and development of storytelling with key message(s) and familiarisation
with appropriate science communication tools and channels in order to effectively
communicate with different communities. The Public Engagement Triangle devel-
oped by Science for All Expert Group in the UK provides a tool that blends three
main science communication approaches: Transmit, Receive and Collaborate (Pub-
lic Engagement for Science and Society – A Conversational Tool, 2010). The tools
help define approaches and methods of effectively engaging the public.
    There is a wide and comprehensive range of science communication activities,
and as technology continues to develop at a rapid pace, the number of communi-
cation channels available to connect science and society are growing (Research
International, n.d.). In all cases the type of activity, its aim and the medium used
must be suited to the target user. A few examples illustrate different strategies and
channels to accomplish different objectives: (1) to encourage students to pursue
careers in science, a scientists in school programme may be a useful strategy; (2) to
develop effective policy, public consultation may be needed; and (3) for dissemi-
nating research findings or applications to raise awareness in a wide cross-section of
the general public, a media campaign can be the most appropriate strategy.




What Should Science Communicators Know?

Goals of science communicators include increasing people’s awareness and under-
standing of scientific methods, concepts and issues and to apply that understanding
in their lives. To teach how to achieve these goals, science communication
programmes draw from the key cognate areas of science, education, studies of
science in society and communication studies (Mulder, Longnecker, & Davis,
2008) as well as marketing, media studies and psychology.
   Despite the interdisciplinary nature and diversity of work, there are key charac-
teristics that define science communicators, and that should be covered in a degree
programme. Thinking like a science communicator involves understanding how
people communicate, how people learn, what is knowledge, how to determine
credibility of sources and information and what to believe, what factors impact
our worldviews, how attitudes and behaviour can be changed and when and how
particular media can be used to reach specific audiences.
   Science communicators should understand that they must know about their
target audience and respect their users’ needs (Crona & Parker, 2011; Gerodimos,
2008; Manzini, 2003; Meier, 2011) and ability to actively participate in knowledge
generation and use. They must also understand the needs of the scientists and
organisations that want to communicate (Katz, Solomon, Mee, & Lovel, 2009;
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .   145


Palmer & Schibeci, 2012). They should be familiar with market research that
enables them to better understand their target audience (Meier, 2011).
   The deficit model should be familiar to science communicators (Sturgis &
Allum, 2004). Science communicators will avoid the incorrect but frequently
used assumption that when people are provided with clear scientific information,
they will hold the same views and attitudes as the scientific community and will
change their behaviour (Cialdini, 2003; Lee & Garvin, 2003). Science communi-
cators will be familiar with a variety of modes of communication that may be more
appropriate to the community’s needs (e.g. dialogic, facilitation) and more likely to
achieve their objectives (Bauer, Allum, & Miller, 2007; Lee & Garvin, 2003; Nisbet
& Scheufele, 2009).
   Science communicators should understand the science that they are communi-
cating. They should be able to design and create appropriate and effective resources
that meet their audiences’ needs based on empirical evidence about what makes
particular resources more likely to succeed (Nye & Hargreaves, 2009; O’Neill &
Nicholson-Cole, 2009). They should understand that how messages are framed
impacts how those messages are received (Chong & Druckman, 2007; Kahneman,
2011; Maheswaran & Meyers-Levy, 1990; Schultz & Zelezny, 2003; Stiff &
Mongeau, 2003; Tversky & Kahneman, 1981). Science communicators should
also be familiar with methods that can be used to evaluate communication resources
to determine their success (Brossard, Lewenstein, & Bonney, 2005; Creswell, 2009;
Krippendorff, 2013; Kvale & Brinkmann, 2009; Yin, 2009).
   As evident by the discussions throughout this book, there are numerous mech-
anisms and media for communicating about science with the public. There are
similarly different contexts or areas of science that are typical foci of science
communication with the public (e.g. health, agriculture, environment, new tech-
nologies, etc.). In a discipline as diverse as science communication that spans a
wide breadth of science areas, it is usually necessary to consider and set priorities
for a new graduate degree programme. Unless resources are sizable, one
programme cannot serve all needs; objectives will need to be set and a focus for
the new programme determined.
   Some universities may have to determine policies such as the most appropriate
language(s) for tuition and for resource production. Modes of delivery and hence
focus of skill development may vary depending on local customs, needs and
available resources. Delivery and interaction can be via mechanisms as diverse as
street theatre and touch-screen technology kiosks. For example, face-to-face drama
and theatre has been used as an effective communication mode for health- and
development-related messages (Gosh, Patil, Tiwari & Dash, 2006; Mlama, 1991).
In the Mekong Delta of Vietnam, radio was useful in reaching farmers in the 1990s
(Heong, Escalada, Huan, & Mai, 1998). Communicators in India have used touch
screens in markets to provide local producers with information in English and
regional languages about silk-related information (Kirsur, Phaniraj, Jadhav, &
Qadri, 2010).
146                                                              N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


Setting Priorities to Focus on Effective and Useful Local
Degree Programmes

To design a programme that can effectively cater to a community’s needs, some key
questions must be considered. Will it be most useful to the community for the
programme to specialise in a particular context or mode of communication
(e.g. science journalism with a focus on health or museology with a focus on
physical science and technology)? Or is it preferable for the programme to provide
graduates with capabilities across a spectrum of sciences and channels? Another
question to be asked early in the planning process is whether it is preferable for the
programme to provide depth or breadth. With limited resources, it can be difficult to
provide both, so curriculum developers must consider their options and community
needs carefully.
   The backdrop of our perspective is a current push within Australia for improved
science communication, exemplified by the Inspiring Australia strategy, which
states that
   There are four key reasons why the Australian Government should invest in science
   communication, alongside investing in science. These are to increase appreciation of
   science in Australian culture, facilitate informed citizen participation in decision making
   and science policy development, boost confidence in the Australian Government’s research
   investment and ensure a continuing supply of well‐qualified science graduates. (Inspiring
   Australia A National Strategy for Engagement with the Sciences, 2010, p. 4)

   In India, the Department of Science and Technology in the Ministry of Science
and Technology Communication has a mandate of ‘popularisation of science and
technology’ through the National Council for Science and Technology Communi-
cation (NCSTC). Science communication efforts in this regard are focused around
using various methods to increase public awareness, understanding and interest in
science. One way to do this is through development of books, scripts, films and
video and radio programmes such as a 33 part radio serial titled Jeevan Ek Roop
Anek in 2004 on Biodiversity with All India Radio (National Council for Science &
Technology Communication, 2005).
   Institutional science communication objectives are motivated by political, cul-
tural, and social contexts, the target audience needs and the availability of expertise
and resources. In one example in Indonesia, food production and the environment
pose critical challenges to the country’s development. The House of Natural Fiber
in Yogyakarta is a new-media art laboratory which brings artists and scientists
together to tackle local issues. Its Micronation/Macronation project is an art display
and a series of seminars on innovative sustainable agriculture practices that combat
climate change (HONF Foundation, 2012).
   In Africa, although no formal university science communication programmes
exist, increasingly regional bodies like the New Partnership for African Develop-
ment, the African Union and the United Nations Economic Commission Africa are
encouraging governments to make science more accessible to the public
(UNESCO, 2011). The focus has mainly been on training science journalists and
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .   147


media-ready scientists through workshops and providing informal science educa-
tion experiences for students, but major science and technology projects such as the
Square Kilometre Array, the introduction of genetically modified organisms in the
agricultural sector and climate change require science communicators to commu-
nicate with the public (Outram, 2010).
   Short professional science communication courses or workshops exist such as
the science communication postgraduate short course for the 24 universities
involved in the Development Research Uptake in sub-Saharan Africa (DRUSSA,
2012). However, these types of short programmes rely on a small pool of interna-
tional consultants and therefore are costly, provide poor opportunities for ongoing
professional development and do not build local capacity for teaching science
communication. If scientific research is a serious priority for economic develop-
ment in African countries, then science communication tertiary education, whether
as stand-alone university courses or integrated within science faculties, cannot be
overlooked.
   The absence of tertiary science communication programmes in many developing
countries could be a result of a number of factors: governments’ concentration on
capacity building in scientific research, low awareness of the value of science
communication skills or a lack of resources, personnel and infrastructure to provide
science communication programmes. Whichever is the case, in order for countries
to develop scientific research programmes that support economic and social devel-
opment, they will need to draw from students who are interested in and engaged
with science. Additionally, knowledge brokers or science communicators are
required to cultivate a scientifically literate public and to design engagement
activities that recognise local indigenous knowledge systems.
   Journalist’s stock questions of who, what, when, where, why and how can be
used to consider what key information is needed in the early stages of planning a
new degree programme. Considering the questions below and having a few sce-
narios in mind can serve as a preliminary checklist and help design an appropriate
degree programme for the particular community.




Objectives

Why should science be communicated in a particular community? What are that
particular community’s needs? What are the key objectives? Are these objectives
realistic and achievable? When might they be achieved? Who are the most appro-
priate target audiences? Which strategies are most likely to achieve the communi-
cation objectives?
148                                                    N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


Target Audiences

Who will benefit? What are their needs, interests and expectations? What is known
about their values, attitudes, awareness and understandings in relation to the
scientific context in question? Why would they participate in communication
activities? When is the information most needed or likely to be of most use?
Where will the communication be most effective? How are the audiences likely
to participate or respond?



Channels, Mechanisms and Teaching

What are the media or channels that would be best for communicating with the
target audiences? How are the key communication objectives likely to be effec-
tively and efficiently achieved? When is communication most likely to be effective?
Who is available and best able to develop and deliver the programme? What
existing resources and collaborations might be called upon for delivery?



Students

Who will enrol? Are they prospective professional science communicators who will
communicate a range of science or are they scientists who want to be better
communicators of their own work? What are their strengths and weaknesses?
Why do they want to communicate science? What are their motivations and
aspirations? When will they most benefit from the degree and be able to put what
they learn into practice? Where will they be able to work?




Industry/Employers

What infrastructure and other resources exist that can be used by graduates and
contribute to the course? Where are industry and employers located? Who will
employ the graduates? When are employers most likely to take on graduates? Why
would graduates be of use to potential employers? Which graduate capabilities will
be most useful? What skills and knowledge will be needed?
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .   149


Teaching and Learning in Science Communication

University programmes of science communication teach communication theory
and skills for the effective interpretation, engagement and awareness of science
for different audiences. Typically essential skills for science communicators
include media skills training – understanding and working in or with the media,
public speaking, presentation and facilitation skills, writing and editing skills and
considerations for targeting specific audiences. Optional studies for science com-
munication degree students depend on the particular programme and include
creative nonfiction science writing, documentary film-making, exhibition develop-
ment and science theatre. During science communication training, students become
familiar with science communication models from the top-down ‘deficit model’ to
consultation and engagement models as well as research into science communica-
tion models for developing countries and emerging economies (Greco, 2005).
Although it is now widely accepted that the deficit-transmission model is frequently
inappropriate and is still overused as a default approach, a case can be made for its
utility in given contexts (Sturgis & Allum, 2004).
   The composition of education programmes for professional science communi-
cators is closely linked to institutional and national science communication objec-
tives as well as local champions. Distinctions can be seen in programme foci of
science communication programmes at universities around the globe (Hong &
Wehrmann, 2010; Mulder et al., 2008; Trench, 2012).
   Mulder et al. (2008) examined university science communication programmes
around the world and considered four areas that support science communication as
a discipline: science, communication studies, education and social studies of
science. An understanding of science is vital for science communicators, and
some programmes require students to major in science at an undergraduate level
or have a science degree prior to enrolling in a master’s level science communica-
tion programme. Communication studies are a common element in all university
programmes. This teaches students communication theory and skills to interpret
and effectively produce a diversity of communication materials from press releases
to museum exhibits. Education studies can provide understanding of learning
theories associated with informal learning or informal science engagement for
both children and adults. Social studies of science or science and technology studies
(STS) are centred on ethics, policy, history and philosophy of science. All four
cognate areas are combined with varied emphasis in different university science
communication programmes.
   After reviewing 20 university science communication programmes, Hong and
Wehrmann (2010) recommended that science communication curricula reflect the
profiles of professional science communicators. By resonating with real-world
practice, students are more competent and equipped for professional practice.
Their review found that many universities provided vague programme objectives,
lacked information on the basic requirements of the above cognate areas and related
poorly to science communication professional practice. They recommended
150                                                      N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


programmes that have specialisations should stress their strengths and market their
uniqueness; for example, the Laurentian University emphasises developing practi-
cal skills for personnel at science centres.
   Here, we share the composition of science communication studies at the Uni-
versity of Western Australia (UWA). We make no claim that the approach taken by
this programme is superior and acknowledge that there are useful examples from
other programmes. Our comprehensive knowledge of the UWA programme and
involvement with its development from its beginnings enables us to share specific
knowledge that may help in the development of other programmes.
   The specific classes (units) recommended to students in the UWA degree depend
on their focus. Core units for all students are Science Writing, Science Presenta-
tions, a literature review unit, and Evaluating Science Engagement. All students in
the research stream must prepare their research proposal and conduct a substantial
dissertation research project, and students are encouraged to aim to publish at least
one journal article from their research. Students whose focus is informal science
education also take Exhibitions and Interpretation, Science Performance and Learn-
ing Technologies and select elective classes from the Graduate School of Educa-
tion. Those students whose focus is corporate science communication add to their
core units Communication Strategies for Change, Science and the Media and
usually select elective classes from the marketing discipline in the Business School.



Examples of Diversity in Programme Development

Historically the early development and subsequent trajectory of many university
programmes reflects the strength, expertise and interest of their founders. We stress
that in countries with small population bases, it can be better for programmes to have
unique strengths that service particular needs. Collaborative approaches and poten-
tial for exchanges between programmes could be used to provide greater depth and
breadth than can be achieved if each programme attempts to provide everything
across the broad spectrum of science communication. This provides students in the
region with greater breadth and depth of opportunities. Examples from Australia and
New Zealand are presented to illustrate diversity in existing successful science
communication programmes and their focus in these two countries.
    In New Zealand, the University of Otago’s postgraduate Master of Science
Communication has three specialisations, Science and Natural History Film-
Making, Creative Nonfiction Writing and Popularising Science. This reflects
strengths of the inaugural Director of the Centre for Science Communication,
Professor Lloyd Davis, who is an award-winning nonfiction author and factual
film-maker. A highlight in the annual calendar of the centre is the ScienceTeller
Festival which celebrates science documentary film-making.
    Across the Tasman Sea, the Centre for Public Awareness of Science (CPAS) at
the Australian National University (ANU) offers a unique Master of Science
Communication Outreach which enables students to travel to remote and regional
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .   151


areas of Australia with the Shell Questacon Science Circus to deliver science
performances and other science engagement activities. The Science Circus is the
foundation programme of CPAS. It has been built on a long-standing partnership
with Questacon, Australia’s National Science and Technology Centre, and based on
passions and skills of ANU Professors Chris Bryant and Sue Stocklmayer and
Questacon’s Founding Director, Dr. Mike Gore (Bryant, 2001). CPAS has
expanded over its many years and now also offers more general science commu-
nication courses.
   The Master of Science Communication at the University of Queensland is based
not in a science faculty but in the School of English, Media Studies and Art History,
making it unique amongst the current science communication programmes in
Australia and New Zealand. Availability of contributors from Media Studies and
the strong humanities and science and society background of its convenor, Associ-
ate Professor Joan Leach, means that there is a greater focus in this programme on
critical analysis of science in society.
   At UWA, postgraduate courses in science communication began with a broad
coverage in the areas of corporate communications and informal education, based
on contributions from Professor Longnecker, whose experience included profes-
sional science communication in a research organisation, and Associate Professor
Jan Dook, a developer of education resources and provider of professional devel-
opment for science school teachers. Because of an approach that included contri-
butions from the Graduate School of Education, the programme attracts science
educators as well as science communicators.



Bridging the Gap Between Theory and Practice

In designing university courses for professional graduates, there can be tension
between provision of theoretical background and practical experiences (Ginsburg,
Regehr, Stern, & Lingard, 2002; Greenberg, 2007; Van De Ven & Johnson, 2006).
An employer may justifiably expect that graduates are able to practise science
communication upon graduation. A theoretician may justifiably assert that gradu-
ates must have the theoretical knowledge to determine what to do and why it is
likely to be effective.
   Time is needed to cover both theory and practice well. The expertise needed to
teach both is likely to require collaboration between people with different back-
grounds. Practitioners who may be expert in delivery are not always conversant
with the theoretical foundation of what they do. In the development of science
communication as a discipline (Gascoigne et al., 2010), it is increasingly recognised
that both theory and practice are necessary for a truly useful programme and that
bridging the gap between theory and practice has the potential to improve efficiency
and effectiveness of science communication activities (Fishbein & Cappella, 2006;
Kollmuss & Agyeman, 2002).
   The tension between theory and practice can be relieved to some degree through
contributions of industry experts. For example, professional journalists can teach
152                                                      N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


students interview skills and share tacit knowledge about a science journalist’s
working environment. Professional science communicators can point out the reality
of constraints they face in their workplaces. The practicum placement described
below is one mechanism that helps students bridge the gap between theory and
practice.



Who Should Teach Science Communication?

It is vital to acknowledge that good science communication involves understanding,
skill and experience (Silva & Bultitude, 2009). There are scientists who are good
science communicators and who can make valuable contributions to an academic
programme. They may teach and inspire early career scientists by sharing their
experience and help science communicators by providing the perspective of scien-
tists who actively communicate. For scientists who are good communicators to
make a useful contribution to teaching science communication and especially if
they are involved in developing curriculum, it is vital for them to have reflected on
their own skills (Venville, Rennie, Hanbury, & Longnecker, 2013). They need to
recognise what makes them successful and be willing to engage with the discipline
of science communication and its literature. They must also be aware of the
growing evidence base of factors that make science communication more or less
effective. The rules of thumb they may have developed to guide their own com-
munication activities could work for them but may not necessarily represent best
practice nor be theoretically grounded.
    A teaching practice to be avoided involves assigning science academics with
neither interest nor experience to teach science communication based on the false
assumption that science communication is something that everyone can do and
therefore that anyone can teach. This is a practice that is both disturbing and
unfortunate and does a disservice to students and the community whom they will
serve as well as the assigned academic and the discipline of science communica-
tion; but it occurs with some regularity for teaching of ‘generic skills’ like com-
munication. This makes as much sense as asking someone who breathes to teach
about respiration. Communication is like breathing in that it is a natural part of the
human condition. Communication and breathing are both things that all of us
do. Because someone breathes does not mean they understand the physiology of
the process and can teach it without a serious amount of preparation. Similarly, just
because a scientist communicates does not mean that they have a clear understand-
ing of the theoretical framework that underpins the process nor even that they do it
well. And it certainly does not mean that they can teach it.
    Almost every student who comes into a postgraduate science communication
programme will be better at something than any individual teacher. Some students
come in with outstanding design skills. Some are excellent speakers or performers.
Some write beautifully. Some have detailed knowledge of their particular branch of
science. Some have particular insights about the community and networks that
enable effective engagement which may include dialogue or co-produced
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .   153


communication. Some are experienced teachers. It is possible to take advantage of
different students’ experiences and expertise and get them to share their expertise
with each other. A demonstrated way for students to learn and improve their existing
skills is to involve them as part of the community of scholars (Bartle, Longnecker, &
Pegrum, 2011; Biggs & Tang, 2007; Castelli, Longnecker, & Gondwe, 2012;
Longnecker, Castelli, & Gondwe, 2012; Marton & Säljö, 1976a, 1976b). Most
cohorts of our students include people with varying life and work experiences. In
an informal way, those with more experience mentor others within their peer group.
This benefits all parties as teaching others is an effective way to learn.



Evaluation and Research

A critical but sometimes overlooked part of science communication education is
familiarising students with evidence of effective science communication. Students
need to be exposed to existing evidence in peer reviewed and grey literature. A class
blog with a reading list of seminal papers and exemplars is a useful, assessable tool
in which students can engage with the literature and with each other, developing
into a community of scholars while practising another style of science writing
(Sullivan and Longnecker, in press; for example, see http://communication
strategies.wordpress.com/).
   Students of science communication should also become familiar with methods
of collecting their own evidence. This can include content analysis (Krippendorff,
2013) as well as formative and summative evaluation. Knowledge and some
working understanding of both quantitative and qualitative research methodologies
(Cresswell, 2009; Kvale & Brinkmann, 2009) are useful for practising science
communicators.
   Evaluation methodology is not always taught but is vital if science communi-
cation graduates are to be able to evaluate what they or others do in order to
improve the outcomes. One efficient mechanism for delivery when resources are
limited can be to use a research methodology class or unit provided by other
disciplines from the social sciences. Or evaluation experts can be brought in to
teach modules intensively until local capacity is developed. Additionally, some
exposure to evaluation methodology can be built into assessments. For example,
students might be asked to conduct an evaluation of an existing resource and
prepare a professional consultancy report. This enables students to learn something
about the methodology of evaluation as well as critically consider criteria that make
science engagement effective and develop the skill of professional writing.



Authentic Assessments

Assessments within university programmes should reflect current practice of pro-
fessional science communicators (Hong & Wehrmann, 2010). As discussed earlier,
154                                                            N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe


science communicators design and create appropriate and effective resources that
meet the needs of their target audiences, local communities and often those
scientific institutions that they represent. In order to design effective resources,
science communicators must understand the science that they are communicating,
understand how people receive and process information and learn and be aware of
factors that influence peoples’ attitudes and perceptions, what can influence behav-
iour change and when and how to use particular media to reach specific audiences.
A description of a few specific assessments is provided to illustrate how skills
training and theory can be integrated in a teaching programme.
   If practicable, students should be allowed to choose topics of personal interest.
This is not always possible as in two of the three examples given below in which
clients are organised by the teacher and students work as professional consultants.

   Displays
   In a class, Exhibitions and Interpretation, students examine key factors of
   effective interpretation and exhibition design. They put the theory that they
   learn into practice by developing and delivering an interpretive guided walk
   and designing and creating a display for a client. After completing the display
   assignment students have:
   •   A greater understanding of factors in effective exhibitions and displays
   •   Identified key objectives and target audiences for a display
   •   Experience in teamwork, design and construction of a display
   •   Evaluated their display by identifying constraints they faced in its design,
       creation and installation, describing the elements likely to be most suc-
       cessful and how others could have been improved




   Examples of displays developed and created by students; Frog life cycle (left) and Inter-
   national Centre for Radio Astronomy (right)

                                                                                  (continued)
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .       155



   Communication Strategy Consultancy
   In a class, Communication Strategies for Change, students learn about com-
   munication amongst experts, industry, policymakers and the general commu-
   nity. The aim of this assignment is for students to work as a team for a client
   in a professional consultancy capacity to develop a communication strategy
   for that client. The team presents their work by giving an oral consultancy
   presentation and producing a professional consultancy-style report.
      After successfully completing this assignment, students should be:
   • Able to more confidently participate in professional consultancy activities,
     either as a consultant or as a client
   • Familiar with examples of successful and unsuccessful communication
     activities and methodologies
   • Better able to choose appropriate strategies for different objectives and
     audiences
   • Familiar with methods of evaluating effectiveness of communication
     activities
   • Better able to work effectively and professionally in a team
   • Able to give a professional consultancy-style oral presentation
   • Able to submit a professional, consultancy-style report




   Examples of student team consultancy reports. These projects are usually conducted by
   teams of three students working for an external client.

                                                                              (continued)
156                                                             N. Longnecker and M. Gondwe



   Research Profiles
   Students need to be given opportunities to develop their interviewing skills and to
   write articles and produce other materials such as podcasts and videos (e.g. www.
   dayinscience.unsw.edu.au;       www.youtube.com/user/CommunicatingScience).
   Producing these materials are authentic tasks commonly undertaken or contracted
   by professional science communicators. The students’ products can be useful to
   the university and other research organisations. Students comment that producing
   materials that can be used is a motivating experience and can lift the quality of
   their work.




   Examples of student articles, published in a university newsletter, with student bylines.
   Students build their portfolio and the university benefits with more stories about current
   research.



Practicum Placements as a Capstone Experience

A practicum or work placement component in a degree programme serves multiple
purposes. It provides an opportunity for students to put into practice things they
have learnt in their degree programme by conducting a project in a ‘real-world’
workplace (Dook & Longnecker, 2008). In our experience, results of the practicum
project must be of potential value to the host in order for the placement to be
mutually beneficial. For students who are unsure what type of science communi-
cation they want to practise after graduation, it is an opportunity to test the water in
a particular type of workplace.
   Another outcome of practicum placements is the opportunity of creating relation-
ships with professionals in the industry. Students gain an opportunity to network with
professionals in their own practicum and through those of their classmates. This
Graduate Degree Programmes in Science Communication: Educating and Training. . .        157


provides a greater awareness of the variety of jobs in science communication and can
lead to job offers upon graduation. Academic teaching faculty increase their own
network of potential contributors to the programme and to collaborative communica-
tion activities. And the host benefits from having enthusiastic students working with
them and the opportunity to vet potential employees.
   Various hosts for practicum students from our programme include broadcasters,
producers, local museums, zoos, botanic gardens and other wildlife and environ-
ment agencies, nongovernment organisations, health agencies and government
departments. Host agencies need not be co-located. Indeed our students have
undertaken practicum placements in other Australian states and overseas.
Exchanges in other countries for short practicum placements of about 4 weeks
could be developed as a mechanism to broaden exposure of novice science com-
municators to various approaches and to develop broad understandings that can be
brought back to their own community. This would be most useful if the practicum
involved work on a specific project that is relevant either in context or approach.



Conclusions

It is not possible for a programme to teach all aspects of science communication if
resources are limited. It is better to determine the needs of the community, audit the
resources which are available and set priorities. Some of the questions provided in
an earlier section of this chapter can be used to guide discussions and determine
direction in development of a graduate science communication programme.
    Success can breed success. As students produce assessments, good examples can
be shown to subsequent cohorts who then have a better idea of what is expected of
them. Professional work and case studies should also be used. The bar will be high
in a professional course. Some graduates of the programme will be happy to assist
subsequent cohorts.
    A new science communication programme is likely to reflect the strengths and
interests of those who initiate and develop the programme. With forethought,
careful planning and communication and collaboration between universities,
programmes within a region can complement each other and provide a greater
range of opportunities for students.
