Outreach Activities by Universities
as a Channel for Science Communication

Lloyd Spencer Davis




Abstract Universities engage in outreach activities about science for a variety of
reasons, including enhancing their reputation, recruitment and the personal satisfaction
of those involved. Be that as it may, such activities are typically portrayed as a form of
science communication whereby the public is informed about science. Outreach activities
may be classified according to their costs, their reach (i.e. the audience size) and their
persistence (i.e. the duration of the activity and how long it is available to the public).
When costs of many activities traditionally favoured by universities as outreach for
science are weighed against their reach and persistence, they prove not to be the most
effective forms of outreach in terms of the value they provide. Encouraging and
facilitating staff (and, where appropriate, students) to engage in interviews about science
with the media as well as to popularise science – through writing books and articles for the
popular press and, where possible, being involved in documentaries about science – are
amongst the most effective means by which universities can communicate science to the
public. Enhancing such practices will require universities to recognise and reward staff
for popularising science, rather than rewarding only publications and citations in scien-
tific journals. Online outreach activities are also an area of great potential when it comes
to persistence and the size of the audience: promoting science on the university’s website,
encouraging staff to write blogs or contribute to group blogs or forums about science and
posting material about science to iTunes U are some of the most cost-effective ways to
communicate science to the public. When using more traditional forms of outreach – such
as public talks, café scientifiques and U3A – their effectiveness may be enhanced if they
occur regularly or are packaged as a group of activities in a way that the public can
subscribe to them. Talks in schools, often treated as a source of potential recruitment by
universities, can also be viewed as critical for helping to reverse a developing trend in
recent times of disinterest in science amongst secondary school pupils. Finally, there may
be social reasons favouring outreach activities by universities that go beyond a simple
cost-benefit analysis, such as engaging indigenous peoples in science.

L.S. Davis (*)
Centre for Science Communication, University of Otago, P.O. Box 56, Dunedin, New Zealand
e-mail: lloyd.davis@otago.ac.nz

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,          161
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_10, © Springer Netherlands 2014
162                                                                          L.S. Davis


Introduction

Universities play an important role in conducting research on science communica-
tion and providing education pathways for science communicators (see chapter on
“Science Communicators as Commercial and Social Entrepreneurs”). However,
universities also have a significant role in reaching out to various communities and
publics in ways that enhance understanding of science and instil favourable atti-
tudes towards science.
   This chapter will focus on specific ways that universities popularise science
and interact with those outside the university gates. Despite outreach being a
common and much-trumpeted aspect of science communication, with few
exceptions (e.g. Davis, 2007; Stocklmayer, 2003), there is a remarkable dearth
of literature comparing the relative merits of different types of outreach. As a
way of providing a guide to effectiveness or, more correctly, value (see
discussion below), each activity will be discussed in terms of its reach, cost
and persistence. To illustrate particular outreach activities, I will be concen-
trating on outreach experiences at the University of Otago in Dunedin,
New Zealand.



Reasons for Outreach

Outreach activities involving science are often portrayed as having the lofty goal of
increasing the enlightenment and engagement of the public with science. Indeed,
many governmental bodies that fund science (e.g. the US National Science Foun-
dation) now insist that a proportion of grants be expended on public outreach
activities. Outreach activities are certainly not new, having been around in some
forms since the days of Aristotle, but the prominence given to outreach, and the
expectation that scientists will engage in it, has become especially strong over the
last 20 years.
   However, while no doubt there is a degree of altruism in the motivations of
universities that undergo outreach, it should not be forgotten that often there are
other more self-interested agendas in play.
   Universities have a variety of reasons for promoting science through outreach
activities. First and foremost is their reputation: universities benefit from enhancing
their reputation for conducting top-quality science – especially science that is
relevant to the lives of those in their respective communities. The latter focus is
particularly important to the ongoing funding of science programmes within uni-
versities: they need to show the relevance of the science that is being done in order
to justify the levels of funding they are receiving from governmental sources.
Showing the public the benefits that accrue directly to them from such research,
then, is a vital part of the self-preservation of universities.
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication         163


   The second, related goal of university outreach programmes involving science –
and arguably the most proximate, if not the most important, motivator – is recruit-
ment. Science divisions within universities need to ensure they have an ongoing
supply of students. With science deemed less attractive as career options by many
high school-aged students (Boe, Henriksen, Lyons, & Schreiner, 2011), outreach
focused on school students is typically aimed at cultivating a pool of prospective
students.
   Thirdly, individual scientists – whether or not they believe that as
scientists they have an obligation to inform the public – stand to benefit in
several ways from the popularisation of science. Well-known popularisers of
science – for example, the likes of university professors Richard Dawkins
(Oxford University), Lawrence Krauss (Arizona State University) and Tim
Flannery (Monash University) – stand to make considerable money from the
sale of popular books they have authored and other outputs that have been
monetised (e.g. speaking fees, appearance fees, documentaries and the like).
Even for those producing outputs with much more modest income-generating
capacities, the additional income to be made from popularising science cannot be
dismissed as a significant motivator. Neither can the intoxication of fame or
notoriety that can accompany being in the public spotlight.
   Of course, not all the outreach stems from self-interest. Many practitioners of
science – whether researchers or teachers – genuinely wish to address what they
believe to be a fundamental issue with science: it is obfuscated behind a wall of
jargon, formulae and probability values that makes comprehension of science a
difficult matter for the public. Additionally, science has never been more important
to every aspect of daily life, and it behoves the average person to have an
understanding of science in order to make informed choices from such matters as
whether to get an LCD or plasma TV through to how to interpret the rhetoric around
global climate change and determine the most appropriate actions to take.



Measuring Effectiveness of Science Outreach Activities

Measuring the effectiveness of science outreach activities is not as simple, then, as
measuring increased awareness of, comprehension of and engagement in science.
The different agendas motivating outreach activities by universities and their staff
mean that success might in some instances best be measured by other factors, such
as brand recognition, funding success, recruitment, income and fame.
   For the purposes of this chapter, effectiveness of outreach activities will be
measured using three criteria – cost, reach and persistence – that mirror those used
in my paper discussing the popularisation of Antarctic science (Davis, 2007). In
reality, this is more a measure of value rather than effectiveness, per se: that is, it
represents the “bang for the buck” that is likely to be obtained with each category of
outreach activity. Such a cost-benefit analysis is predicated on the notion that, for a
given cost, an activity is going to be more “effective” (i.e. have more value) if it
164                                                                         L.S. Davis


reaches more members of the public and if the engagement and enlightenment can
be more persistent (i.e. occur over a longer period). This may or may not accord
with other drivers of outreach. For example, it is conceivable that a one-time
expensive programme of outreach targeting a select number of prospective students
may be deemed highly effective if recruitment is the primary motivator of the
activity and it, indeed, results in increased recruitment of students.




Definition of Criteria for Evaluating Effectiveness

Each criterion for effectiveness was broken into three broad categories:
• Cost: low <$1,000; medium $1,000–$10,000; and high >$10,000
   Cost is a measure of the total costs of an activity. All costs are in US dollars.
• Reach: low <1,000; medium 1,000–10,000; and high >10,000
   Reach is a measure of the audience for the activity or the number of people
participating in a given activity.
• Persistence: low <1 month; medium 1 month–1 year; and high >1 year
   Persistence is how long the activity went on for and was available for people to
interact with it (Davis, 2007).



Case Study Subject: The University of Otago

The University of Otago is New Zealand’s oldest university, established in 1869,
when it was known as the University of New Zealand. New Zealand now has eight
universities and the University of Otago is the second largest (over 22,000 students
in 2010) in a city of 104,000. The University of Otago is the top-ranked university
in the country for research quality (Anonymous, 2007).



The Role of University Science Communication Programmes
in Outreach Activities

Centres or programmes for science communication exist within universities pri-
marily to produce graduates capable of bridging the void that separates the com-
plexities of science from public understanding and to conduct research into how
best that might be done.
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication       165


    It is not uncommon for them also to be seen within their respective institutions
as vehicles for (1) training practising scientists to be better communicators
(i.e. helping facilitate university outreach), (2) promoting the science going on
within the institution (i.e. actively engaging in university outreach) and
(3) increasing the communication skills of students undertaking degree
programmes in science (Rifkin, Longnecker, Leach, & Davis, 2010; Rifkin,
Longnecker, Leach, Davis, & Orthia, 2010). Indeed, many programmes in
science communication (e.g. the science communication programme at the University
of Western Australia (N. Longnecker, personal communication)) are sustained by
such “service” roles.
    The numbers of students engaging in full-time study of science communication
tend to be low (e.g. the Centre for Science Communication at the University of
Otago is amongst the largest, if not the largest, postgraduate programmes for
science communication in the world, with 60 or more students at any one time).
Engaging in outreach and teaching communication skills to large classes of under-
graduate science students may, oftentimes, provide the respective justification and
“bread-and-butter” income to sustain university programmes in science
communication.
    Certainly, the niche nature of most university programmes in science commu-
nication makes them vulnerable without such outreach and service activities.
Taking Australia as an example, the science communication programmes at the
University of Sydney, University of New South Wales and University of Rock-
hampton have all been closed in the last few years. And while the University of
Otago is the only university within New Zealand to have a dedicated science
communication programme, the University of Auckland disestablished its only
lectureship in science communication in 2011. This is a pattern that appears to be
occurring elsewhere in the world and is not confined to just Australasia. For
example, Imperial College of London, formerly the world’s largest tertiary post-
graduate facility for science communication and one of the pioneers in the field, has
closed one of the three streams of its science communication programme (creative
non-fiction writing).
    While science communication was seen as trendy and relevant around
the turn of the twenty-first century, with a plethora of programmes spring-
ing up around the world (Mulder, Longnecker, & Davis, 2008; Schiele,
Claessens, & Shi, 2012), often in second- and third-tier universities, this
“polytech” approach to the discipline, whereby the emphasis has been upon
skills-based training rather than research-informed teaching, has not always
been readily sustained. Ironically, it is in countries in the developing
world, especially Asia and South America, where university-based science
communication programmes seem to be at their healthiest and vibrant best
(Davis, 2010a; Schiele et al., 2012).
166                                                                        L.S. Davis


Science Outreach Activities by Universities

Typically universities are involved in promoting science, especially that conducted
within their own institutions. This can take many forms. Here, I will examine each
in terms of their cost, reach and persistence.




Advertisements

Often this takes the form of advertising aimed at securing applications
from prospective students, but, in its broadest sense, it may be considered a
form of outreach as such advertisements can be designed to enhance the reputa-
tion of the institution for its prowess at science and may, to a limited extent,
convey something about the science being conducted within the university’s
walls.
    Leaflets, brochures and rack cards are common forms of such promotions.
Typically these are available freely and distributed locally: usually within the
university and/or the local catchment area for students. Such leaflets at the
University of Otago follow a standard format, always incorporating a 275-word
profile and photograph of a student or staff member in the area of science that is
the focus of the leaflet. COSTS are LOW, REACH is LOW but PERSISTENCE
is reasonably HIGH as such leaflets remain available year-round and year after
year. However, promoting science is not really the priority of such leaflets: they
are more about raising the profile of a particular science rather than
communicating it.
    Newspaper and magazine advertisements are even more limited in the infor-
mation they can convey. At best they may depict an image of someone in a
laboratory or conducting research in the wild. COSTS are LOW to MEDIUM
(charges for a 3-column advertisement in the Otago Daily Times are in the vicinity
of $500, whereas a half-page advertisement in a national magazine in
New Zealand will be in the vicinity of $3,000 or more), and while REACH is
potentially MEDIUM to HIGH (readership of most of the major magazines in
New Zealand number in the tens of thousands or higher according to the
New Zealand Audit Bureau of Circulation), given that PERSISTENCE is typi-
cally LOW (newspapers and some magazines) to MEDIUM (for some magazines
such as North & South) and the amount of science that can be communicated is
extremely limited, such advertisements are unlikely to be useful for outreach
per se.
    On the other hand, television advertisements have more potential for promoting
actual science carried out at universities. While most of the television campaigns of
universities in New Zealand, including the University of Otago, focus on the
opportunity to develop one’s potential that enrolment in that university brings,
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication         167


Massey University’s 2012 television advertisement1 notably features the scientific
achievements of their staff and students. While REACH of television campaigns is
HIGH (in fact, very high), COSTS of producing television advertisements and
paying for their placements are correspondingly HIGH and their PERSISTENCE
is LOW. Although persistence of such advertisements can be theoretically extended
through their placement on online video-sharing sites such as YouTube and the like,
the numbers of views tend to be very low and are relatively insignificant compared
to the reach obtainable when broadcast. For example, the University of Otago TV
commercial showcasing the university’s research vessel Polaris II was uploaded to
YouTube on 28 June 2011, and, just over a year later on 8 July 2012, there had been
only 358 views.2 Again, the main aim of such advertisements is to attract students,
not outreach per se, but this is an area that has much more potential than is currently
being exploited.
    Short stories about science packaged into the time slot of commercials
(“SciCommercials”) are being advocated as a means to promote science, inform
the public and change people’s attitudes about critical issues involving science
(Finkler, 2014). Such commercials could be modelled on the approach of American
restaurant chain Chipotle, for example, which uploaded a 2-min 20-s advertisement
to YouTube on 25 August 2011 called “Back to the Start” featuring an animated
story with an environmental message accompanied by a Willie Nelson rendition of
the Coldplay song “The Scientist”. As of 8 July 2012, it had been viewed 6,621,046
times. It is also said to have “stolen the show” when it was broadcast as part of the
advertisements accompanying the 2011 Grammy Awards watched by 39 million
viewers (Summers, 2012). While the animation, Willie Nelson and the rights to
Coldplay’s song undoubtedly were expensive, the point is that the use of (1) visual
storytelling techniques more usually associated with documentary and (2) a strong
soundtrack created outstanding brand awareness as well as empathy with the
environmental message. Were such an approach to be employed by universities,
whereby they created short science stories within the time span devoted to
advertisements and packaged them with evocative soundtracks, it could potentially
see universities turn advertising for students into an effective form of branding and
outreach too.



Public Lectures

Departmental seminars and lectures open to the public (and typically free) are the
standard university fare when it comes to promoting subjects such as science. They
often utilise the staff on hand or visiting researchers and typically use the
university’s own lecture facilities. Hence, COSTS tend to be LOW (although they


1
    http://www.youtube.com/watch?v¼X7cqYAWVQGs
2
    http://www.youtube.com/watch?v¼2WvIUr2KzUA
168                                                                             L.S. Davis


can extend into the medium and even high brackets if outside speakers have
expensive associated travel costs or speakers’ fees). But even for the most
sought-after speakers, REACH also tends to be LOW (at the University of Otago
in the last 12 months, only the primatologist Dr. Jane Goodall and climate change
specialist Dr. James Hansen have attracted audiences in excess of 1,000). PERSIS-
TENCE is, of course, LOW.
    Admittedly, the recent practice of videoing public talks and putting them online
can arguably increase both reach and persistence. While there is little doubt that is
true for persistence, there is resistance amongst the public to download and watch
presentations that conform to the typical 1-hour duration of public talks at univer-
sities. The longer an online video is, the less likely those starting to watch it will do
so in its entirety, with videos of 1 hour or more being watched on average for little
more than 20 % of their length, while only about 1 in 10 viewers will watch the
entire video3 (see also web-based promotions below).
    There is also an argument that can be made, in terms of the penetration of
outreach activities, that such public talks are preaching to the converted and that the
audience is made up of those already interested in science and is oftentimes
dominated by staff and students from within the university. The academic nature
of such talks and their usual fairly formal format do not work so well for the
uninitiated. In other words, the worth of public university talks are rarely
questioned despite their relatively low reach and penetration, given that their
costs are low and there is an underlying assumption that they are a vital and integral
part of the way university scientists disseminate their science to other academics
and interested members of the public. As a means of science outreach, however,
they probably offer better “value” than they do real effectiveness, with very limited
penetration into the public domain at large.



Café Scientifiques

Perhaps as an antidote to the formality of the university’s public talks, the concept
of café scientifiques arose in 1998, whereby science is taken out of the universities
and into public meeting places that are more convivial and more congenial to
exchanges of ideas (Dallas, 2006). Café scientifiques may well occur in cafes, but
they are also held in pubs or other intimate surroundings. Their mantra is that they
are limited in size and the audience is encouraged to be involved with the speakers.
The University of Waikato in Hamilton, New Zealand, specialises in café
scientifiques, holding them at monthly intervals, whereas the University of Otago
holds them typically during Dunedin’s biennial International Science Fair, but they
are not otherwise a normal part of the calendar year. While COSTS are LOW,
REACH is LOW and PERSISTENCE is LOW, making them an enjoyable and


3
    http://wistia.com/blog/does-length-matter-it-does-for-video-2k12-edition/
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication                      169


inexpensive means of outreach is the point rather than an especially effective means
of outreach if scale and penetration are the paramount concerns. The participation
by the audience is definitely a positive and seen by some as important for public
engagement in science as opposed to simply public awareness of science (Dallas).



Talks in Schools

There is little doubt that the most effective time in someone’s life to instil a positive
attitude to science is when they are young (Wulf, Mayhew, & Finkelstein, 2010).
For this reason, if no other, it is vital that universities take their science to schools.
There is, of course, the added motivating factor of the recruitment of potential
students down the track, but many universities, the University of Otago included,
supposedly have their staff give talks in schools simply as a means of raising
awareness of science and turning students onto science (Zega, 2012). The impor-
tance of doing so is underscored by significant reductions in the proportion of
secondary school students in New Zealand opting to take science subjects (biology,
chemistry, physics and mathematics) over the 10-year period from 2001 to 2011
(Gluckman, 2011; Hill, 2012). The percentages of students taking these subjects fell
to below 20 % of students for all science subjects with the exception of biology, and
even biology is predicted to fall below 20 % over the coming decade if current
trends continue.
    In recent times, the most notable example of university-led science outreach into
schools in New Zealand has been the involvement of four institutions dealing with
science – the University of Otago, the Royal Society of New Zealand, the Mac-
Diarmid Institute and the Allan Wilson Centre – contributing to the awareness of
science at a tiny school in rural New Zealand: Tolaga Bay Area School. This
culminated with a forum and celebrations to mark the transit of Venus in 2012
(Winter, 2012). Despite PERSISTENCE being HIGH in the Tologa Bay venture
(the association of the science organisations with the school and its community has
been ongoing for 8 years), it has been very HIGH in terms of COSTS, and REACH
has been LOW. As admirable and as successful as such a programme has been for
all those involved, the reality is that such a level of investment does not scale
nationwide. Hence, while it may be an effective form of outreach for those lucky
enough to be the recipients of such attention, it cannot be said to offer good “value”.
    For the most part though, having university researchers and lecturers give talks
in schools is LOW COST and, while REACH is LOW and PERSISTENCE is LOW
(in that these are typically one-off exposures of the students to science), the lasting
effects can potentially be substantial.4 Given the low cost typically associated with


4
 Speaking from personal experience, a talk by heart surgeon and scientist Dr Christian Barnard
when I was a school student had a profound effect on me, to the point where I still recite his words
and use them to guide my own life.
170                                                                         L.S. Davis


outreach activities to schools and the critical time in the development of the
students’ attitudes to science, it seems imperative that universities make this a
priority of outreach, even if the reach is low.



Talks to the Elderly

At the other end of the age spectrum, there are the elderly. Often these are time-rich
people with a thirst for knowledge, as is evidenced by the popularity of programmes
such as the University of the Third Age (U3A) (Huang, 2006), in which university
staff are encouraged to participate as a form of outreach. COSTS are LOW,
REACH is LOW and PERSISTENCE is LOW, but their undeniable success
(Swindell, Vassella, Morgan, & Sayer, 2011) makes them worthwhile forms of
outreach. If science communication is partly about having an informed public that
can direct public policy through their votes, then this is one segment of society that
should not be forgotten.
   To give one local example, the small Central Otago community of Wanaka has a
resident population of only 7,000, yet so popular has been their U3A programme of
speakers (more than half of which during 2012 involved University of Otago staff
associated with sciences) that they have had to introduce a reservation scheme and
restrict entry because the numbers showing up for the talks exceed the 140-seat
capacity of the venue (J. Darby, personal communication).
   One of the characteristics of programmes of talks like those of U3A is that the
regularity and predictable nature of the activity develops a following. This “sub-
scription” characteristic is one way outreach activities can be enhanced: while the
individual components of the activity may have low persistence, the subscription
nature of the activity enhances persistence and creates engagement in science that
goes beyond simple awareness.
   This subscription characteristic can be applied to programmes of talks given
anywhere (e.g. public talks at universities) if there is a regularity to the talks and
they are packaged together. For example, departmental seminars can assume this
characteristic, as can the likes of highlighted lecture series, such as the Distin-
guished Communicator Lecture Series run by the Centre for Science Communica-
tion at the University of Otago.



Press Releases

Press releases are potentially a form of outreach or a means to outreach, with the
aim being to generate interest in the news media about science being conducted at
the university. This is an important part of any university’s marketing and commu-
nications arms. There is a definite skill to writing press releases (Brechman, Lee, &
Cappella, 2011; Riesch & Spiegelhalter, 2011), and this will influence the attention
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication        171


of the media. There are also strategic reasons for when and how to release them for
maximum effect. COSTS are very LOW, REACH is LOW and PERSISTENCE is
very LOW, but they hold the promise that they can lead to other forms of outreach
(television, radio and newspaper interviews) where the reach will potentially be
huge for little cost.



Appearances by Staff and Students in the News Media

Oftentimes the result of a press release initially, sometimes the result of being
sought out due to the nature of a news story requiring expert comment, whatever the
initial reason for their involvement, university staff (and, to a lesser extent, post-
graduate research students) are often called upon to be interviewed by radio,
television and newspapers about science stories. This has been aided in
New Zealand by the establishment in 2008 of the Science Media Centre, which
acts as a conduit between the news media organisations and the scientists. The
New Zealand Science Media Centre was modelled upon similar centres in the UK
and Australia.5
   COSTS of these types of outreach are LOW, and while PERSISTENCE is LOW
for most news media, REACH is HIGH, making this good value relative to other
forms of outreach.
   Some commentators are better than others and become media stars in their own
right, with the media tending to favour them or seek them out for comment.
Articulate and informed commentators on science, such as the late Sir Paul Calla-
ghan (Callaghan & Hill, 2007), can have a reach and effectiveness that goes well
beyond the persistence of the news item. Amongst the most notable university-
based science communicators in recent times are Professors Carl Sagan, Stephen
J. Gould, Richard Dawkins and Stephen Hawking.



Books and Documentaries

All four of the above made their mark through writing books that were extremely
popular and, especially with Sagan, their involvement with television
documentaries.
   This is arguably one of the most effective means by which universities can
potentially undertake outreach. COSTS are LOW (at least for the author if not the
publisher), REACH is typically MEDIUM to HIGH and PERSISTENCE is HIGH.
Yet this sort of communication, as vital as it is, happens almost in spite of the
universities rather than being encouraged by them. The trend in the last decade or so


5
    http://www.sciencemediacentre.co.nz/about/
172                                                                         L.S. Davis


in New Zealand (which followed those in the UK, USA and Australia) has been to
measure performance of university-based scientists almost entirely by their outputs
and citations in refereed journal articles (Davis, 2007). Virtually no credit is given
to academics that publish popular dissertations of their science.
   Similarly, participation by staff in documentaries about science is also viewed as
an extraneous activity that gets little formal recognition in the university system,
even though the potential reach of such activities is on such an extremely high scale
that it is nonsensical not to encourage it if reach and penetration are part of the
criteria for measuring the effectiveness of outreach.
   Professors Richard Dawkins (UK), Tim Flannery (Australia) and Lawrence
Krauss (USA) are in the enviable positions of being largely encouraged by their
universities to publicly communicate science through books and documentaries.
But they are the exceptions, not the rule, and in all three cases it has been their
individual persistence, not to mention their prowess at storytelling and communi-
cation (e.g. Dawkins, 2006; Flannery, 2007; Krauss, 2012), which has seen them
accorded such status.
   Universities would do well to recognise the benefits that come from such a form
of outreach and encourage it rather than actively discourage it as they do by
rewarding alternative outputs such as publication in refereed scientific journals.



Web-Based Activities

There are several ways that universities may exploit online publishing for the
purposes of outreach.
    The first and most obvious is through their website. Oftentimes these feature
research by scientists. For example, the front page of the University of Otago
website6 on 8 July 2012 featured five stories and profiles, all of which were about
either science or the health sciences. The likelihood is, of course, that these will
mainly be seen by those already seeking information about the university, including
its sciences. COSTS of university websites are MEDIUM to HIGH, but as the costs
for maintaining its website need to be borne by a university anyway, the COSTS for
the actual outreach component are likely to be LOW, PERSISTENCE is MEDIUM
to HIGH and REACH is MEDIUM to HIGH.
    In other words, while it is not targeted, this is one form of science communica-
tion and outreach that universities can implement easily and should not ignore:
universities benefit from increasing public awareness of the science being carried
out within the institution, even if that form of promotion is not the initial reason
responsible for bringing people to the university’s site. At the Centre for Science
Communication, we post online profiles of all student research, past and present.7


6
    http://www.otago.ac.nz
7
    http://www.sciencecommunication.info
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication            173


While this in itself is unlikely to bring much traffic to the site (although it is indexed
and discoverable by search engines), anecdotal evidence suggests that it is popular
with visitors to the site – including prospective students – in making them aware of
the types of research being carried out at the centre.
    Another way, especially with the advent of iTunes U (Lu & Zhang, 2011), is to
have material online for the public to download. This can take the form of podcasts
or vodcasts (i.e. video podcasts) of lectures, public talks or outputs produced by
staff or students such as films and can even include documents for download.
    While some universities (e.g. MIT) have been quick to embrace the notion of
universal access to course material or open educational resources (OER) as it is
otherwise known, issues remain (Hassler & Jackson, 2010). When OER is done to
the sophisticated and extensive levels as the likes of MIT and the Southern Institute
of Technology (SIT)8 in New Zealand, COSTS are HIGH, reach is potentially
HIGH and persistence is HIGH. SIT joined iTunes U in 2008 and recorded its two
millionth download in 2010. As a form of outreach when done on this scale, open
educational resources through iTunes U is a worthwhile option, particularly if the
costs can be justified for other reasons, such as attracting more students.
    Many universities and their staff, however, are reluctant to place their intellec-
tual property and content online for free, instead using iTunes U or other online
repositories (e.g. YouTube, Vimeo) mainly for one-off lectures, videoed public
talks and the like. It is debatable how effective this form of outreach is, as research
shows people are reluctant to watch videos of one hour duration (i.e. the length of a
typical lecture) and of those that start watching videos of such length, the vast
majority abandon the video well before its end. The University of Otago was the
first New Zealand university to join iTunes U, doing so in 2008. Documentary films
produced by students of the Centre for Science Communication occupied 9 of the
top 25 items downloaded as at the end of that year. The popularity of those films
illustrates three qualities for online videos to be successful for outreach: (1) they are
not too long (24 min in the case of the student films), (2) they have high production
values (too often university-produced videos with poor footage and editing are put
on iTunes U and other online aggregators) and (3) they use storytelling to convey
the information (Davis, 2010a).
    Hence, while COSTS for individual web-based items of outreach can be LOW
and PERSISTENCE is HIGH, REACH will be uncertain and is likely to be LOW
unless universities make quality rather than quantity their aim, in which case reach
can potentially be MEDIUM to HIGH (Rifkin, Longnecker, Leach, & Davis, 2012).
    One way to address the issue of viewer/user fatigue is to create smaller bite-sized
offerings online. TED talks,9 with their 18-min maximum, have shown the way
with this. The Centre for Science Communication opted to apply this model to short
talks about science that are screened before a live audience and then put online.10


8
 http://itunesu.sit.ac.nz
9
 http://ted.com
10
   http://www.scitell.com
174                                                                           L.S. Davis


This has a two-fold basis for outreach: there is the immediate outreach opportunity
as such talks are presented to the live audience, and then the reach is extended and
persistence guaranteed by the online presence. COSTS are MEDIUM (mainly
associated with filming and editing), REACH is MEDIUM to HIGH and PERSIS-
TENCE is HIGH.
   Blogging is perhaps the easiest way that universities can promote science online.
For example, the Australian National University’s Centre for Public Awareness of
Science (CPAS) has a blog about science.11 However, many universities, including
the University of Otago, have been slow to embrace blogging because of security
concerns, with worries about potentially providing access to their servers for
malicious commenting, spam, hackers and viruses. The Centre for Science Com-
munication gets around this by hosting our blog on an off-site server12 – but such
practices create issues when it comes to integrating with university-based websites.
   Unpublished research carried out by students at the Centre for Science Commu-
nication showed that by far the most telling component with respect to driving
traffic to a blog was the frequency of posts. High-quality photographs and tying a
blog post in with other forms of outreach (e.g. press releases, public talks) also
increased traffic.
   Blogging is LOW COST, with typically a LOW to MEDIUM reach (albeit
potentially high with an extraordinarily popular blog) and HIGH PERSISTENCE
(Davis, 2007). It is something universities should definitely encourage for science
outreach, albeit there is an unstated cost of time since, to be effective, blogs need to
be updated regularly. Blogging is another of the forms of outreach that benefits
from having a “subscription” element to it, with good blogs developing a loyal
following of readers who come back to them regularly.
   Consideration should also be given to having university staff participate in
community-based blogs about science, such as SciBlogs,13 which is run by the
Science Media Centre and is the largest blogging network of scientists in
New Zealand.



Displays, Museums and Aquaria

There has been a long tradition of universities providing displays that exemplify the
research, including science, done within their walls. These can be formal and
persistent publicly accessible exhibits such as museums and aquaria or short-lived
transitory displays used for a specific purpose and then often discarded. The former
are really expensive to maintain with very HIGH COSTS, although REACH is
HIGH and PERSISTENCE is HIGH too. It is likely that costs for such entities


11
   http://diffusion.weblogs.anu.edu.au
12
   http://centreforsciencecommunication.com
13
   http://sciblogs.co.nz
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication          175


within universities would need to be justified for reasons other than just outreach.
At the University of Otago, the Department of Zoology closed its publicly acces-
sible museum more than a decade ago, whereas a public aquarium is still
maintained as part of the Marine Studies Centre. (albeit the aquarium is temporarily
closed while a new building to house it is being sought).
   Temporary displays about science are likely to be LOW to MEDIUM in COST,
have LOW to MEDIUM reach and have LOW to MEDIUM persistence. The nature
of such displays and their uses is so variable that their overall effectiveness in
science outreach by universities is hard to quantify.




Bringing People into the University to Experience Science

One effective way to “reach out” has proved to be, ironically, to “pull people in”.
Universities practise outreach for science by bringing people into the university for
hands-on experiences of science. This can take the form of open days, whereby the
public are invited to attend, in which case it is likely to involve the use of temporary
displays as outlined above. In-depth experiences of science, by their very nature,
typically dictate working with small groups. The University of Otago does this with
its Hands-on Science, a week-long residential programme for secondary school
students from around New Zealand (Zega, 2012). For the most part these are used as
marketing tools by the universities, whereby they identify and target potential
students and bring them to the university to experience how science is taught and
conducted there. While the motive may be to attract students, one consequence is an
enhancement of the participants’ appreciation of science, whether or not they return
to the university as fee-paying students. Given that COSTS are HIGH, REACH is
LOW and PERSISTENCE is LOW, this type of science outreach is probably
largely practised by universities because of the recruitment element to it.
    A variation on this type of university-funded experiential exposure to science is
the outside-funded event. For example, the University of Otago’s Marine Studies
Centre runs residential events for outstanding school children from around the
South Island of New Zealand under its government-funded Gifted and Talented
Programme (Zega, 2012). These are less aimed at showing off the university’s
sciences than they are at extending the students’ knowledge and appreciation of
science. COSTS are HIGH, REACH is LOW to MEDIUM and PERSISTENCE is
LOW. Programmes of this type exist largely through government assistance
targeting children with particular needs or potential. In essence, they function like
any other university fee-paying course, with costs expected to be covered largely by
the subsidy: otherwise the costs would be hard for universities to justify based on
outreach alone.
176                                                                                 L.S. Davis


Science Competitions

The University of Otago, as with other institutions elsewhere, has been involved in
stimulating interest in science by running various competitions. For example, the
Department of Mathematics initiated a nationwide Junior Mathematics Competi-
tion in the 1980s that still attracts over 10,000 entries each year from more than
250 schools (Zega, 2012). Competitions generally have a MEDIUM to HIGH
COST associated with running them, their PERSISTENCE is LOW and REACH
can vary from LOW to HIGH.
   Often competitions will take time to develop an audience of participants. But,
again, their repetitive nature lends them a tradition that brings with it all the
advantages of “subscription” in favouring reach and persistence.
   The University of Otago’s Centre for Science Communication helped facilitate a
science filmmaking competition for secondary school students around New Zealand
on behalf of the Royal Society of New Zealand.14 Concurrent research showed this
particular competition increased the likelihood that students will carry on to careers
in science as well as enhancing their abilities to communicate science (Davis and
Dawes, unpublished data). However, even though PERSISTENCE was extended to
MEDIUM to HIGH by posting the films online (which had the benefit of providing
outreach to an audience beyond just those involved in the competition), reach was
LOW, and, given the COSTS for this particular competition were very HIGH, it
was subsequently cancelled by the RSNZ as not being a cost-effective means of
outreach.
   The unpredictable and variable nature of the reach afforded by competitions
makes them a somewhat risky form of science outreach for universities. If they are
successful they can really help raise awareness about science, but if they are not, as
with the RSNZ example, the costs probably do not justify the outcomes in terms of
outreach.



Festivals

University-run science festivals, while expensive to run, can be excellent ways of
reaching out to the public: a joining of the “town and gown”.
   At one end of the scale, the Centre for Science Communication at the University
of Otago holds a premiere of its student films each year in the city’s Regent Theatre,
where it attracts audiences of 1,100–1,800. COSTS are MEDIUM, matching the
MEDIUM REACH, and even though PERSISTENCE is LOW, it has developed a
“tradition” and become a well-known and anticipated event on the city’s calendar.
This is yet another case where the regularity of outreach activities is an important

14
   http://www.royalsociety.org.nz/2006/05/04/six-big-science-adventures-assigned-to-nz-
students/
Outreach Activities by Universities as a Channel for Science Communication          177


component for developing a following that effectively creates an extended form of
reach and persistence.
   At the other end of the scale, the Centre puts on the ScienceTeller Festival15
aimed at showcasing the best in storytelling about science. The inaugural
ScienceTeller Festival was held over 5 days in November 2011 and featured
46 events, 27 speakers, 18 films, 5 exhibitions, 4 workshops and 4 performance-
based shows. COSTS were very HIGH, REACH MEDIUM and PERSISTENCE
LOW. Such large-scale forms of outreach are probably only justifiable if they are
held regularly and develop a following that raises awareness of the science beyond
just a single event, and over time REACH might be expected to attain HIGH levels.



Conferences

While university-run conferences are often of an academic nature, they can be open
to the public and, sometimes, a sizeable proportion of the registrants can come from
the public. This is especially so when the conference or meeting has a well-
developed theme around topics that are controversial in the public realm
(e.g. evolution and Darwinism, climate change).
    For example, the University of Otago symposium to mark the bicentenary of the
birth of Charles Darwin (Davis, 2010b) attracted approximately 80 members of the
public, as did one on climate change and the news media. However, as a form of
outreach, it is probably not that effective with REACH being LOW, PERSIS-
TENCE being LOW and COSTS being MEDIUM.



Short Courses

Universities can also reach out to nonuniversity students by offering short courses
in science to the public that are not intended for degree qualifications. The trick here
from the university’s perspective is to balance the goodwill generated by such
courses against the costs that such courses entail.
    In most instances, courses like this have a charge attached and they are less
outreach than they are just another form of educational programme. The Centre for
Science Communication has undertaken several such courses with a science focus,
but with MEDIUM COSTS, very LOW REACH and LOW PERSISTENCE, they
would seem hard to justify as a good value form of outreach. That being said, the
in-depth nature of such courses probably means that they have more effect than
most activities on the participants (i.e. they are more effective at raising levels of



15
     http://www.scienceteller.com
178                                                                          L.S. Davis


understanding about science as opposed to just awareness), although the line
between university course and outreach becomes blurred.



Indigenous Peoples

In some societies, indigenous peoples are often under-represented in science at
universities. This is true of New Zealand, where most if not all universities are
attempting to address this by developing specific programmes targeted at indige-
nous peoples, including science outreach activities.
   The Science Wananga programme of the University of Otago was started in
2008 and attempts to engage Maori secondary school students in science by running
science camps in five areas throughout New Zealand. These camps are described as
changing the students’ attitudes to science in many positive ways (Zega, 2012).
Although COSTS are very HIGH and REACH is LOW with LOW PERSISTENCE,
the value of increasing science literacy in underprivileged groups cannot be under-
stated. This is an example where the penetration of the outreach activity – taking
science into areas of society that are otherwise difficult to reach – is arguably more
important than its reach or audience size per se, overriding a strict cost-benefit
analysis.



Conclusion and Recommendations

Activities that universities traditionally favour as outreach for science (e.g. talks,
bringing people into the university to experience science, displays, etc.) are not the
most effective forms of outreach in terms of the reach and “value” they provide
(Table 1). Universities would do well to find ways to encourage their staff to
produce popular books and films and to blog. When it comes to creating awareness
of a university’s science, the use of “sci-commercials” for television remains an
unexploited potential option. To be most effective, big costly events such as
festivals, and less costly activities like talks to schools and other community groups,
should have a regularity to them. Regularity of outreach initiatives is one of the key
features affecting their effectiveness. One-off events have limited ongoing appeal
and, apart from profile building for specific purposes, are unlikely to bring “bang for
buck”. Certainly, the online marketplace is where best value outreach for science
can be exacted by universities (Table 1).

Recommendations

Some universities may well have the necessary funds to engage in science outreach
activities irrespective of the benefits that accrue relative to their costs. Where that is
not the case and where communication of science to the public is the top priority
(as opposed to recruitment or some form of affirmative action for indigenous
peoples), it is recommended that universities prioritise their outreach in the follow-
ing way to maximise the value derived:
180                                                                                  L.S. Davis


1. Develop the profile of science on their websites.
2. Encourage staff (and, where appropriate, students) to engage in interviews with
   the media (television, radio and newspapers) about science. This can be facili-
   tated through judicious use of press releases.
3. Encourage staff to write books and articles about science for the popular press
   and, where possible, be involved in documentaries about science. This requires
   finding some ways to give recognition to time spent popularising science as
   opposed to just rewarding staff for publications and citations in scientific
   journals.
4. Encourage staff to write blogs about science or participate in group blogs or
   online forums about science. Again, there needs to be some form of allowance
   and recognition for the time involved.
5. Where engaging in small-scale localised outreach activities (such as public talks,
   café scientifiques and U3A), maximise the benefits by having them regularly or
   packaging them together in a form to which the public can “subscribe”.
6. Facilitate staff (and, where appropriate, students) giving talks about science in
   schools. While the underlying motivation for universities of providing such talks
   is often associated with recruitment, the evidence suggests that this is a partic-
   ularly crucial time to turn children on to science irrespective of any future
   recruitment that may be derived.

Role of Learned Societies in Communicating
Science

Leo Tan Wee Hin and Ramanathan Subramaniam




Abstract Examination of the scientific society movement in the developed world
shows the predominance of three key constructs that account for their effectiveness
and dynamism – civic activism by the scientific community, pursuit of scholarship
through science communication, and entrepreneurship. Some examples of these
learned societies in different regions of the world are explored with a view towards
identifying some best practices. It is shown that leveraging on the brick-and-mortar
model for the establishing of scientific academies and scientific societies is not a
particularly prudent option for countries in the developing world. The “invisible
college” model represents a more effective model for the setting up of such learned
societies in the shortest possible time. It is argued that the institutional framework
provided by scientific academies and scientific societies affords immense scope for
realizing the multidimensional facets of science communication in tandem with
other efforts.




L. Tan Wee Hin (*)
Singapore National Academy of Science, Singapore, Singapore
National University of Singapore, Singapore, Singapore
e-mail: leotan@nus.edu.sg
R. Subramaniam (*)
Singapore National Academy of Science, Singapore, Singapore
Nanyang Technological University, Singapore, Singapore
e-mail: subramaniam.r@nie.edu.sg

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,    183
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_11, © Springer Netherlands 2014
184                                                 L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


Introduction

In today’s globalized world, there is practically no facet of society that has not been
influenced by science and technology, either directly or indirectly. For example, the
chemical formulation of paint used to coat the walls of our house, the electricity
which comes to our house via power lines after fuel has been combusted in power
stations, and personal accessories such as mobile phones and digital watches are
testament to the extent to which science and technology (or the products thereof)
have become embedded in our lifestyles. They have also been harnessed for the
cause of economic development – the economies of the Western world and several
countries in East Asia are very much driven by science and technology.
    Many countries in the developing world have, however, not been able to
capitalize on the full potential that science and technology offer for socioeconomic
development and thus promote growth in their societies. A host of reasons can be
advanced for this – for example, low literacy levels, a culture that does not embrace
science readily, corruption, poverty, and political instability. Further compounding
this state of affairs is the apathy of the scientific community in the country to
vigorously promote the cause of science.
    The promotion of science through subject teaching in schools is the baseline
from which science literacy can be developed in society. Beyond the schooling
years, if students do not enter any of the institutes of higher learning to do science,
their contact with science via institutional dissemination efforts may be restricted to
science centers and science-based learned societies.
    The objectives of this chapter are threefold:
(a) To briefly review the learned societies movement in science
(b) To identify some best practices in their operation and management
(c) To make some recommendations for improving the effectiveness of existing
    learned societies in the developing world as well as the formation of new
    learned societies, all of which have implications for communicating science
    to the masses



Science-Based Learned Societies

Civic society activism has a long history. Such activism arises owing to some
perceived shortcomings in society, either due to the absence of players who can
address these areas or the neglect or inactivity of the government in these areas. A
group of individuals coming together for a common cause can weld themselves into
an affiliation which can wield tremendous clout. In science, these affiliations
commonly include scientific academies, associations for the promotion of science,
and scientific societies; the latter is often discipline based. Many such bodies have
become powerful organizations in their own right and are a force to be reckoned
with by the state.
Role of Learned Societies in Communicating Science                                 185


   The roots of science-based learned societies can be traced to the activities and
efforts of the Royal Society, which was set up in the UK in 1660. However, its
origins can be traced back to even earlier, when a group of natural philosophers
“began meeting in the mid-1640s to discuss the new philosophy of promoting
knowledge of the natural world through observation and experiment, which we
now call science” (https://royalsociety.org/). The success of the Royal Society
emboldened the formation of similar learned societies in other parts of Europe as
well as later in the USA. The efforts of these societies have been the subject of great
interest in the scholarly literature (McClellan, 1985). Over the years, many of these
societies went on from strength to strength, mainly due to more scientists joining
the movement and wanting to make a difference to society as well as the urge to
enhance scholarly pursuits.



Selected Explorations of Learned Societies in Science

A study of the operations and management of science-based learned societies can
provide useful ideas for culling best practices for emulation by others. As it is not
within the scope of this chapter to do an exhaustive analysis, we focus on learned
societies in four regions of the world, Europe, USA, Asia, and Africa, and see what
experiences can be identified for consideration. It has to be borne in mind that the
cause of science communication can be aided greatly with a vibrant institutional
culture.



Europe

While there is some evidence that the roots of scientific societies can be traced to
Plato’s Akademia in Athens in 385 BC as well as the Accademia Segreta in Naples
in the 1540s (Eamon & Paheau, 1984), it is the formation of the Royal Society in the
UK in 1660 (Hall, 1991; Hunter, 1989) and its success that encouraged the
evolution of other learned societies in the rest of Europe (McClellan, 1985;
Ornstein, 1975; Roberts, 1991). Table 1 shows some of the learned societies that
were formed in Europe.
    These academies possess multidisciplinary expertise among its members. Con-
siderable emphasis is placed on the promotion of scholarship in the sciences, not
only through regular meetings between scientists and lecture demonstrations by
scientists for the public but also through the publication of journals for the dissem-
ination of knowledge. Many academies also function as think tanks for scientific
issues of interest, and their advice is often sought by governments.
    The spawning of a multiplicity of learned societies catering to diverse aspects of
the sciences is also a characteristic feature. For example, Table 2 shows a selection
of learned societies in the UK just for the sciences.

   The proliferation of such societies in the UK should not be interpreted as a
consequence of disenchantment with existing learned societies but as a logical
sequel arising from the emergence of new disciplines as well as the inclination of
some members to pursue new directions not catered for in the original mission
objectives of the learned societies. For example, the evolution of journals beyond
the natural sciences (physics, chemistry, and biology) is a case in point – owing to
the creation of new knowledge at the interfaces of these disciplines, and between
these and other disciplines, new journals would be better positioned to spur growth
in knowledge in these areas. These societies have been active in the wider cause of
communicating science to their audiences.
Role of Learned Societies in Communicating Science                                     187

USA

Table 3 shows the evolution of learned societies in science in the USA. Compared
to the UK, scientific academies and scientific societies were formed in the USA
only much later.
   Of particular interest is the American Chemical Society, which is the world’s
largest scientific society with about 163,000 members (http://www.acs.org). The
emphasis that the Society places on scholarship can be gauged from the fact that it
publishes 49 journals catering to a wide variety of interests in chemistry, and these
have evolved into respectable international journals. It also took the lead in
conceptualizing the abstracting and indexing of the global chemical literature
through the establishing of the Chemical Abstracts Service in 1907 so as to better
facilitate the process of research as well as finding of chemical information. To
promote better interaction within the chemical community, it organizes numerous
meetings, including conferences, local gatherings, and exhibitions annually. All
these have established the Society as a well-run institution that has an active public
education outreach. The practice of publishing scholarly journals for science
communication is widely prevalent among science-based learned societies in the
USA – in fact, Hampson (2012) cites the publishing of journals as one of the four
key facets of contemporary culture of science communication in the USA.



Asia

In Asia, the development of science-based learned societies has only a relatively
short history (Table 4). It started in India, with the formation of three academies in a
span of 5 years in the 1930s. China and Japan followed suit later in 1949. More
recently, the Malaysian Academy of Sciences was formed.
   We focus on Singapore for the Asian context. The Singapore National Academy
of Science and its nine constituent societies – Singapore National Institute of
Chemistry, Institute of Physics Singapore, Singapore Institute of Biology, Singa-
pore Mathematical Society, Singapore Institute of Statistics, Singapore Society for
Biochemistry and Molecular Biology, Singapore Society for Microbiology and
Biotechnology, Singapore Association for the Advancement of Science, and
Science Teachers’ Association of Singapore – are involved in the promotion of
188                                                   L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


Table 4 Formation of some learned societies in Asia
Year established          Name of learned society                              Country
1930                      The National Academy of Sciences, India              India
1934                      Indian Academy of Sciences                           India
1935                      Indian National Science Academy                      India
1949                      Chinese Academy of Sciences                          China
1949                      Science Council of Japan                             Japan
1953                      Pakistan Academy of Sciences                         Pakistan
1954                      Australian Academy of Science                        Australia
1973                      Bangladesh Academy of Sciences                       Bangladesh
1976                      Singapore National Academy of Science                Singapore
1976                      Sri Lanka Academy of Science                         China
1976                      National Academy of Science and Technology           Philippines
1982                      Nepal Academy of Science and Technology              Nepal
1990                      Indonesian Academy of Sciences                       Indonesia
1995                      Akademi Sains Malaysia                               Malaysia


science and technology. The presence of this diversity of societies to cater toward
the cause of science in the tiny island state is an indication that a plurality of learned
societies is desirable for better provisioning to segmented interests as well as for
enhancing the overall outreach of these bodies.
    Of interest to note is that the academy and its constituent societies were borne of
the efforts of the scientific community and not out of government intervention (Tan
& Subramaniam, 1999, 2009a, 2009b). To date, none of these have permanent
premises or full-time staff. The annual reports of the academy and its nine societies
show that the absence of premises and full-time staff has not come in their way in
the pursuit of their mission objectives. Also, they do not receive any funding from
the government. In fact, most academies now receive some funding from govern-
ments. Noteworthy is the inclusion of science teachers as an association plus the
presence of science teachers in the other societies. Both the academy and its
constituent societies view school students as an important target for science com-
munication outreach, and thus the presence of science teachers helps in realizing
this important objective. It has to be reiterated that in the traditional mode of
operation of scientific academies and societies, membership is predominantly or
completely comprised of scientists. This is an important departure in the Singapore
context and reiterates the point that when the model of a scientific academy or
scientific society is to be emulated by other countries, some adaptation for local
contexts is necessary while keeping in mind the spirit of the original mission.
    The Young Scientist Badge Scheme is one of the contributions of science-based
learned societies in Singapore to the scientific literature (Tan & Subramaniam,
1998, 2009a, 2009b). The scheme is jointly organized by the Singapore National
Academy of Science, the Singapore Association for the Advancement of Science,
the Science Teachers’ Association of Singapore, and the Science Centre Singapore.
It is an innovative scheme that encourages primary school students to engage in
project-based investigations in 17 scientific areas. Students need to purchase an
Role of Learned Societies in Communicating Science                                189


activity card (S$2.50) – for example, I am a Young Physicist, I am a Young Chemist,
etc. – and complete a selection of activities enumerated in the card. For example,
for the “I am a Young Astronomer” badge, students need to build a telescope in the
cheapest possible way, be able to identify certain constellations in the night sky in
Singapore, write an essay on a famous astronomer, and so on. When the activities
are completed, the class teacher certifies these, and the prestigious badge is
presented at a public investiture ceremony held to honor the young scientists.
Started in 1982, the scheme is still going strong. Typically, over 50,000 students
earn their “stripes” annually. The scheme is an important revenue generator and the
funds help to support deserving programs for the cause of science. Its mechanics of
revenue generation is very simple – more students buy activity cards than complete
the cards, thus ensuring a healthy surplus of funds! More importantly, the scheme
has helped to promote the cause of communicating science to the student
population.



Africa

The conditions for the emergence of science-based learned societies in Africa were
not very encouraging at the start. It was only about 250 years after the establishment
of the Royal Society that the first learned society in the sciences took root in
sub-Saharan Africa (Table 5). The second academy was established about
57 years later. In recent times, a few more academies have been established. The
academies have their own premises and receive some funding from their govern-
ments. Their current numbers (9) are, however, rather few for a continent of
54 states.
   A few reasons can be cited for this state of affairs. Several of these countries
have been under colonial rule for a long time, and, as a result, irreverence for
science, which is commonly perceived to be Western in origin, could have devel-
oped. The scientific community is also small in number and could not come
together for a common cause owing to political instability, poverty, and general
apathy. This could possibly explain why several of these countries are reluctant to
embrace science for economic development.
   More reasons can also be cited for the slow establishment of scientific academies
and societies in the African continent. Firstly is the obsession with the brick-and-
mortar model, replete with secretariat staff (Tan & Subramaniam, 2009a), for the
setting up of these academies, which is the current mode of operation in Western
countries. While this model has been working smoothly in the Western world, it has
to be borne in mind that many of these academies started off on the “invisible
college” (Clarke, 1985, p. 23) model – that is, they were run initially as volunteer
outfits with no permanent premises; it was only later when they have established a
track record that they were transformed into institutions for better functioning, and
many do receive funding from their governments. In our opinion, the brick-and-
mortar model is not suitable for developing countries aiming to start scientific
190                                                     L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam
