Ghana          Ghana Academy of Arts and Sciences                            1959
Nigeria        Nigerian Academy of Science                                   1977
Kenya          Kenyan National Academy of Sciences                           1983
Uganda         Uganda National Academy of Sciences                           2000
Zambia         Zambia Academy of Sciences                                    2005
Tanzania       Tanzania Academy of Sciences                                  2005
Mauritius      Mauritius Academy of Science and Technology                   2007
Mozambique     Academy of Science of Mozambique                              2009
Ethiopia       Ethiopian Academy of Sciences                                 2010


academies and scientific societies. Indeed, there seems to be a prevalent view that
only with the setting up of premises and the hiring of secretariat staff, all of which
entail costs, can work be started by academies and societies! The “invisible college”
model offers the best chance for realizing the vision and mission of scientific
academies and scientific societies because the start-up costs are minimal, no
buildings are needed to situate the offices, and also such a mode of operation can
attract those with a passion for promoting science; a core group of scientists can
rally together to form one. A good example is the Singapore National Academy of
Science and its nine constituent societies – since its formation in the 1970s, none of
them have permanent premises or full-time staff (Tan & Subramaniam, 1999,
2009b). Secondly, there is the inertia on the part of the scientific community to
come together for a worthy cause for the benefit of the country and do something
constructive for the community. This is inhibited by the emergence of a champion
or iconic figure from among them who can rally other scientists around for the cause
of science – often personality clashes and group rivalry can prevent these from
being realized. The few scientific academies that have evolved in the African
continent are an encouraging sign, though. Thirdly, there is a lack of branch
academies as well as scientific societies catering to the wide range of scientific
disciplines. Given the size of many states in the African continent, it is desirable for
more than one academy to be in operation in each state, especially in major cities
and also a range of disciplinary societies. This is common even when the Royal
Society was first established in the UK – other learned societies started to sprout in
inner cities for the cause of science. Again, if the brick-and-mortar model were to
be emulated for their establishment, many of these may not even be established.
The “invisible college” model offers the best promise for scientific academies and
scientific societies to start operations in the shortest possible time.
    In the case of a few established scientific academies set up in the African
continent, a satisfactory track record seems to have been established. For new
academies of science, it seems that progress has been slow and not up to expecta-
tions. According to Mohamed Hassan, former executive director of the Third World
Academy of Sciences, this could be due to two reasons – lack of leaders who can
communicate well and reach out to people as well as who can work on a full-time
Role of Learned Societies in Communicating Science                                  191


basis for the first 3 years, and failure to involve young scientists who are in the
forefront of their fields (quoted in Nordling, 2010). To this, we can add another
reason – the priority to elect Fellows without the academies first chalking up an
impressive track record of reaching out to the community through various science-
based initiatives. For example, in the case of the Singapore National Academy of
Science, it spent the first 35 years building a track record before electing Fellows
only in 2011.




Suggestions for Science-Based Learned Societies
in the Developing World

Science-based learned societies, especially in the UK and USA, have not only been
effective in promoting their mission objectives but have also been able to transform
themselves into venerable institutions that are held in high esteem by stakeholders.
Examination of their progress over the years indicates three key features which
stand out in these countries. First is the spirit of civic activism for a worthy cause –
by not waiting for the state to take action on perceived shortcomings in promoting
the cause of science, a group of scientists took the lead in addressing these
deficiencies by galvanizing the scientific community into action. Second is the
pursuit of scholarly excellence – the coming together of scientists for the exchange
of ideas on research, organizing of public lectures, establishment of scholarly
journals for the dissemination of research results, public programs, and conferring
of a range of awards to recognize scientific talent have helped to promote the cause
of science and science communication. Third is the pursuit of entrepreneurship –
for example, the establishing of revenue-generating operations such as sale of
scholarly literature through journals, book publishing programs, membership fees,
and income from events. The revenue stream helps the learned societies to guard
their independent status and not be beholden to governments or other donors for
sustenance. Also, it has helped a number of these societies to move away from the
original “invisible college” model and have their own premises and staff to improve
their functioning further. Over a period of time, they have been able to establish a
track record. This has helped a number of these bodies to become institutions of
excellence in their own right and which others, even from foreign countries, want to
be associated with. Many of these societies now employ a large complement of staff
to oversee their myriad of operations and programs. The internationalizing of their
operations has also helped in the entrepreneurship aspect – for example, people
from outside the UK see their election as Fellow of one of the learned societies,
such as the Royal Society of Chemistry, Institute of Physics or Institute of Biology,
as an honor which they are prepared to sustain by payment of annual
membership fees.
192                                                   L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


    From Singapore, five aspects can be identified in particular for consideration.
One is the pursuit of the “invisible college” (Clarke, 1985) model for operation.
Leveraging on this model allows for the setting up of learned societies with very
little fuss and in the shortest possible time. For example, many learned societies can
be established in different parts of a developing country with this model. This
provides scientists in these parts of the country a platform to provide leadership for
the cause of science communication and build a track record of service to society. If
they were to wait for election to a national body, it may not even materialize simply
because of the competition. It may therefore be better for a group of them to start a
suitable learned society and move up the value chain in due course. Second is the
need for an academy to have a number of constituent societies affiliated to it so that
more disciplinary interests in science could be pursued; in this way, the outreach
effectiveness for the cause of science can be enhanced. Third is the desirability for
scientists to establish a track record of service to learned societies before election to
Fellowship of the academy. As mentioned earlier, many of the new academies have
invariably viewed the election of Fellows as an important consideration. In the case
of Singapore, this option was not pursued at all during formation, even though there
were a good number of scientists at that time; it was held in abeyance for over three
decades so that a track record can be established by the academy and its constituent
societies. While scientific excellence is still the primary consideration for election
to Fellowship, examination of the backgrounds of the 11 Fellows elected in 2011
shows that all of them have a track record of service to learned societies. Fourth is
the involvement of science teachers as a constituent society of the academy and
their presence in other constituent societies; their presence helps to reach out to a
key target audience – school students. Fifth is the long-running Young Scientist
badge scheme. Details of this are available in the public domain and allow other
learned societies to either emulate this program or adapt it to local contexts.
    From Africa, it can be seen that there is overreliance on the brick-and-mortar
model for the setting up of scientific academies as well as the emphasis on the
election of Fellows. Also, the number of academies in the continent is rather few,
and there are very few learned societies catering to specialized interests. We feel
that for new academies to be set up in Africa, it is necessary to move away from this
model and embrace the “invisible college” model used by learned societies in
Singapore.
    The concept of learned societies represents a paradigm shift in the way science is
organized. While universities still represent the traditional portals of teaching,
learning, and research in the higher education sector, learned societies in the
sciences can help in uniting scientists and interested people into a “club” for the
pursuit of other suitable objectives. The locus of influence is broadened with such a
membership base, which typically comprises people from academia, public sector,
private sector, and others. The sheer diversity of the membership base allows
specific interests in the various disciplines to be pursued more effectively as there
is strength in numbers.
    More encouragement needs to be given for the establishment of scientific
academies and scientific societies in the developing world, especially the African
Role of Learned Societies in Communicating Science                                 193


continent. The success of this movement in the developed world allows inspiration
for its emulation by others. While the “invisible college” model can allow for the
emergence of more of such learned societies catering for diverse interests in the
African continent, any support from the developed world to make this movement a
success will contribute to capacity building. For example, rich nations and interna-
tional aid agencies that provide aid to developing countries can channel some of
these funds for programs that can be spearheaded by these learned societies. It is
common knowledge that often aid does not reach the intended recipients. More
importantly, provision of aid does not serve to wean countries away from the
dependency mind-set. For example, one report indicated that over the past
60 years, about US$1 trillion dollar worth of development aid has been given by
rich nations to African countries, but this has not translated into increase in per
capita income for the people in real terms (Moyo, 2009). One reason for this could
be that sufficient funds have not been allocated for capacity building efforts, not
only within the country but also in the scientific community.
   The cause of science communication can be greatly facilitated by the formation
of more learned societies in science in developing countries, especially those in the
African continent. The inertia and apathy of the scientific community need to give
way to enthusiasm and confidence for the future. After all, it requires only a few
scientists with passion, commitment, and zeal to form a learned society – for
example, the American Chemical Society was formed when 35 chemists got
together; the American Physical Society was formed when 36 physicists met
together; and the Microscopical Society of London was established when 17 biol-
ogists got together. It needs to be reiterated that the success of the learned society
movement is as much not only in their formation but also in their successful
operation over the years.
   The institutional framework afforded by science-based learned societies offers
tremendous scope for realizing science communication objectives from
multidimensional perspectives. Many of such bodies in the developed world have
been able to promote their mission objectives very effectively. Their low numbers
in many developing countries mean that this unique setup has not been exploited
purposefully for science communication efforts.



Conclusion

Science communication cannot be accomplished by a sole player. It needs a
diversity of players for its objectives to be realized. Science-based learned societies
have been involved in the cause of communicating science even before institutions
such as science centers were established. The scientific society movement is a very
noble grassroots endeavor for the cause of science. While these learned societies in
the developed world have generally been successful, the situation is not particularly
satisfactory for countries in the developing world, more so in the African continent.
The public interfaces afforded by learned societies for science communication in
194                                                         L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


these countries have yet to be fully tapped. This chapter has examined some reasons
for this state of affairs and also suggested some areas for consideration in an attempt
to improve the functioning of these societies as well as the establishing of new
learned societies, all of which have implication for the cause of communicating
science.

Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying
Talent in the Sciences: The Singapore
Experience

Shirley S.L. Lim, Horn-Mun Cheah, and T.S. Andy Hor




Abstract Science olympiads are important vehicles for science communication,
especially between scientists, educators, and students. When students participate in
these olympiads, they gain exposure to a different view of science from that which
they experience at the high school level. Close contact with university faculty and
science researchers will provide students with a glimpse of what careers in science
can offer. For those who will not pursue science any further than high school, at
least they will hopefully have a higher level of scientific literacy.
   There is scant literature on the science olympiads, especially on best practices
from countries which have consistently done well in these competitions. Aiming to
address this issue, our chapter focuses on the Singapore experience. There are
multiple factors that contribute to Singapore’s sustained success in these competi-
tions over three decades. These include (1) a strong culture in science and mathe-
matics learning in schools and universities coupled with ample career opportunities
in science and technology, (2) the internationally competitive science and mathe-
matics curricula in Singapore schools, (3) the strong support from Singapore’s
education authority for students to be exposed to such competitions, (4) the

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,            195
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_12, © Springer Netherlands 2014
196                                                                   S.S.L. Lim et al.


involvement of dedicated university academics and teachers in mentoring students,
and (5) the partnership of scientific societies in the natural sciences in organizing
subject-based national olympiads to identify top talents for further grooming and
the rigorous enrichment programs. Students could also be motivated by the honor of
winning a gold medal which potentially enhances their chances of gaining admis-
sion into a number of top universities (inclusive of the two premier local universi-
ties), especially when only few foreigners are admitted annually.




Introduction

The increasing role that science plays in socioeconomic development as well as in
everyday life is well recognized. Given today’s globalized and connected nature,
the ability of an economy to leverage on the discoveries of science and technology
(S&T) has direct implications on the growth and competitiveness of the economy.
Ensuring that a country’s citizenry has sufficient S&T literacies has, thus, taken on
greater imperative.
   Over the years, various science communication initiatives have been found to be
effective in instilling the citizenry with knowledge of S&T. For example, science
centers popularize S&T to the masses through exhibitions, enrichment programs,
promotional activities, and publication programs – since they are informal destina-
tions for science learning where assessment is not required, even adults find the
exhibits and knowledge gain appealing (Falk & Storksdieck, 2005). Likewise,
science journalism (via media such as newspapers, magazines, television, and
radio) has been reaching out to people to inform them of developments in S&T as
well as highlighting socio-scientific issues such as tsunamis and nuclear radiation.
In societies where personal computer penetration rates are high, the Internet offers
people a window to numerous websites to learn more about S&T.
   One aspect of science promotion which has garnered increasing momentum in
recent years is the national science olympiads. Assessing advanced content profi-
ciency and practical skills in biology, chemistry, and physics, these olympiads have
for several years served to instill the importance of science to students as well as
identify budding talents in the sciences in a country. The questions in the theory
tests of these competitions demand higher-order thinking skills, requiring students
to demonstrate their ability to reason, support their arguments with evidence, and
integrate and apply their knowledge across different conceptual strands. Olympiad
winners are not only strong in head knowledge but must also prove their ability to
demonstrate science process skills to solve practical problems in the practical
segment of the competitions. Each discipline’s national science olympiad, by its
very nature, reaches out to large number of students. In populous and large
countries such as China, it is estimated that some 30,000 high school students
participate in the initial rounds of the national olympiads annually. Hence, the
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .    197


potential of the olympiads to contribute to science promotion efforts should not be
underestimated. Exposure to higher levels of content knowledge and the various
ways of doing science are facilitated when high school students get to interact with
fellow science students, enthusiastic teachers, and academics who are experts in
their respective fields. More importantly, these students who are still in their teens
get to experience and understand attitudes and practices of scientists firsthand when
they are immersed in the scientific realm during the mentorship programs.
   The number of member countries that participate in the three science olympiads
has increased several folds through the years (Table 1). After more than 40 years
since their inceptions, member countries for the International Chemistry Olympiad
(IChO) and International Physics Olympiad (IPhO) swelled by a factor of 24 and
17, respectively. Although the International Biology Olympiad (IBO) has been in
existence for 24 years, there is already a 10.5 increase in its membership. These
phenomenal increases provide evidence that many countries now recognize the role
that science olympiads can play in reinforcing the importance of science among
school students as well as providing them the necessary exposure of participating in
an international competition, thereby honing their social skills and promoting the
practice of networking.
   The olympiads also serve as an important platform to identify the top talent in
the three science disciplines. Firstly, the student competitors are benchmarked at
the international level and not just within national boundaries (in national-level
examinations). Secondly, unlike other international benchmarking studies such as
the Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) or Programme
for International Student Assessment (PISA), the olympiads pit the best in each
country with one another. In addition, TIMSS and PISA involve strictly pen and
paper assessment, whereas science olympiads place heavy emphases on practical
skills, thus providing a more holistic gauge of student capabilities. The nature of
science olympiads, whether national or international, is such that only students who
have deep conceptual understanding of the subject matter and are very proficient in
experimental work will have the requisite problem-solving and critical thinking
skills to emerge among the top of their cohort. Hence, olympiad competitors and
winners could be considered to be some of the best science talents nationally and/or
internationally.




Literature Review

In the area of science communication/promotion, it seems that science olympiads,
whether the national or international versions, have been the subject of only a
limited number of studies in the literature. Despite being a premier event in the
international science education calendar, the olympiads have not attracted the
attention of science education and science communication researchers. This could
be due to the following reasons:
198                                                                          S.S.L. Lim et al.

olympiads
                                            Number of      Current
            Year of                         participating number of      Number of olympiads
            first      First host           countries at participating held since inception
Olympiad olympiad country                   inaugural      countries     (2013 inclusive)
IBO         1990       Czech Republic       6              59 (63)       24
                           (Olomouc)
IChO        1968       Czech Republic       3              72 (73)       45
                           (Prague)
IPhO        1967       Poland (Warsaw)      5              82 (85)       44

respectively), and not all member countries send a delegation every year
(#): Number of member countries
IBO International Biology Olympiad, IChO International Chemistry Olympiad, IPhO Interna-
tional Physics Olympiad



(a) The confidential nature of the items set in the tests means that science education
    researchers have no access to test papers before the competition begins. Thus,
    they are not able to construct appropriate research questions in relation to the
    items in the tests and sample the students. It is pertinent to highlight that it is not
    the primary objective of the science olympiads to provide such platforms for
    science education research. Unlike TIMMS and PISA, whereby an independent
    agency is solely responsible for the setting of the questions, olympiad questions
    are set by a different country every year. Although the questions designed by
    the host countries are ultimately shared with the science fraternity for usage as
    practice questions, the intellectual property rights of the questions belong to the
    academics who were involved in the question-setting process.
(b) As students sit for the tests under intense conditions and are usually exhausted
    after that, their availability to take part in surveys is limited.
(c) Organizers are usually reluctant to allow access to science education
    researchers to study aspects of the olympiads as the findings may have political
    or sensitive implications, especially if comparisons were to be made among the
    different educational systems. Recently, however, there was a survey conducted
    by some veteran science olympiad mentors on the teaching and assessment of
    practical skills in the IBO participating countries. This survey was administered
    online to the IBO coordinators (i.e., not the student participants) from all the
    member countries, and it was on a voluntary basis that responses were given.
(d) Detailed scores of students for each of the questions are not made available to
    outside parties. According to the international science olympiad rules, the final
    scores and ranks of all the competitors must be disseminated before the
    participants depart from the host country. The list has only the final scores
    without the details of the individual components of each test and questions
    therein. Hence, detailed analyses of the assessment and performance of the
    students are not possible with these compiled data.
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .     199


   There are, however, a few reports on some aspects of the science olympiads such
as their impact and influence on student recruitment, attitudes toward science, etc.
Wetmore (1978) reported that the organization of an annual fun-filled “science
olympiad” was an effective means to recruit science-oriented students at a small
American college. Although these events were called olympiads, they mainly
comprised demonstration sessions and “games” in biology, chemistry, and physics,
organized as a day-long session for school students in the geographic region of the
organizing college. It was organized in response to the difficulty of getting school
students who are interested in science to enroll in the college in question. Similarly,
Wilson (1981) noted that science olympiads are effective in promoting interest in
science. Again, though these were called olympiads, they were more of events
organized to foster college-high school interaction, with a view toward encouraging
high school students in science.
   Male and female North Carolina high school students were reported by Jones
(1991) to have different preferences in the selection of science competitions (e.g.,
the olympiads) in which to participate. Her results showed that males tend to have
higher interest and participation in the physical sciences, while females have more
interest and participation in the biological sciences. To address this gender bias, she
called for female students to be given more opportunities to get research experi-
ences in the physical sciences as well as for teachers to teach content in these
subjects in ways that better engage them.
   Baird, Shaw, and McLarty (1996) found that instruments based on the Test of
Integrative Process Skills and Group Assessment of Logical Thinking were fairly
good predictors of success in science competitions for junior and senior high school
students in the USA. The data used in their study were extracted from regional,
state, and national levels of three selected science olympiads that were described as
day-long series of events designed to foster academic competition in activity-based
science.
   The offer of rewards in competitive events such as science fairs and science
olympiads is often criticized, and there is a tendency for these competitions to be
viewed by advocates mainly as events that assess the mastery of content and
process skills in students. Findings from Abernathy and Vineyard’s (2001) study
showed that students from Utah (USA) who participated in science fairs and science
olympiads expressed that they enjoyed their experience in these competitive events
and even had fun, once they were engaged, despite pressure from teachers to
participate, regardless of external incentives. While Covington (2000) observed
that the competitive nature of science fairs and science olympiads promote stu-
dents’ performance goals rather than learning goals, Abernathy and Vineyard’s
results showed that the students valued learning goals despite the embedded
competition.
   In addition, Abernathy and Vineyard argued that the notion that students are
affected emotionally in competitive situations, with decreased motivation, cannot
be generalized as the literature on motivation and achievement goals have focused
primarily on classroom-based competitions for scant rewards (see Covington &
Teel, 1996). They caution that extrapolation of such inferences to the science
200                                                                    S.S.L. Lim et al.


olympiads and other events is not warranted as there is yet to be any research
conducted to study the impact of extracurricular competitions on students’ motiva-
tion and goals.
   The first European Science Olympiads, held in 2003, has been the subject of two
studies. Its genesis is traced by van Kampen et al. (2004), who assessed an
experimental problem in biology with a physics component set in the competition.
The tasks for biology in the competition were explored by O’Kennedy et al. (2005),
who also elaborated on the benefits of students being organized in interdisciplinary
teams to tackle tasks.
   Oliver and Venville (2011) explored the attitudes of students who took part in
the Australian Science Olympiad on two aspects during the summer camp: school
science and science. The students generally have favorable dispositions toward
school science and selected science as one of their preferred subjects. An interesting
finding emerging from the study is that exposure to the summer camp has the effect
of igniting their passion for science.
   Examination of the limited literature on science olympiads shows the following
observations:
(a) Almost all studies have come from Western countries (USA, Europe, and
    Australia, as cited by the examples above). To the authors’ knowledge, there
    is only one international conference presentation by Lim and Lim (2004) on the
    Singapore approach to preparing students for competition at the annual IBOs.
    This is surprising as Asian countries (e.g., China, Chinese Taipei, Korea,
    Thailand, etc.) have consistently been doing well in the international science
    olympiads, and a perspective from them is lacking.
(b) Studies have concentrated on only certain aspects of the olympiads (e.g., mostly
    based on data collected at national-level competitions in individual countries),
    thus indicating the possibility of fruitful research directions in other areas.
(c) Best practices from countries which have done well in the international science
    olympiads are lacking in the literature. This limits the sharing of experiences to
    the wider fraternity.
    In light of the above, the principal objective of this chapter is to share the
Singapore experience in the international science olympiads and how this serves
as another avenue of direct communications between scientists and potential
scientists (i.e., students talented in science). Teams that represent the country are
usually selected from the winners of the national olympiads. The inaugural Singa-
pore Physics Olympiad (SPhO) and Singapore Chemistry Olympiad (SChO) were
held in 1988 and 1989, respectively, thereby giving a participation history of about
25 years. The first Singapore Biology Olympiad (SBO) was held in 2000, with the
first national team competing in 2001 in Brussels, Belgium (Table 2). Singapore has
done consistently well in these international competitions, especially in recent
times, as can be seen from Table 2. In fact, over the years 2009–2012, the Singapore
teams for the IBO, IChO, and IPhO have ranked among the top five countries in
their respective competitions, with a few exceptions in one or two years.
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .               201

   As a tiny nation with no natural resources, Singapore places great emphasis on
developing and nurturing her human resources, in particular, through the education
system. Sustainability and renewal of a pool of scientists are important for a
country’s continued development, growth, and innovation. Through the national
olympiads as well as the successful participatory track record at the various science
olympiads, many students, parents, and members of the public have been brought in
contact with academics and researchers in the tertiary institutions and research
institutes. This in itself serves as a very powerful communication channel between
scientists, the public, and potential scientists. The experiences of Singapore in the
science olympiads would, thus, be of interest as it can offer useful pointers to other
developing countries as to how greater overall science awareness can be fostered
among citizens as well as the grooming of future cohorts of scientists. As most
countries view sustainability and renewal of scientists as vital to their continued
growth and development, the positive experiences from Singapore may thus serve
as an encouragement to participate in the olympiads.



The Singapore Experience

The thrust for Singapore to participate in the science olympiads came from the
discipline-based scientific societies – the Singapore Institute of Biology (SIBiol),
the Singapore National Institute of Chemistry (SNIC), and the Institute of Physics
Singapore (IPS), all of which are constituent members of the Singapore National
Academy of Sciences (SNAS). The key office bearers in these professional socie-
ties are persons from various vocations who are keen to promote their respective
disciplines. It is fortuitous that the lead was taken by them as it transcends
institutional affiliations – if it were to be promoted by a particular university, it
may not be that easy to get the cooperation and support of other stakeholders.
   Owing to the complexities of preparing students for the international science
olympiads, it is difficult to identify a single factor that can best explain Singapore’s
consistently good showing over the years. More than one factor is definitely at
202                                                                       S.S.L. Lim et al.


work, and we discuss this “ecology for success” in this section by highlighting
important aspects of each individual factor.
(a) Promotion of STEM culture in Singapore
    Since the time that Singapore gained her independence in 1965, there has always
been a strong emphasis on the sciences, technology, engineering, and mathematics
(STEM), both in the educational and economic sectors. Singapore has no hinterland
to provide natural resources – the only resource that the tiny island has is human
resource. Hence, her leaders decided early in the days of new independence, that the
education system has to be rigorous and intensive, with emphasis on immediately
practical, rather than intellectual, applications, such as on technical sciences, rather
than political discussion or philosophy. This is the most practical way for the small
island state to strive towards a knowledge-based economy in the shortest period of
time. It is common knowledge that a large portion of Singapore’s budget (second
only to defense expenditure) is devoted to education to facilitate a large and
competent workforce. However, the strong push of S&T is much more than
historical – it is a pragmatic way for the small country to drive up quality of life
through economic development that is S&T based. Emphases on S&T permeate all
the way from schools to universities; ultimately, those who are science-inclined
usually have successful careers in science, engineering, and technology.
(b) Support from the country’s national educational authority
   If prestigious competitions such as the international science olympiads do not
have the support of a country’s educational authority, it is difficult for such events
to be mainstreamed into the national science education calendar of events. In the
case of Singapore, her Ministry of Education (MOE) has been very supportive of
the scientific community’s efforts in this direction. The support takes several forms
– for example, active encouragement to schools and the scientific community to
identify and nurture young students who have a strong interest and aptitude in
science; ample funding for the mentorship and selection of the Singapore team, as
well as a yearly budget to send a contingent, inclusive of students and mentors and
officials, overseas for these and similar competitions so as to provide international
exposure to the students.
(c) Leveraging on tertiary education expertise in the sciences to mentor students
   Universities represent a concentration of intellectual resources in various disci-
plines, including the sciences. Thus, it is logical to leverage on the expertise of
faculty members in these sectors to help develop students for the olympiads. The
nature of questions set in the theory and practical papers in these competitions
challenges the analytical mind and creative thoughts of our intellectually capable as
they are specially crafted each year by university faculty and educational experts
from the host country.
   School teachers, with limited free time, given their onerous responsibilities in
schools, usually assist in the initial identification of school representatives to be sent
for the national olympiads. In addition, for certain disciplines such as biology,
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .       203


students need exposure to many biological fields that are either not included or
briefly covered in the regular Singapore school syllabi (e.g., systematics, ecology,
animal behavior, evolution, etc.) and access to specialists in these specific areas, as
well as resources are crucial in order to prepare for the IBO. University staff
members are ideally positioned in this regard owing to their depth of knowledge,
experience, expertise, and research-level knowledge. For the IChO and IPhO,
research-active and experimental scientists and teaching faculty members are
drafted annually to help the students prepare for the olympiads. In the IBO
preparations, depending on the focus (especially for the practical tasks) of any
particular year’s competition, extra experts may be called upon to mentor the
students in the required field.
   Natural sciences staff members in the two premier universities in Singapore –
the National University of Singapore and the National Institute of Education,
Nanyang Technological University, both of which are in the top 50 in the interna-
tional rankings – provide active support in the mentorship of students. More
importantly, these scientist mentors have the opportunity to communicate their
passion and practices to enthuse their mentees in their various fields of expertise.
Within any subject discipline – biology, chemistry, and physics – there are usually
sufficient experts specializing in the various subtopics to provide students with the
necessary guidance. The role of institutional mechanisms to incentivize staff
participation should not be discounted, though most will still contribute even
without such inducements. For example, many of the enrichment sessions are
conducted on Saturdays, which is a nonworking day for the public sector in
Singapore; sometimes, owing to schedule constraints, mentors have to meet the
students during evenings too.
(d) Involvement of teachers in mentorship
    With students spending a good portion of their time in school, it is only logical
that school teachers should play a role in the mentorship program. However, not all
teachers would necessarily be up to the task or possess the inclination for such
work. In Singapore schools, a fair number of teachers have graduate degrees (e.g.,
MSc and PhD). Such staff members are likely to have the aptitude and subject
mastery to contribute to the initial grooming and eventual selection of the students
for competition at the national science olympiads’ level. In addition, the various
discipline-based scientific societies regularly conduct workshops for these teachers
to facilitate the preparation of their students for the national science olympiads, i.e.,
Singapore Biology Olympiad (SBO), Singapore Chemistry Olympiad (SChO), and
Singapore Physics Olympiad (SPhO). The involvement of school teachers is espe-
cially useful for preparation of the students in relation to the theory component of
the olympiads and to a limited extent on experimental work.
    It is pertinent to note that for every trip that the national team makes, the
delegation usually includes a school teacher or MOE official, funded by the
MOE. This provides a good learning experience for the teacher or MOE official.
Through the years, many education officers have been to at least one of the
international olympiads in their respective fields. When they return to the schools,
204                                                                    S.S.L. Lim et al.


they bring back firsthand experience to share with their colleagues and students
with regard to the proceedings of the international olympiads, thereby fostering a
continued interest in these competitions as well as facilitating preparation of future
cohorts of students.
(e) Involvement of scientific societies
   Selection of students for participation in the international science olympiads is
based on their performance in the national olympiads in the various sciences. These
national olympiads are organized by the respective scientific societies – the SIBiol,
the SNIC, and the IPS, all of which are constituent societies of the SNAS. The
membership of the constituent societies spans a wide spectrum – for example,
public sector, private sector, and individuals who are there just for the love of the
discipline. However, it is mainly the university dons and research scientists who
take the lead roles in setting questions for the national olympiads, organizing the
competitions, marking the test papers, and identifying the top students not only
through test results but also through interviews.
   More importantly, these volunteers are not remunerated and members are there
just to promote interest in their respective disciplines. The national olympiads
provide a robust platform for the organizers to identify top talent to take part in
the international olympiads – a selection process that is transparent, meritocratic,
defensible, and bereft of favoritism. That is, only the best students get to don
national colors in the international science olympiads.
(f) Rewards system for winners of international science olympiads
   In the process of identifying talent among students, there needs to be adequate
follow-up mechanisms to ensure that the talents so identified are nurtured further.
Otherwise, it becomes a one-off affair with no proper follow-through. In the case of
Singapore, for admission to universities, the entrance requirements are waived for
gold medal winners of the olympiads – that is, they will be accepted into the local
universities in their respective disciplines of excellence, regardless of the outcome
of the school leaving level examinations such as the Singapore-Cambridge GCE
“A” level or International Baccalaureate examinations. The rationale is that they
have already demonstrated their proficiency and competency in the subject in a
highly regarded and rigorous international platform. In addition, scholarships for all
medal winners (regardless of color) for tertiary study at local universities and/or
universities overseas are also often offered after winning such prestigious interna-
tional recognition.
(g) Internationally competitive national science and mathematics curriculum
   In Singapore, government schools follow a national curriculum. Reviews of the
school science and mathematics curriculum are done on a regular basis. The
reviews are aimed at ensuring that the country’s curriculum adequately prepares
our future generations with the necessary 21st century skills to thrive in our
complex and multidimensional world, such that Singapore’s future needs are met.
They are also instrumental in producing a sustained workforce that is
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .     205


internationally competitive – after all, Singapore is also home to about 10,000
multinational corporations, which provide good jobs for her people. In this regard,
the national education authorities routinely scan the horizon for best practices, not
only from studying other countries’ educational systems but also surveying the
educational research literature for important trends. It is pertinent to note that the
Singapore education system has gone from strength to strength as a result of the
continual evolution of the system. Initiatives such as “teach less, learn more” and
the nurturing of the “thinking child” are examples of how the national education
authorities have revised the curriculum to create white space and learning time
beyond the classroom as well as to de-emphasize assessment modes based on
testing only rote learning abilities.
    The McKinsey Report (2007), in a review of the education systems of 20 coun-
tries, cited the Singapore education system as being among the best in the world.
Some of the reasons contributing to this include high quality of teachers and school
leaders; hiring of teachers from the top third of graduating students; career advance-
ment opportunities for teachers in any of three different tracks, leadership, teaching,
and specialist; and continuing professional development programs of about 100 h
annually for teachers. Not to be overlooked is the high quality of teacher education
provided by the sole teacher training institute in Singapore.
    Singapore’s strong performance in international benchmarking studies in the
sciences and mathematics reflects curricula that are able to meet international
standards. The incremental effort necessary to do well in the olympiads can be
achieved with suitable mentorship programs as the fundamental proficiency in
subject content is already present at a high standard.
(h) Mentorship of students for international olympiads in sciences
   The first stage in preparing students for the international olympiads in the
sciences is the organizing of the national olympiads in biology, chemistry, and
physics. In this respect, the role of the MOE is crucial in garnering the schools’
support to send their top students in each discipline to compete in the SBO, SChO,
and SPhO.



Singapore Biology Olympiad

The initial number of student competitors for the first round of competition (i.e., the
3-h theoretical tests) is usually about 300–350, depending on the cohort size for
each year. The top 10 % of these students (usually about 30–35 students) will be
selected for the next round of competition – the practical tests which involve an
entire day of participation. This second round of selection is very intense and
vigorous as it involves four practical tests in various fields in biology, each of
1½ h duration. Only students who did well in both the theoretical tests and practical
tests will be awarded SBO medals. The top eight to 10 students (dependent on their
scores) will be short-listed for interviews before being invited to join the advanced
206                                                                      S.S.L. Lim et al.


mentorship program: a five day-long residential camp. At the end of this intensive
period, the final four students who get to wear national colors at the IBO will be
announced.



Singapore Chemistry Olympiad

Schools are invited to send about 15–20 competitors each – these students will
necessarily be the top students in the school. Participation in the SChO is on a
voluntary basis. The students have to sit for a theory test that lasts for up to 4 h. The
top 60 (5) students from the theory test go on to the practical round, after which
about 20 students are selected to join the IChO squad. There are enrichment lessons
and practical tasks organized for them, typically lasting for about three months.
These sessions do not disrupt their regular school schedules as there is no full-time
mentoring or special night or week camps as organized by some IChO member
countries. There are typically selection tests along the way to trim down the squad
from 20 to about eight and then to the final four who would represent the country to
take part in the IChO.



Singapore Physics Olympiad

The procedure is similar to that for the SChO, except that the top 40 students from the
theoretical test will go on to compete in the practical test. Upon selection, the
candidates are given further mentorship, which involves both the building up of
theoretical knowledge as well as practical skills. Often, the practical sessions require
the candidates to operate under similar conditions as the IPhO, working on experiments
selected from past IPhO. From this pool, about 20 students are selected for advanced
mentoring and they then sit for a selection test. Eight of the top performing students in
this selection test will take part in the Asian Physics Olympiad, and the best five
performing students from this competition will go on to participate in the IPhO.

   Interviews are conducted when selecting the medal winners from the SBO and
SChO for further mentoring, as they need to also have the aptitude, equanimity of
personality, and mental toughness to take part in the grueling high-stakes interna-
tional olympiads. Special efforts are also taken to ensure that the selected students
will also make good ambassadors for the country when they participate in the
competitions. It must be emphasized that the interviews are restricted to the top
winners and are not a proxy for those not included in the top as that would defeat the
principle of meritocracy that is enshrined in the country’s constitution. There have
been instances in which a student will be selected for advanced mentorship for more
than one science olympiad, and it is evident that he/she can only choose to
participate in only one olympiad selection.
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .     207


   Preparation for competition generally covers the following aspects:
1. Solving of questions from past year olympiads
2. Hands-on practical sessions
3. Lectures/tutorials on specialized topics by university academics
4. Selection tests (both theory and practical)
    Aptitude and attitude are important attributes in any competition. Hence, it is not
always the top four or five (in scores) who will be selected. The students need to
take the mentorship sessions seriously and show potential in their skills mastery as
well as tenacity during the sessions. The enrichment period is sufficient for mentors
to interact with the students, assess their personality, and gauge their proficiency in
the subject. Those students who feature slightly lower in the top ranks in the
national olympiads can still make it to the international olympiads if they display
scholastic excellence, intellectual rigor, and competitive spirit as well as do well
during the mentorship sessions.
    It is pertinent to point out that in the typical Singapore science curriculum, the
practical skills imparted during class are not as rigorous or exacting as that expected
at the olympiad level. Given that the practical component in the olympiads carries a
significant weighting in the overall scores and that students who are especially
proficient in practical work can surge ahead in their overall t-scores, the selection
process also takes this into consideration, with other things being equal. Extra effort
is therefore devoted to practical work – the hands-on sessions in the laboratories
cover a range of explorations using various laboratory techniques. Emphasis is also
placed on acquiring skills in precision and good experimental techniques, and these
are only possible through deep exposure and interaction with mentors in a tertiary
setting.
    The involvement of alumni of the olympiads is also a feature of the olympiad
experience in Singapore. These past winners share their experiences in taking part
in the competitions, especially the mental preparation, the psychology of the high-
stakes tests, and also stories of “failures” and perseverance, which are important
aspects of lessons in life. It is through these experiences that the future leaders of
Singapore benefit the most.



Discussion

One way to groom winners in the international science olympiads is to mentor
talents in the respective natural sciences from young through intensive coaching.
Such practices are not unknown, and have, in fact, produced consistently strong
results in these competitions. Singapore has deliberately refrained from adopting
such approaches, preferring to focus on the holistic development of the child
instead. As such, the emphasis on S&T has been fairly uniformly applied across
the education system, structured such that strengths in the humanities are developed
concurrently. The selected competitors thus emerge from the system by being the
208                                                                    S.S.L. Lim et al.


best in the respective areas rather than being subject to targeted development. In
fact, the performance of students in the international olympiads in the sciences
reflects the state of biology, chemistry, and physics education in the country, which
has always been benchmarked to international standards. Top talents in the sciences
in schools will inevitably surface in the national olympiads. The fun of learning
science and being engaged in it should not be overlooked. Students hot-housed for
the purpose of taking part in such competitions may develop an aversion to science
in later years owing to the “artificial” manner in which they have been mentored.
    Singapore’s approach to the selection of the final team to represent the country
has been a subject of interest among some country coordinators at the IBO. As
mentioned above, mentors have the liberty not to select the top four or five scorers
in the advanced mentorship program, be it due to aptitude or attitude. In many
countries, it is mandatory to pick the top scorers regardless of character or temper-
ament. Winning medals and benchmarking against the world’s elite are secondary
to Singapore’s goal in cultivating an education system that is dynamic and living – a
system that will produce students, not only for national and international competi-
tions or entry into local or overseas universities but scholars who can respond to
challenges in this fast changing world of ours. It is important to remember that the
resources, time, and effort placed into the organization of the many national
olympiads are not solely for the purpose of identifying medal hopefuls, as a larger
body of students can be stimulated to learn more about the science disciplines in the
due process.
    As Singapore’s history of participation in the IChO and IPhO is twice as long as
that for the IBO (about 25 years compared to 13 years), awareness of the respective
national competitions (SChO and SPhO) is probably much greater in senior high
school students (i.e., between the ages of 16 and 18 years). In order to encourage the
study and appreciation of these two disciplines in younger science students (less
than 16 years of age), the IPS, and SNIC (in collaboration with two local schools),
organized the Singapore Junior Physics Olympiad (SJPhO) and Singapore Junior
Chemistry Olympiad (SJChO) in 2008 and 2010, respectively. The popularity of
these junior olympiads pleasantly surprised the organizers: (a) in 2012, 1,693
students competed in the SJPhO, and (b) in the SJChO, 2,000–2,500 students
have competed annually in the three short years since its inception! As there is
hardly any extrinsic reward given out at these junior olympiads, nor do the winners
gain automatic selection to the SPhO or SChO, the high participatory rate is
evidence that students are excited by the opportunity to learn more about science
beyond the school context. There is obviously no “decreased motivation” in the
students nor were they “emotionally affected” in competitive situations as
described by Covington and Teel (1996) in classroom-based competitions – the
Singapore observation provides support for Abernathy and Vineyard’s (2001)
argument that the generalization proposed by Covington and Teel should be viewed
with caution.
    The Singapore experience with the international science olympiads suggests that
it requires the coming together of many parties for synergistic collaboration – the
MOE, universities, discipline-based scientific societies, and schools – in order to
Science Olympiads as Vehicles for Identifying Talent in the Sciences: The. . .     209


achieve a good degree of success in the competitions. A multiparty collaboration is
indispensable as no single party has the necessary resources and expertise to groom
students for sustained interest in the international science olympiads. No one party
can be singled out from among them for being overly instrumental in contributing
to the success of the country in the science olympiads.
   A combination of factors, therefore, is responsible for the country’s consistently
strong showing in the science olympiads. With its internationally competitive
science and mathematics curricula in place as well as the strong support from the
MOE (including financial), it is less challenging for university academics and
scientific societies in the natural sciences to play their role in bringing students to
the next level. In a few countries, commercially hired mentors are also involved in
the mentoring of students for the olympiads. Singapore has refrained from taking
this path as there are many other benefits of the closer communication and interac-
tion between students and the university academics which we have outlined earlier.
The subliminal role-modeling that university academics can promote is also a
strong factor in getting students to not only do well but also be enthused about
science. In this respect, the case study of Singapore has shown that the model
works!
   Many former Singaporean science olympians have embarked on careers in
science after studying and graduating from top universities such as Stanford,
Harvard, MIT, Caltech, Cornell, Duke, and Imperial College. A former IChO
medal winner who did his basic degree in MIT, followed by a PhD in the University
of California (Berkeley), is now an academic in one of the local premier S&T
universities. Another Chemistry olympian is currently a postdoctoral fellow at the
Stanford Medical School, after graduating from Harvard University, followed by an
MD in Duke University. Many former IPhO medal winners have graduated from
MIT in the 25 years of participatory history. One alumnus who did his undergrad-
uate degree at Imperial College (London) is now a faculty member at a local S&T
university after completing his PhD studies in MIT. In addition, this young aca-
demic has been nominated by his students for teaching awards almost every year – a
testimony to his passion in sharing his love for the physics discipline with his
students. Another physics olympian is at the University of Colorado (Boulder)
pursuing his PhD studies after a basic degree from MIT. Past members of the
Singapore IBO teams have also done very well academically in science: (1) one
IBO alumnus, who participated in the 2006 IBO in Argentina, recently published
two papers in science, while still an undergraduate! (2) three alumni from the 2003
IBO, held in Belarus and 2004 IBO in Australia, are currently pursuing their PhDs,
and (3) one alumnus from the first ever Singapore team to participate in the 2003
IBO in Belgium has graduated with a PhD and is currently a biology researcher at
one of the local universities. This is a credible record for IBO alumni as Singapore
has only participated in the IBO for 13 years.
   Another important aspect of the cultivation and nurturing of future scientists is
the continued recruitment of members to join the professional societies. In order to
ensure sustainability and to instill vibrancy in the professional societies, “young
blood” must be recruited for the passing of the baton. Through close association
210                                                                          S.S.L. Lim et al.


with the drivers behind the national olympiads and academic mentors who are
mostly active council members of their professional societies, olympiad alumni are
drawn into the respective societies’ activities. Upon completion of their studies, and
after entering the workforce, many alumni have become members of the profes-
sional societies – in fact, one alumnus of the first Singapore IBO team (2001) has
recently taken up the responsibility of being a council member of the SIBiol. Hence,
through the science olympiads and indirectly through the communication and
induction into the whole ethos of science culture, the love of science is passed
from generation to generation – with far greater outreach than just mere competi-
tions for academic excellence.



Conclusion

There is a hardly any literature on the Asian experience in the international science
olympiads. A number of Asian countries have consistently performed well in these
competitions. This chapter focused on the Singapore experience with the national
and international olympiads in biology, chemistry, and physics and is a small
contribution in this regard. While there may be some useful pointers for developing
countries to draw from the Singapore experience, we must be mindful that many
developing countries face a number of challenges in their socioeconomic develop-
ment. Participating in international science olympiads may not be in their list of
priorities as ample funds have to be allocated to prepare and send a team overseas
for the annual competitions. However, this should not detract them from either
organizing national olympiads in the various sciences or invigorating existing ones
as a way to reinforce the importance of science to students. Indigenous expertise
can be judiciously harnessed for this purpose, either at the regional or national level
in a country. For example, as the Singapore experience with the olympiads shows,
there are opportunities for scientific societies to take a greater stake in organizing
national science olympiads, thus contributing towards raising the profile of science
in a country, and for university scientists to provide mentorship opportunities for
top performing students, as a way of nurturing talent. Participation in international
olympiads can be a logical sequel in due course if circumstances permit. The
far-reaching impact of such olympiads to cultivate and nurture future generations
of scientifically literate citizens and science talents cannot be underestimated.

Acknowledgments This chapter has benefitted greatly from the constructive comments on earlier
drafts from Poon Chew Leng, Tan Aik Ling, Lee Yew Jin, and Tan Hong Kim. Kwek Leong
Chuan is acknowledged for providing updated information on former physics olympians.
