Challenges Facing Developing Countries
in Communicating Science to the Public

Leo Tan Wee Hin and Ramanathan Subramaniam




Abstract Developing countries face a range of challenges in communicating
science to their people. This chapter explores a selection of such challenges from
the lens of our observations and experiences, and suggests some possible foci for
attention. We focus on six key issues: weak ‘modern science’ culture, lack of or
inadequate institutional mechanisms to popularize science, inertia on the part of the
scientific community to establish scientific academies and scientific societies,
inadequate mechanisms for the dissemination of the results of scholarly scientific
activities, lackadaisical efforts in science journalism, and social factors. The chapter
concludes by arguing that for developing countries, aid that leads to capacity
building can be more effective than handouts for promoting the cause of science.




Introduction

Science and technology are agents for promoting socioeconomic development in
countries. Harnessing of these tools has allowed many countries to provide their
people with a good standard of living. With the pervasiveness of globalization and
the rapid pace at which new developments in science and technology are occurring,
many aspects of societal activities are becoming impacted by their influences.
For example, mobile phones have allowed instant connectivity across vast distances

L. Tan Wee Hin (*)
Singapore National Academy of Science, National University of Singapore, Singapore,
Singapore
e-mail: leotan@nus.edu.sg
R. Subramaniam (*)
Singapore National Academy of Science, Nanyang Technological University, Singapore,
Singapore
e-mail: subramaniam.r@nie.edu.sg

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,        213
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_13, © Springer Netherlands 2014
214                                                 L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


without the need to lay telecommunication cables, and lots of applications are
riding on this platform. With a network connection and a computer, the vast corpus
of knowledge available on the World Wide Web can be accessed.
    Many countries in the western world and in East Asia have made impressive
strides in economic development as a result of the recognition of the potential of
science and technology to aid national objectives. For example, Singapore, Japan,
Taiwan, and Hong Kong in the East as well as countries in Europe, USA, and
Canada have achieved the status of “developed nations” as a result of this, aided, of
course, by good governance and a culture that does not deride modern science.
    In the developing world, aside from a few countries, the picture is not very
encouraging. They continue to be plagued by poverty, corruption, low literacy
levels, poor infrastructure, and weak governance, all of which have contributed to
the relatively low levels of socioeconomic development. An important reason for
this state of affairs is that science and technology are not deeply embedded in the
national psyche. This is where science communication efforts can be helpful.
    The principal objective of this chapter is to explore the challenges facing
developing countries in communicating science to their people.



Challenges in Communicating Science

Developing countries face a range of problems in communicating science to their
people. These are too numerous to be covered in the limited confines of this chapter
and, accordingly, we have to be selective in what can be covered. The emphasis is
more on issues that we feel are important, based on our involvement in the field of
science communication for a few decades. We do not profess that these are the most
important issues and neither do we claim that each challenge has been explored of
its full dimensions. As we see it, there are six issues of significance, and these will
be explored here.
(a) Weak “Modern Science” Culture
   Indigenous cultures in many developing countries have a rich heritage formed by
the agglomeration of experiences of the people with the land over a long period of
time. Science is actually a part of this indigenous culture. Native ways of knowing
have spawned a wealth of traditional knowledge in many areas, and these have been
passed down in oral tradition over several generations. For example, knowledge of
the local flora and fauna has helped them to identify sources of materials for use
as food and medicine. Water management, agricultural practices, and resource
utilization have been around for a long time, and these have contributed toward
sustainable development in the land.
   With science and technology impacting society and influencing economic
development, the term “science culture” has come on board in recent times. It
can be broadly defined as the extent to which people are familiar with science as a
discipline and how it can be used productively by society and the individual.
Challenges Facing Developing Countries in Communicating Science to the Public       215


In particular, it refers to the common folk’s attitudes toward and understanding of
science (Solomon, 1996). This, in turn, is dictated by not only literacy levels but
also science literacy levels – the role of educational institutions is very important in
this respect.
   There is a perception in many developing countries that embracing a “modern
science” culture can endanger native culture to some extent. This is not true as the
experiences of countries in East Asia show that Confucian culture is still strong
even though science and technology have percolated to many aspects of societal
activities. The genesis of the misconception can probably be traced to the fact that
several countries in the developing world have been under colonial rule for a long
time and an aversion to modern science could have developed as it is commonly
seen to be western in origin. After all, the domination of the western world in
science and technology is well recognized. Nobel Laureate Abdus Salam, a keen
observer of events in the developing world, noted that science is often treated as an
adornment and perceived even as a fringe activity that needs to be tolerated. In other
words, science is seen to be peripheral to nation-building efforts. Akande (2010)
puts it across lucidly: “The biggest challenge to creating a science culture in Africa
is the widespread public perception that science is foreign-led and irrelevant to
people’s daily needs.” Though not explicitly stated, the alluding here is more to a
modern science culture.
   Thomas and Durant (1987) enumerate nine reasons why people need to know
something about science in modern society. Besides the economic reason, they
argue that science can enrich the culture of a country – after all, it does not reflect
particularly well of a country if its people are antagonistic toward science, given its
contributions to society in general. Also they argue that science enriches the
intellectual life of a country – for example, scientists and people in science-related
disciplines impact more on general scholarship and industry. Another reason they
provide is that science can enhance the democratic process in a country – this may
not particularly go down well in countries that are ruled by dictators, autocrats, and
the military as their power base may be at stake with an informed citizenry.
   Receptiveness of the potential of science and technology for economic devel-
opment and their relevance to everyday life can come about only if people are
convinced about the benefits – for example, the use of genetically modified crop
varieties to boost agricultural yields, the use of modern technologies to harvest
crops with minimal wastage, and efficient methods of rainwater collection can be
cited on how the practices of science can be grounded in a national perspective. On
the individual front can be cited the need for vaccination against infectious diseases,
the importance of personal hygiene, and the use of medication for illnesses. All
these have brought about some recognition among people in developing countries
of the benefits of science, but this has generally not been translated into enhanced
science literacy levels.
   There is thus an imperative to promote the primacy of modern science within a
cultural context in developing countries so that it can find greater resonance among
people. When science is treated as alien, the resistance to its assimilation is
pronounced. On the other hand, when science is shown to lead to personal benefits,
216                                                   L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


it can lead to better engagement with society. Promoting science within a cultural
context can also lead to the eradication of superstition – for example, the ritual
murder of albino infants in East Africa is thought to confer riches on the parents,
while sex with young children is thought to cure sufferers of AIDS and HIV. These
are testament to the extent of how deeply rooted some misconceptions are in these
societies and how delinking science from social contexts or culture can only further
perpetuate such mind-sets.
(b) Lack of Institutional Mechanisms to Popularize Science
   While formal educational institutions have the mandate to ensure that students
are schooled in a number of subjects, including science, dedicated institutions that
popularize science and technology to the masses are more effective in sensitizing
them to the way science and technology are relevant to their lives and how these can
help to improve society. Such institutions include science centers/science
museums. Science centers can help to reach out to large numbers of people, inclu-
ding the adult population, which is a key target audience of such efforts. They
popularize science through various means – for example, exhibitions on themes or
topics such as energy, AIDS, water, and environment; science enrichment programs
that complement the science taught in schools; science publications programs
such as newsletters and popular science magazines; and promotional activities
such as science camps and competitions. Owing to the multifaceted nature of
their attractions (Delacote, 1998; Persson, 2000), science centers are popular not
only with students but also adults.
   Examination of the website of the Association of Science-Technology Centers
makes for interesting reading. Almost every state in the USA has at least a science
center/science museum. All countries in Western Europe have science centers, with
some having more than one. The situation in East Asia is similar – every country
has at least a science center/science museum. Science centers in all these countries
have been registering record attendances. When it comes to developing countries,
the situation is not encouraging. While China and India have a number of science
centers, there are none in the African continent beyond South Africa, which has
close to ten science centers. This means harnessing the institutional efforts of a key
driver of science and technology for communicating science is not available in
these countries. Governments in these countries, unlike those in China, Hong Kong,
India, Japan, Singapore, and Taiwan, have still not factored in the establishment of
science centers in their thinking despite the fact that such centers have been around
for over 40 years and have proved to be effective in reaching out to the masses.
Several science centers in the USA have, however, been established by philan-
thropic and private initiatives; this is clearly not a model for those in developing
countries, where the role of government is still important. The scientific intelli-
gentsia in these countries has also been lackadaisical in their efforts to engage their
governments for the setting up of science centers.
   Actually, setting up of a science center is not difficult (Tan & Subramaniam,
1998, 2003). Land can be provided by the government for the setting up of the
science center; if this is not possible, a suitable derelict building can be converted to
Challenges Facing Developing Countries in Communicating Science to the Public     217


a science center. This has been the case for a few science centers in the developed
world. For example, the Singapore Science Centre started operating in temporary
premises and functioned there for a few years before moving to its current site.
Cookbooks for the fabrication of exhibits on a range of themes in the sciences are
available in the public domain, mainly due to the efforts of the Exploratorium in
San Francisco. Reasonably equipped wood, metal, and electrical workshops can be
used to fabricate a range of exhibits in the sciences. Also, science centers provide
employment opportunities for scientific and other staff, who can drive the activities
through various programs, such as exhibitions, science enrichment programs, and
science promotional activities. Linkages to local schools invariably get established
when a science center is established – such linkages can help to drive science
communication efforts to a higher gear as the student community is an important
segment in such dissemination efforts. For example, annually over 230,000 school
students attend the science enrichment programs conducted at the Science Centre
Singapore. By starting small, a science center can grow in size organically in
due course.
(c) Inertia on the Part of the Scientific Community to Establish Scientific Acade-
    mies and Scientific Societies
   The scientific intelligentsia in a country represents a concentration of expertise
in a number of areas. Especially in developing countries, their small numbers, when
amalgamated together, can be a formidable concentration of power which can be
wielded for the cause of science. By its very nature, scientific academies and
scientific societies allow scientists to come together for a common cause that can
find expression in altruistic objectives (Tan and Subramaniam, 1999, 2009b). Since
these are professional societies, they do not report to the government and can chart
their own course of action. The early experiences of the Royal Society (Hunter,
1989) and other academies in Europe show that a lot can be done for the promotion
of science under the aegis of these platforms, including promoting science to the
masses through various means with a voluntary cadre.
   It took quite some time for scientific academies and scientific societies to strike
roots in developing countries. Especially in the African continent, countries have
been slow to come on board these platforms. It is worth reiterating that Nature
editor, Dr Philip Campbell, in a recent opinion piece, expressed optimism that “the
development of scientific academies could help to put science to work in Africa”
(Campbell, 2006).
   We explore more of the role of learned societies for the cause of science
communication in another chapter.
(d) Inadequate Mechanisms for Dissemination of Results of Scholarly Scientific
    Activities
   The journal is the most important vehicle for the dissemination of scholarly
activity by the scientific community. Results of research find primary expression in
these platforms and constitute a form of science communication to specialist
audiences. The emphasis given to scientific research can be seen from the
218                                                    L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


multiplicity and diversity of journals catering to many aspects of scholarly
endeavors, so much so that impact factors and ranking indicators have to be used
to differentiate them. While international journals, especially those published in the
western world, still command prestige and recognition, there is a need for a
proliferation of national journals in diverse scientific disciplines to spur scholarly
efforts by the scientific community in each developing country. If submissions only
to international journals are embarked on, then very few papers will ever be
published. Scholarly efforts by scientists in developing countries will thus be
significantly affected. There is thus a need to nurture local talent in the sciences,
and this is where national journals can help to promote the spirit of research. For
example, research on the local flora and fauna may have difficulties finding support
in international journals, given the competition for print space and high rejection
rates. National journals are thus ideal platforms for such research. Quite a lot of
fundamental research can still be done using the limited facilities and manpower
available in universities.
   The rise of national journals in many developing countries is a welcome devel-
opment for scientific research. It promotes indigenous enterprise in a number of
scholarly disciplines. For example, inspection of the number of journals indexed in
www.ajol.info shows that the number of journals published in the broad field of
science and technology in the African continent is impressive (Table 1). The
423 journals are impressive as a gauge of scholarly scientific activity and are an
indication that research is being recognized for building up capacity. It can be seen
that there is a preponderance of journals catering to health but the numbers devoted
to the hard sciences are rather modest. Given that the African continent has
54 sovereign countries, the number of science-related journals works out to an
average of about eight per country. It could be an indication that research activities
in science and technology are not up to the desired levels. If one factors in the
publication frequency and the number of pages allocated for each issue of the
journals, it is clear that this is not a satisfactory state of affairs. In fact, it is very
important for every developing country to have a range of journals in each scientific
discipline and which comes out regularly so as to spur scholarly activity in the
sciences and provide platforms for their scientists to achieve national recognition.
In due course, quality will improve – this is how all journals started! While the
current thinking in the scientific community lays inordinate emphasis on publi-
cations in international journals, we feel that an array of national journals in various
disciplines in developing countries is still very important to promote a culture of
publication among their scientists as well as spur scholarly activity in the sciences.
The transition to publication of work in international journals can come in
due course.
   A possible reason why the number of journals is not up to the desired mark could
be the emphasis on the hard copy, with the attendant printing and distribution costs.
Web-based journals may be the answer since page length as well as printing and
distribution costs are less of an issue. In fact, with the growing emphasis on open
access journals, this model can help the results of scholarly activity reach out to
large target audiences as compared to the print version. This is an option worth
Challenges Facing Developing Countries in Communicating Science to the Public            219

Aquatic sciences                                                             8

pursuing for developing countries and one that can promote a fillip to scholarly
scientific activity.
   Robust publication activity is essential for scientists to build up a portfolio of
credits that can inspire confidence when communicating science to the public and to
promote the spirit of science. National journals do help to contribute toward this in
developing countries.
(e) Lackadaisical Efforts in Science Journalism
   More than any other platform, science journalism offers immense scope to
communicate science to the masses. Such forms of journalism are traditionally
accomplished via newspapers, television, radio, and popular science magazines
and, in more recent times, via the Web. The reach of the mass media is wide, and it
can be harnessed for communicating important messages and news to the public.
As it is, politics, sports, and other developments tend to push science to the
background, so much so that they have not really been given the necessary space
and opportunity to realize their full potential for communicating science to the
public. The situation is exacerbated by the lack of reporters trained in science
journalism and the absence of such programs in the local institutes of higher
learning in developing countries. Aggravating the problem is the low literacy levels
in many developing countries, which means that messages about science are
unlikely to find the necessary resonance in these people.
   The situation has, however, improved over the years. Newspapers in China and
India feature science content and analyses routinely. Some newspapers in Africa
now feature science on a regular basis – for example, the Daily Nation and the
Standard in Kenya, This Day in Nigeria, and the Ghana News Agency. Popular
magazines such as the monthly Science in Africa are now reaching out to a good
number of people. International efforts have also helped in this regard – for
example, the World Federation of Science Journalists has been conducting online
mentoring since 2006 to help journalists in Africa develop careers in science
220                                                  L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam


reporting through its program called SjCOOP, while the Association of British
Science Writers has been working with the Uganda Science Journalists Association
to institute an annual prize that recognizes science writing in the press. Such efforts
are commendable as they contribute to capacity building in science communication
in developing countries.
    With the ubiquity of the mobile phone in modern society, penetration rates are
increasing even in developing countries. Internet connectivity via landlines still
lags behind mobile phone penetration. Given the infrastructural bottlenecks that
impede the laying of landlines for Internet access across vast geographical distances
in many developing countries and the convergence of the Internet with the mobile
platform, tremendous scope is now afforded for accessing resources (including
those related to science) on the World Wide Web via mobile telephony. In a report
prepared by Lyons, Philips, Valdés-Valdivieso, and Penteriani (2012), it was noted
that in a number of countries in Africa, web-based access is predominantly via the
mobile phone – for example, in Zimbabwe, it is 58.1 %; in Nigeria, it is 57.9 %; and
in Zambia, it is 44.0 %. This contrasts with the global average of 10 %. The
implications of these observations hold tremendous promise for the cause of
science communication via science journalism in Africa as now the potential
outreach afforded is enormous as compared to newspapers or even television.
Quite a number of applications related to science can, in fact, ride on the mobile
platform and successful applications are available. In India, for example, science
communication via mobile phones has been effective in reaching out to target
audiences via the Science@Mobile initiative of the National Centre for Innovations
in Distance Education – for instance, promoting health awareness and environmen-
tal literacy to common folks as well as weather information and agricultural
fundamentals to farmers.
(f) Social Factors
   A range of social issues plague many developing countries in communicating
science to the people. For example, poverty, famine, corruption, infant mortality,
violence, and political instability often sap the will of the people, so much so that
they are not able to aspire for a higher standard of living. It is a challenge promoting
science to them. Developed countries, by and large, have been able to overcome
these problems significantly, and science and technology are now very much a part
of the country’s ethos. Yet, it is science and technology that can enable people in
developing countries to address challenges related to infant mortality and other
health-care issues as well as famine. For example, empowering farmers with new
ideas that can improve crop yields, educating women about steps that can be taken
to reduce infant mortality, use of information and communication technologies to
enhance governance, protection of biodiversity, and conservation of natural
resources are issues that can be addressed under the ambit of science communi-
cation in developing countries. Efforts by the mainstream media in these areas have
been lackluster at best for a long time. It is only in more recent times that some
improvements are being seen.
Challenges Facing Developing Countries in Communicating Science to the Public                221


Discussion

We have examined six issues that affect the state of science communication in
developing countries as well as how these could be addressed to some extent.
As mentioned earlier, the choice of issues is lensed from our observations and
experiences in this area over the past few decades. It is clear that the level of science
communication in many developing countries is far from satisfactory, especially
those in the African continent, though progress can be seen. Among all developing
countries, it seems that countries in the African continent can benefit the most from
science communication initiatives.
    Many developing countries are the recipients of largesse from international aid
agencies and countries in the developed world. We have consistently argued that
aid which leads to capacity building, as opposed to handouts and bailouts, is more
sustainable in the long run as otherwise it can lead to a dependency mind-set (Tan &
Subramaniam, 1998, 2009a). Quite often development aid does not go to the
intended recipients owing to corruption and infrastructural bottlenecks. What
developing countries need is aid that can be used to empower their citizenry. For
example, scholarships, fellowships, and training programs in science communica-
tion for students in developing countries that are tenable in the developed world can
help to improve science communication when they return back to their countries
after graduation. Likewise, aid that goes towards the setting up of science centers is
of more relevance. Science centers have proved to be effective platforms for
communicating science, and helping to set these up in developing countries can
lead to long-term benefits for the people, including capacity building and catalyzing
the promotion of a modern science culture. Financial aid can also be given to
scientific academies and scientific societies for organizing programs that reach out
to the community rather than for the setting up of premises. In the case of the latter,
recurrent expenditure on premises is an issue, and funds may not be forthcoming in
the future from the aid programs.
    The challenges of addressing science communication inadequacies in develop-
ing countries are complex, but these can be addressed if there is the will of the
political and scientific community. It is a task worth undertaking as it can help
developing countries achieve resilience besides contributing to capacity building.
More importantly, it needs to leverage on multiple platforms in order to be effective.

                          Part IV
General Communication Initiatives
Web-Based Channels for Science
Communication

Karen Bultitude




Abstract Web-based communications are crucial tools for engaging public audi-
ences with science across the world, with their influence especially increasing
within the Asia-Pacific region. This chapter provides an overview of the main
issues associated with using online channels, as well as a brief introduction to the
key existing platforms (wikis and blogs, podcasts and videos, social media and
Citizen Science). Case studies, ‘top tips’ and recommended resources are scattered
throughout the text in order to provide practical examples and sound advice for
people intending to implement such tools for their own science communication
activities. The chapter ends with important guidance relevant across all platforms:
how to design an effective communication strategy, search engine optimisation
(SEO) and tools for evaluating and monitoring success.




Introduction

   I think there is a world market for maybe five computers. (IBM Chairman Thomas Watson,
   1943)

   Making predictions about technological developments is always risky. Software
changes, new online communication channels arise, and old ones lose their popu-
larity. However, for anyone interested in communicating science via web-based
means, there are various issues and trends that are likely to remain critical factors
for the foreseeable future. How can we judge the reliability of online information,
given the plethora of content available? Who does (and does not) have access to
online activities – an important consideration lest we unintentionally discriminate


K. Bultitude (*)
Department of Science and Technology Studies, University College London, London, UK
e-mail: karen.bultitude@ucl.ac.uk

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,          225
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_14, © Springer Netherlands 2014
226                                                                             K. Bultitude


against those on the other side of the ‘digital divide’. There are also more subtle
factors relating to disparities in the extent to which different people will actively
contribute to online communities and opportunities for ‘niche’ audiences to be
more effectively reached via online media.
   Each of these issues will be briefly considered within this chapter, followed by
an outline of key existing web-based channels for science communication. Blogs,
podcasts and vodcasts are all adapted versions of more traditional ‘broadcast’
media types, representing a shift from centralised information sources to user-
generated online material. Social media (especially Facebook, Twitter and other
microblogging platforms) and wikis enable rapid content sharing and communica-
tion. Citizen Science offers the potential for a deeper and longer-term engagement
between public groups and scientific content through collaborative effort.
   It has of course not been possible to go into detail on every aspect of web-based
science communication channels within this chapter; the intention is to provide an
overview of key issues and important media, with links to relevant websites and
resources provided at the end of each section. The chapter concludes with top tips
for successful communication via web-based channels, including advice on design-
ing a communication strategy, search engine optimisation and tools for evaluating
and monitoring success.



Asia-Pacific Context

As might be expected within so diverse a region, Internet penetration (the propor-
tion of the population that uses the Internet) varies greatly within the Asia-Pacific.
Within the 21 APEC (Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation) member states, the
average penetration rate is 57 %; however, Australia, New Zealand and Canada
are all at 83 % or above, whilst Indonesia, the Philippines, Thailand and Vietnam
have less than 35 % of their populations using the Internet, and Papua New Guinea
approximately 2 % (Internet World Stats, 2012). In notable contrast to much of the
rest of the world, the mobile broadband penetration is significantly higher than fixed
broadband penetration within APEC member states (APEC, 2011). Other key
observable trends are highlighted within the box below.1




1
  Further information about key trends, policies and developments in individual Asia-Pacific
countries is available from http://www.digital-review.org/about-us/project.html
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                       227



   Current Trends Regarding Broadband Penetration in APEC
   Countries

   • Within most APEC developing countries, mobile services are crucial to
     achieving coverage.
   • A higher density of population within a country is likely to lead to higher
     broadband penetration.
   • Broadband is ‘affordable’ in most APEC economies where the cost is
     below the threshold of 4.05 % annual GDP spending per capita; above this
     threshold, the cost becomes prohibitive and significantly affects take-up.


   From a science communication perspective, the signs are positive for being able
to access large portions of the population via web-based channels: Internet usage is
strong in many Asia-Pacific countries and growing in the remainder. In particular,
APEC-based Internet users rate using the Internet for ‘knowledge and planning’ and
‘personal interest’ as the third and fourth most important functions (after ‘emailing’
and ‘social networking’ and before aspects common in other regions such as
‘gaming’; ‘news, weather and sport’ and ‘shopping’ (APEC, 2011)). Placing such
importance on these functions suggests that Internet users within the Asia-Pacific
are potentially willing and interested in engaging with web-based channels for
science communication; the task now is to ensure that such initiatives are high
quality and succeed in attracting those audiences. The remainder of this chapter
provides advice on how to achieve this most effectively, as well as key issues to be
taken into account when designing web-based initiatives.



Overview of Current Trends
This section explores five key issues in using web-based channels that may poten-
tially impact upon the effectiveness of your science communication initiatives.
Initially, we consider who does (and doesn’t) use online media, as well as how such
users are likely to judge the credibility of any sites they visit. Depth of interaction
amongst users varies, with some acting merely as ‘lurkers’, whilst others take a
more active role. Finally, we consider the opportunities presented through the rise
of ‘niche’ audiences.



The Digital Divide
The ‘digital divide’ is usually taken to refer to a disparity regarding access to the
Internet. This may be due to financial or socioeconomic factors (e.g. the poorer
parts of society cannot afford their own computer), but can also relate to provision
of infrastructure such as reliable satellite connections or broadband networks,
228                                                                                  K. Bultitude


which affect a broader population (van Dijk, 2005). For example, despite its
affluent status, Australia’s vast geographic size has limited regular Internet use in
some rural areas, although the intended National Broadband Network will ‘provide
minimum speeds of 100 Mbps to 93 % of Australian households by 2018’ (World
Internet Project, 2012, p. 19).
    As first noted by Hargittai (2002), there is however a second-level digital divide
relating to skills rather than access: people may physically be able to sit at a
computer connected to the Internet but may have little idea how to use it effectively.
Indeed, within APEC, there are three factors recognised as contributing to the
digital divide: accessibility, affordability and technology awareness/skill (APEC,
2011). Finding ways to support development of such skills is therefore just as
important as providing affordable physical access to web technologies in overcom-
ing the digital divide2 (van Deursen & van Dijk, 2010).
    Globally, there have been various demographic factors identified that affect the
likelihood of Internet use, with gender (men more frequently use the Internet than
women), age (Internet use increases as age decreases) and income level (higher
incomes generally indicating greater take-up) being the most prominent (World
Internet Project, 2012). More detailed research within America has also suggested
that ethnicity, education and geographic location are also important predictors of
who does or does not access the Internet (Baym, 2010; Zickuhr & Smith, 2012).
Furthermore, how long an individual has been using the Internet also naturally
affects their skills and confidence (Hargittai, 2002).



Reliability and Trust of Online Content
Whilst web-based channels offer huge potential for science communication, there
are also major problems that arise, most notably how ‘legitimate’ sources make
themselves heard above the ‘noise’ of other providers. In part this is a matter of
effective management of the web channel in question (see, e.g. ‘Search Engine
Optimisation’ at the end of this chapter), but it is also an issue relating to how – or
even if! – users judge the credibility of online sources.
   In an extensive review of the issue, Metzger (2007) identifies five criteria from
the wider literature for assessing the credibility of Internet-based information:
accuracy, authority, objectivity, currency and coverage or scope. However, in
user tests, it was clear that few if any users applied all of these criteria with any
rigour. In some ways, this is again an issue of ‘web literacy’ – the importance of
educating users to be critical about the sites they are viewing – but it does also
provide a useful framework if you are developing your own site and wish to ensure
that it appears credible to external users.




2
    See Section 7 of APEC (2011) for more details of specific policy recommendations in this area.
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                                    229


Participation Inequality
A key factor to recognise when working with online communities is that not all
participants will contribute equally. This issue was quantified in 2006 by Jakob
Nielsen who identified the ‘90:9:1 rule’:
    In most online communities, 90 % of users are lurkers who never contribute, 9 % of users
    contribute a little, and 1 % of users account for almost all the action. (Nielsen, 2006)

    As Nielsen himself notes, the proportions shift a little depending on precisely
what medium you are interested in (e.g. blogs and wikis tend to have a higher
participation inequality), but as a general rule of thumb, the 90:9:1 rule has proven
remarkably adept at delineating the major ‘participation’ groups across almost
every type of web-based channel. Depending on the intention of your communica-
tion, this can have dire consequences, the most notable of which is that any
feedback you receive is unlikely to be representative of your user group. Instead,
it will be dominated by a small minority of users. Fortunately, there are a number of
tips that you can follow to encourage wider involvement, for example, rewarding
participants and promoting quality contributions.3



The ‘Long Tail’ Effect
Online media have undergone three major changes in recent years which have
dramatically affected their reach (especially in comparison to more traditional
media formats (Metzger, 2007)):
• Decreased costs
• Increased access to information production (e.g. user-generated content)
• Increased dissemination
   Audience targeting has from the start been a cornerstone of effective science
communication (Bultitude, 2011); the above developments in online media have
meant that it is now more feasible to target multiple smaller ‘niche’ audiences, each
in slightly different ways, rather than a single undefined ‘general public’. The
overheads and/or effort involved in reaching specific audiences now present
much lower barriers, thereby allowing science communicators to slightly adjust
their message/content to different audience groups. Chris Anderson refers to this
phenomenon as the ‘long tail effect’, where
    The mass market is turning into a mass of niches. That mass of niches has always existed,
    but as the cost of reaching it falls [. . .] it’s suddenly becoming a cultural and economic
    force to be reckoned with. (Anderson, 2008)




3
 See Nielsen (2006) or Kraut and Resnick (2012) for a more complete list of advice – pre-edited
copies of the latter book chapters are available for free from http://kraut.hciresearch.org/content/
books
230                                                                                   K. Bultitude


   Anderson’s classic example of this effect comes from marketing but is equally
applicable to science communication:


   Question: What percentage of the top 10,000 titles in any online media store
   (iTunes, Amazon, etc.) will sell at least once a month?
   Hint: in traditional media 20 % of products account for 80 % of sales. . .
   Answer: 98%!


    The implication is that we need to be very aware of the multiplicity of different
‘niche’ audiences for online science communication. They offer great potential for
tapping into audience interests and motivations in an effective and resource-
efficient manner, resulting in a greater overall reach for our work.

   Case Study: Successfully Reaching Multiple Audiences
   Accessing several different audiences may be achieved in a variety of ways:
   at one extreme, back in 1998, NASA set up a completely separate website for
   elementary school pupils (ages 5–10 years). The design and written text
   within The Space Place is produced specifically for that age group, and as
   the team behind it explain:
      We wanted to reach this young audience with the message that science and tech-
      nology and learning about space are fun and within their grasp. We designed it with
      a kid-friendly look and feel. It is modular, so visitors can pick and choose different
      standalone projects or activities that interest them at the moment. (NASA, n.d.)

       Although the content and subject matter are very similar to NASA’s main
   site (http://www.nasa.gov), the explicit targeting of a particular demographic
   has resulted in strong success in attracting and retaining that age group: in
   2011, the site was hosting around 14,000 daily visits (NASA, n.d.). Multiple
   language options also encourage wider take-up: The Space Place has addi-
   tionally been translated into Spanish in recognition of the high strong His-
   panic population within America.
       On the other hand, it is also possible to successfully incorporate multiple
   audiences within a single site. The most obvious way to do this is to delineate
   between different ‘modes’ of involvement, for example, the main menu on
   the website for Science Centre Singapore (http://www.science.edu.sg/) offers
   clearly defined sections for ‘visitors’ (mainly families and tourists), ‘schools’
   and ‘community’ (linked closely to their wider social media efforts). Another
   approach is to divide the content according to subject matter, thereby
   allowing the site users to self-select which areas are of greatest interest to
   them and ignore the sections that they would otherwise find tedious. The
   SciBlogs site from New Zealand (http://sciblogs.co.nz) uses this strategy to

                                                                                     (continued)
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                        231



     reach multiple audiences with slightly differing interests by clearly highlight-
     ing blog post categories covering ‘science’, ‘agriculture’, ‘technology’,
     ‘health and medicine’, ‘environment and ecology’ or ‘science and society’.
     Thus, a visitor to the site does not have to wade through posts about ‘health
     and medicine’ to find content about ‘agriculture’ and vice versa.


Existing Current Platforms
‘Wikis’, ‘blogs’, ‘podcasts’ (and even ‘vodcasts’), ‘social media’, ‘Citizen Sci-
ence’, the list is endless. Web-based communication is full of buzzwords and jargon
that can be confusing at best and daunting at worst. This section aims to clarify what
each of these terms mean and highlight why they are relevant for science commu-
nication purposes. Of course this list is not exhaustive – mainstream Internet
activities such as standard website development, online gaming, ‘mashups’ (com-
binations of multiple media types) and mobile phone ‘apps’ (applications) can also
be successfully harnessed for science communication purposes, though unfortu-
nately due to space limitations such approaches are not outlined here. However, as
emphasised later in this chapter, it is important to consider each ‘channel’ as one
element in a coherent communication strategy, working together – often with
different audiences – to achieve your goals.



Resources
In keeping with the spirit of this section, you may also find it useful to visit Cindy
Kendall’s public wiki.4 This site provides a comparative overview of the main
features of blogs, wikis and podcasts, including entertaining and useful videos
introducing each of these three types of web channels, and suggested resources
for creating your own.
   For some excellent practical advice on implementing various web-based chan-
nels, visit the International Cancer Information Services Group (ICISG).5 This
resource is highly applicable to most science communication situations and pro-
vides a wealth of knowledge on a wide range of topics.


Wikis and Blogs
A wiki (a variation of the Hawaiian word for ‘quick’) is simply a website that is
designed to be collaboratively contributed to and edited by multiple users (Klobas
& Beesley, 2006). Usually these start as private documents with a specific common


4
    http://ckendall.wikispaces.com/Blogs+and+Wikis+Overview
5
    http://www.icisg.org/resources/socialmedia8.html
232                                                                              K. Bultitude


purpose shared within the group, such as preparing a draft experimental protocol or
pooling knowledge about a specific topic. However, in many cases, the content
within successful wikis is recognised as being of use more broadly and is made
public – the most obvious case being Wikipedia. However, wikis have rarely taken
off to the same extent as other web-based channels, perhaps because they are so
reliant on goodwill and input from all the collaborators.
   By contrast, blogs (short for ‘weblogs’) are more like an online diary, collating
together reactions, musings, user-created content and useful weblinks from a
variety of sites, all spiced with the author’s own opinions and interpretations
(Rettberg, 2008). Blogs are usually presented in reverse chronological order, with
the most recent entry (‘post’) at the top of the page and older ones underneath.
Comment sections enable readers to provide their own opinions on the post’s
content, providing some manner of two-way communication between audience
and author.
   Within certain countries, there is evidence that 30 % or more of Internet users
read blogs at least monthly (World Internet Project, 2012). According to Technorati
(a popular English-language blog ranking service), there were more than 12,600
active science blogs at the end of 2012 (Technorati, 2012). These were written by
both professional (where the authors are paid to blog for a wider organisation) and
amateur bloggers, some written by teams of authors and others by individuals.
   Recent research has noted that intrinsic (personal) motivations are the main
factors that encourage people to blog about science: in terms of the ‘long tail’ noted
above, these science bloggers generally focus on smaller niche audiences already
interested in science to some extent (Ranger & Bultitude, under review). There is
also evidence that some scientists take up blogging in order to ‘circumvent’
traditional media and gain more control of how their work is publicised (Colson,
2011).
   Creating a blog is a very straightforward process requiring only a few minutes’
effort using one of the many existing free platforms. Most bloggers find writing
regular posts both cathartic and rewarding – it gives you a chance to comment on
matters of interest to you but can also be beneficial in terms of raising your profile
and gaining wider attention for your work. Suzi Gage (winner of the UK Good
Thinking Society’s UK Science Blog Prize 2012) emphasises that blogging is an
important skill for all scientific researchers, and especially PhD students.6

   Top Tips on Maintaining a Successful Science Blog
   Research has shown that the following factors are very important in attracting
   and retaining audiences to your science blog (Ranger & Bultitude, under
   review):

                                                                                (continued)


6
  http://www.scilogs.com/sifting_the_evidence/why-phd-students-should-blog-my-talk-at-uk-sci
ence-blog-prize-evening-also-i-won/
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                                   233



    • Update your blog frequently (at least weekly if possible).
    • Take the time to incorporate plenty of non-text elements (especially
      images and video).
    • Draw upon a wide range of ‘current’ topics likely to be of interest to wider
      groups to make your content relevant.
       There is also some evidence that word count and level of reading difficulty
    within each post can affect readership rates, although fundamentally, if
    people are interested in your blog, then these are less important factors.


Podcasts and Videos
As described by Berry (2006, p. 144), a podcast is ‘any audio-content downloaded
from the Internet either manually from a website or automatically via software
applications’. This can include dedicated feeds which you can set your computer to
automatically download or items that are web-streamed in real time. The same is
true for video, though the term ‘vodcast’ (video podcast) never really took off, and
we shall instead refer simply to ‘online videos’. To make content easier for users to
access, search and use, large hosting platforms have developed which congregate
large numbers of files together; iTunes and YouTube are two of the most ubiquitous
globally, though there are others.7
    The great advantage of both podcasts and online videos is that they are
extremely cheap and easy to create – no licence or studio is required. Indeed
most people with a computer or recent model mobile phone will already have
access to the basic technology required. They also give your audience much greater
control than standard radio and television: they can choose what to listen to and in
what order and also have control over the time and place at which they access the
recordings.
    Certainly the evidence is that use of both podcasts and videos is on the rise:
global figures aren’t available; however, a detailed investigation within America
concluded in 2010 that ‘The percentage of Americans who have ever watched or
listened to a podcast is 45 %’ (Webster, 2010) and that user profiles fairly closely
resembled mainstream media consumers. Likewise, YouTube’s self-reported sta-
tistics indicate that in 2011, the site attracted over 800 million unique users per
month, spread across 43 countries and 60 languages (YouTube, 2012). In designing
your content, it is worth catering for mobile/smartphone audiences: in the case of
YouTube, traffic from mobile devices tripled in 2011 (YouTube).
    There have also been some notable successes in using YouTube to engage
audiences with scientific topics. At the time of writing, there are currently


7
  On this note, it is worth making sure your audience can access the host platform you select – for
example, some schools and other institutions deliberately choose to block YouTube sites, meaning
teachers and pupils can’t access them.
234                                                                       K. Bultitude


61 ‘science and education’ channels on YouTube, with Smarter Every Day8 (one of
the most popular) having over 500,000 subscribers and more than 34 million video
views.
   It is beyond the scope of this chapter to go into technical detail about how to
create and host your own podcast or online video channel; there are plenty of
manuals, websites and even integrated help and advice functions within most
modern computers to assist you with those elements. However, the advice in the
box – developed by Malcolm Love, a former Senior Producer at the British
Broadcasting Corporation (BBC) – is designed to assist you in creating effective
and interesting podcasts and videos about scientific topics.

     Top Tips on Designing Podcasts and Videos

     • Determine to have fun doing it! Your audience will enjoy it more if you are
       clearly enthusiastic.
     • Be clear about what you want to achieve.
     • Make sure every item in your podcast/video has a clear purpose.
     • Always think from the point of view of the listener/viewer. This implies
       that you have a reasonably clear idea of the people you aim to reach.
     • Even if you aim to reach people with specialist knowledge, remember that
       you can’t cram too much information into too small a space. It’s like
       serving food at a dinner party. Make it palatable, interesting to look at,
       pleasingly presented and in a reasonable quantity.
     • Keep the structure simple.
     • Keep the items simple and gradually build your skills of editing and
       mixing. Again, stay focused on the experience for the listener/viewer.
     • Practise interviewing, presenting and running the show, etc.
     • Listen critically to other podcasts. What works? What doesn’t? Why?
     • It’s likely that your team will have come together as enthusiasts or as
       co-workers interested in the project. This means that your skills will be
       diverse. Try to use the skills within the team. Some people will be great
       ‘on air’, others good at organising, editing, researching etc. Involve
       everyone.
     • Get feedback. Learn, revise, adapt.
                                            Malcolm Love, Splendid Thing Media


Social Media
The term ‘social media’ broadly refers to ‘Internet services where the online
content is generated by the users of the service’ (Cann, Dimitriou, & Hooley,
2011, p. 7). The definition is somewhat loose – certainly it is usually taken to


8
    http://www.youtube.com/SmarterEveryDay
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                                   235


include social networking (sites that enable you to connect with other people, such
as Facebook) and microblogging (platforms for posting very short updates, such as
Twitter), but some users also count blogging and even podcasting as social media.
Such media are increasingly crucial channels of web-based communication within
many sectors of society, from business to education. Globally, Facebook and
Twitter reliably rank within the top 10 most active sites on the Internet, with an
estimated 44 % and 8 %, respectively, of global Internet users logging in at least
once a week (Alexa.com, 2013). There are of course other additional services (often
with slightly different functionality) that are popular in specific regions or coun-
tries, for example, Douban, Friendster, Multiply.com, Ning and Foursquare across
most parts of the Asia-Pacific or QQ and Renren in China (Chappell, 2012). It is
also important to note that different demographic groups use different platforms –
so if possible, talk to representatives of your intended audience to find out what they
use or look into the demographic user profiles of the different options.9
    Crucially, social media are very rarely stand-alone communication efforts; they
work in tandem with other science communication endeavours to assist you in
reaching out to (and building) your community. They assist greatly with the
immediate dissemination of news or continuing of conversations, but due to their
short duration and length (e.g. 140 character limits for messages or ‘tweets’ in
Twitter), they are unlikely to provide deeper engagement.
    There are a wide range of resources, advice manuals and guides regarding how
best to utilise social media, many of which are provided in the list of weblinks at the
end of this section. A summary of key tips for success is also included in the box.


   Top Tips for Social Media Success

   • Be clear about who you’re trying to reach (and why!).
   • Make it interactive – the whole point is to get your community involved.
   • Relationships need nurturing; don’t expect too much too soon, but it’s also
     important to sustain your profile/communications or your audience will
     move onto something else.
   • Evaluate your social media efforts regularly and reflect on how they could
     be improved.

percentages rather than raw numbers which can make them hard to compare. Carlos (n.d.) also
provides a useful review of tools to assist in analysing web traffic.

  Case Study: The Importance of Personality in Social Media
  One of the most successful early science communication efforts within social
  media was @MarsPhoenix, the Twitter username for the Phoenix Mars
  Lander. Key to its success was the personal and enthusiastic nature of the
  messages, all appearing to come directly from the Lander itself, thus engen-
  dering a perception of it being a warm and engaging personality. For exam-
  ple, one of the most remarkable updates arrived on the day the Lander
  appeared to find the first evidence of water ice on Mars (https://twitter.com/
  MarsPhoenix/status/839088619):
      Are you ready to celebrate? Well, get ready: We have ICE!!!!! Yes, ICE, *WATER
      ICE* on Mars! w00t!!! Best day ever!!

     However, this personality-driven approach was in many ways accidental.
  As the Lander was approaching Mars in 2008, staff at the Jet Propulsion Lab
  decided to share the excitement via what was then a relatively new medium
  for science communication – Twitter. However, the limitation of only
  140 characters per tweet meant it soon became obvious that it was unfeasible

                                                                             (continued)
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                                    237



   to describe the Lander’s activities in the third person. As noted by the member
   of staff responsible for the tweets:
       The driving force on the first person thing was the 140 characters. . . I would write
       one day ‘the space craft has traveled’ and it took up half my space, so then I started
       to cut out words. Finally I cut out the spacecraft and said, ‘I’. (Madrigal, 2008)

      The personal approach is now recognised more widely as being key to
   encouraging interest and involvement via social media. In his book Organi-
   sations Don’t Tweet – People Do, Euan Semple has noted that in the Internet
   age institutional-style communication is not enough. Online audiences are
   used to dealing with ‘networks of individuals, each with their voice’ (Semple,
   2012, p. 5). In such environments ‘official’ voices can appear forced and
   unauthentic, lacking the richness and empathic connection achieved through
   taking on more of a character.
      The success of the personality-driven approach has now been extended
   throughout the world. Examples in science communication include other
   major scientific infrastructures such as @SkyMapper (a telescope based in
   Siding Spring, Australia) to over 68 separate museum objects around the
   world tweeting as personalities (https://twitter.com/oisinthedeer/tweeting-
   museum-objects/members).



Citizen Science
Citizen Science projects quite literally involve public ‘citizens’ directly in the
scientific process. Such projects utilise the excellent pattern recognition skills,
enthusiasm, geographic spread and sheer time contributions of public volunteers
in order to solve complex tasks that otherwise would not be possible (Hand, 2010).
However, as Mroz (2011) notes, it is crucial that such projects work as a partnership
between the researchers and the citizens, rather than merely ‘free labour’ for the
scientists involved. Haklay (2011) suggests a four-tiered hierarchy, with each tier
representing a ‘deeper’ level of public involvement (figure here adapted from the
original)10:

Zooniverse https://www.zooniverse.org; FoldIt http://fold.it/portal/; Christmas Bird Count http://
birds.audubon.org/christmas-bird-count; GLOBE http://globe.gov/about-globe.

    Haklay’s deepest level of involvement (Extreme Citizen Science or ExCiteS) is
particularly relevant in an Asia-Pacific context since it is deliberately designed to
overcome previous criticisms that Citizen Science is only accessible to relatively
affluent, educated citizens and that most participants interact in a relatively passive
manner, without any ‘ownership’ over the direction of the work. ExCiteS therefore
involves applying such approaches to critical areas of biodiversity conservation in
developing countries, led by the very people who are most affected by the local
environment. The box provides a summary of important considerations to take into
account when designing your own Citizen Science project.

   Key Factors to Identify When Designing a Citizen Science Project

   • Main purpose: is the intention to achieve a specific scientific output or
     encourage broad audience engagement?
   • Level of skill required: the best CS projects are challenging yet not
     off-putting for users.
   • What rewards/incentives/motivations are offered: e.g. Stardust@home
     offers ‘finders’ the opportunity to name their particle as well as
     co-authorship recognition in any paper published based on their results.
   • Quality control: there must be a process in place to ensure that the data/
     analysis gathered is accurate, e.g. bias checking in Galaxy Zoo by photo-
     graphically reversing the images, etc.


Resources
Bonney et al. (2009) have developed a model for designing a Citizen Science
project, whilst the Citizen Science Central website provides a dedicated toolkit
with plenty of practical advice: www.birds.cornell.edu/citscitoolkit/toolkit. For
projects specifically relating to biodiversity and the environment, the guide at
http://www.ceh.ac.uk/news/news_archive/documents/GuideToCitizenScience_
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                               239


Version2_InteractiveWeb.pdf will be particularly useful. Silvertown (2009) pro-
vides an overview of different project types as well as some of the motivating
factors for scientists to get involved in running Citizen Science programmes.

   Case Study: Public Participation in Environmental and Ecological
   Monitoring Through Citizen Science
   Since the inception of the annual Audubon Christmas Bird Count in 1900
   (http://birds.audubon.org/about-christmas-bird-count), Citizen Science activ-
   ities have been particularly well suited to scrutinising the natural world. The
   advent of web communication has dramatically enhanced these opportunities
   at all levels, including local, regional, national and international. Some events
   run as focused one-off activities designed to both collect scientific data as
   well as raise awareness of a particular ecological issue, such as the Great
   Koala Count (http://koalacount.ala.org.au). This event incorporated a mobile
   phone app to encourage people within the state of South Australia to register
   koala populations in their local area. Using an app was key to the project’s
   success – the data collection relied on accurate knowledge of the contribu-
   tor’s GPS coordinates, which could be easily determined via the phone’s
   communication with the data satellites, but was not information that most
   public participants would otherwise have had access to. Smartphone technol-
   ogy also provides the option for taking photos, meaning that (once uploaded)
   accurate verification of the species involved in the sightings can be obtained.
   Over 1,300 submissions were received from more than 1,000 people,
   representing a significant advance in knowledge regarding koala populations
   within the state (Williamson, 2012).
       Smartphone technology has also paved the way for longer-term Citizen
   Science projects acting on a global scale. For example, Project Noah is ‘a tool
   to explore and document wildlife and a platform to harness the power of
   citizen scientists everywhere’ (http://www.projectnoah.org). Like the Great
   Koala Count, Project Noah relies in the main on satellite technologies and
   photographic documentation to monitor ‘thousands of organisms from around
   the world’. The site goes further, however, encouraging the development of a
   ‘community’ of participants. Such participants contribute to and/or identify
   ‘missions’ in order to focus efforts (at both local and global levels) as well as
   opportunities to view – and help to identify – organisms contributed by other
   members. There is also a strong integration of social-media style activities,
   with prominent tags noting how many ‘spottings’, ‘missions’, ‘comments’,
   ‘suggestions’ and ‘favourites’ each member has contributed. The integration
   of digital technologies with high quality ecological information and graphics
   helps the site achieve its aims of:
      mobilizing a new generation of nature explorers and helping people from around the
      world appreciate their local wildlife. Our community is harnessing the power and

                                                                                 (continued)
240                                                                                   K. Bultitude



      popularity of new mobile technologies to collect important ecological data and help
      preserve global biodiversity (Project Noah, n.d.)

      Web communications have also proven essential in Citizen Science activ-
  ities at times of environmental crisis. In the aftermath of the Fukushima
  nuclear disaster, Japanese residents were understandably concerned about
  the levels of radiation in their vicinity. Not only did such citizens quickly
  gain understanding and skills in complex data collection and analysis tech-
  niques, but they also self-mobilised via web-based communication to create a
  collaborative effort at sharing and mapping the relevant data. As noted by
  Kelly (2012),
      With over three million data points collected, this citizen science collective has the
      largest data set of radiation readings in Japan and provides a bold example of the
      potential for citizen science efforts.

     Thus, the incorporation of web-based communication efforts can enhance
  Citizen Science at all levels. Whilst it may seem obvious that
  ‘crowdsourcing’ would benefit via more efficient and accurate data entry
  procedures, the opportunities for effective collaboration and facilitation via
  online means at the ‘extreme’ end are also very apparent.


Top Tips
The final section of this chapter focuses on practical advice relating to three key
stages of web-based communication: designing your online strategy (hopefully
something that is completed prior to implementing any of the tools!); optimising
any websites and other online communications to ensure they reach your intended
audience; and monitoring and evaluating the effectiveness of your activities once
they are implemented. As with the rest of this chapter, reference is made to key
resources and useful guides in order to provide further practical advice for anyone
wishing further information on any of these topics.



Designing a Communication Strategy
Effective forward planning will ensure that your web-based science communication
efforts are well thought through and encounter as few problems as possible. A
dedicated communication strategy will also give you an opportunity to discuss your
intended approaches (and their likely outcomes) with the other people who are
likely to be involved – whether they be your colleagues, senior management or even
your intended audiences.
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                                   241



     Steps to Preparing a Communication Strategy

     • Identifying your audience(s).
     • Clarifying the purpose and/or intended outcomes – what do you want to
       achieve (and why); how does this fit in with your/your organisation’s
       wider aims?
     • Outlining the main approaches/media that are most suitable to achieving
       the above – make sure you target the right media with the right messages to
       successfully reach your intended audiences.
     • Detailing the necessary resources and/or responsibilities – who will do
       what when to achieve your strategy?
     • Including adequate time (and effort!) for regularly reviewing your pro-
       gress – have you succeeded (and what evidence do you have); how could
       you further improve your approaches?


   You may also find it useful to review the following examples of existing digital
media communication strategies: Tate (the umbrella body responsible for the
national collection of British art, consisting of multiple art galleries and museums
around the country) has published their Tate Online Strategy 2010–2012,11 as well
as their Tate Social Media Communication Strategy 2011–2012.12 The Health
Service Executive in Ireland has also made their communication strategy public,13
and a database exists of social media policies from around the world.14

Search Engine Optimisation
Once you have a web-based channel up and running, it is of course crucial to ensure
that it is found by your intended audiences. The dominance of Google and other
search engines has meant that it is increasingly important that your site (or blog
or. . .) is optimised for search engines – that your link will appear more frequently
above others in the list of results. Google has provided their own advice15 on this
front, and there are plenty of companies who claim that they can ‘guarantee’ to get
your site out on top (for a fee and with somewhat limited success); however, the
most important factors to include are shown in the box. Most importantly, don’t be
tempted to tailor your site purely to search engines – remember that the ultimate
users of your site are your audience(s), not computers!

      Success Factors for Search Engine Optimisation

      • Frequently updated content
      • High quality content (interesting topics covered, good spelling and gram-
        mar, no obvious ‘gaps’, etc.)
      • Limited ‘click-throughs’ (your users should find what they are looking for
        within 2–3 ‘clicks’ of their mouse, no more)
      • Links to other popular sites
      • Other popular sites linking to you
      • Relevant page titles and use of key words throughout – when done well
        this will attract the search engines to your site if anyone enters those
        specific keywords


Tools for Evaluating and Monitoring Success
As with any evaluation strategy, when attempting to evaluate and monitor the
success of any online communication, both metrics and impacts are important
(RAEng, n.d.). This means that we need to capture both basic numerics (visitor or
follower numbers, dwell times, return visit rates) and deeper indicators of success
(qualitative feedback or evidence of community opinions/experiences from com-
ments, posts, etc.). There is a vast range of tools that are available for such
purposes; the trick is applying such tools appropriately to obtain useful insights.
    Google Analytics is the most widely used web analytics programme, in part because
it is free and relatively easy to install, but also because it provides straightforward
reports. It is however important to install the appropriate plug-in across the entire website
and to keep in mind that this approach won’t capture social media or other elements that
aren’t embedded directly in your main site. It is also very easy to misinterpret Google
Analytics data; Culture24’s extensive review of practices within the UK cultural sector
identified a ‘health checklist’ in this regard in order to make sure that Google Analytics


15
     www.google.com/webmasters/docs/search-engine-optimization-starter-guide.pdf
Web-Based Channels for Science Communication                                           243


sites are set up appropriately. The same study also identified various social media
analytical tools and an appropriate framework for analysing them.16 In general there
are other similar analytical tools for specific web-based channels (e.g. podcasting)
though it’s worth hunting around before you decide on a specific host platform in
order to make sure it supports the monitoring functionality you desire.
    As adeptly described by Nick Scott, the main message when implementing an
online monitoring and evaluation strategy is to ‘be pragmatic’ about what you
measure (and why) (Scott, 2012). It is also important to treat tools such as Google
Analytics as just one element of your strategy: other techniques could include
maintaining an ‘impact log’ (an informal record of any feedback received) or
incorporating simple user ratings into your site, as well as more detailed (and
time intensive) approaches such as questionnaires and focus groups (Campbell &
Schryer-Roy, 2008; Connected Educators, 2011).



Next Steps
The most crucial current trend, especially within the Asia-Pacific, is the shift
towards greater emphasis on mobile technologies. This means users are likely to
have more regular access to web-based channels, but likely for shorter periods of
time, over lower bandwidths and with smaller screen sizes. Our web communica-
tions efforts need to follow suit. The other important perspective to keep in mind is
that web-based communication is about engaging with, not just communicating to,
our audiences. Interactivity and two-way communication – responding to reader
comments, inviting contributions from others, etc. – is essential to success within
this medium. You will have certain objectives that you wish to achieve, but always
keep the audience’s interests and needs central to what you are doing.
   Finally, a note on dealing with negative feedback online: it may be tempting to delete
such content (particularly if you disagree with it!); however, this is possibly the worst
step that can be taken. Increasingly Internet-savvy audiences are used to seeing a range of
comments – some useful, some banal and some frankly ridiculous. Suppressing negative
comments can in fact do more damage by reducing audience trust and impacting on the
perceived authenticity of the site (Cottingham, 2011). Furthermore, negative comments
can be not only an opportunity to learn and improve your contributions but also a prompt
for further discussion and exploration by you and your community.
   This chapter has provided a whirlwind tour of the key issues and platforms
relating to web-based science communication channels. Of course the discussions
don’t end here; success will come from identifying how best to apply these ideas to
your own circumstances and interests. The key message here is ‘get involved’. It is
no longer possible for anyone wishing to communicate about scientific topics to
ignore web-based media; they are crucial to engaging audiences of almost all
backgrounds. Start small, start with something you feel passionate about, but the
main thing is to get ‘doing’ web communication as soon as possible.
