Science Communication Through Mobile
Devices

Oum Prakash Sharma




Abstract Traditionally, a variety of ways and means including science fairs and
exhibitions, folk media and mass media like newspapers, magazines, science books,
television, radio, etc. were used for science communication. But the advancements
in information and communication technology (ICT) have opened new dimensions
in the field of science communication. Because of its inherent features of accessi-
bility and affordability, ICT has great potential for effective science communication
in society. At the same time, the increasing number of mobile subscribers across the
world indicates that the mobile phone has not remained the means of communica-
tion only, but it is proving to be an effective tool of teaching-learning all over the
world. It is well recognised that mobile phones have added a completely new
dimension to the teaching-learning process due to a wide range of attributes such
as talk, text, camera, video, radio, the Internet, etc. in an effective way. This
chapter, on the one hand, explores the possibilities of mobile-enabled science
communication in society and, on the other hand, it points out the challenges in
science communication through mobile phones. A review of the innovative exper-
iments on mobile-based science communication being done in different parts of the
world has been covered in the chapter. A brief account about Science@Mobile – an
innovative scheme of science popularisation through mobile in India – has also
been given. Possibilities of using mobile phones for science communication,
creating environmental and health awareness and imparting basic knowledge of
agricultural practices and weather conditions to farmers have also been discussed in
the chapter.




O.P. Sharma (*)
National Centre for Innovations in Distance Education, Indira Gandhi National Open
University, Maidan Garhi, New Delhi 110068, India
e-mail: opsharma@ignou.ac.in

L. Tan Wee Hin and R. Subramaniam (eds.), Communicating Science to the Public,       247
DOI 10.1007/978-94-017-9097-0_15, © Springer Netherlands 2014
248                                                                           O.P. Sharma


Richard Feynman defined science as ‘A way of trying not to fool yourself’. Funny,
but true. Feynman was trying to get across the idea that science is our best way of
understanding the natural world and to reason out the events happening around
us. In fact, science is the foundation of an innovative culture and at the core of
significant decisions that we take in our day-to-day life. For that purpose, citizens
need be provided with the minimum science literacy and practical understanding of
the method of science. In this context, science communication in society plays a
very important role. The basic objectives of science communication are to impart
basic scientific literacy for all and to cultivate scientific temper in society. Spread of
scientific temper in society is much more than the spread of knowledge of science
and technology. In fact, it is an attitudinal change which calls for a particular
outlook and rational pattern of thinking. The concept of scientific temper was
anticipated first by Pandit Jawaharlal Nehru in 1946 in his book Discovery of
India, referring to it as ‘a way of life, a process of thinking, a method of acting
and associating with our fellow men’. Besides collecting knowledge and applying it
in day-to-day life through the method of science, the spirit of inquiry and the
acceptance of the right to question and be questioned are fundamental to scientific
temper. It is, therefore, imperative that scientific temper is strengthened and
diffused widely in our society. Widespread scientific information and unrestricted
science communication through all possible ways and means are, therefore, neces-
sary prerequisites for inculcating scientific temper in our society.
    For the purpose of science communication, a variety of means and ways are
being used, and these include popular science fairs and exhibitions, the Internet,
social media, mobile phones and mass media like news papers, magazines, science
books, television, radio, etc. As far as mass media is concerned, it is the most
effective and influential tool for making information available to people on a cheap
and convenient basis (Bora, 2012). But it is a highly impersonal tool that lacks the
personal touch. However, Bora says that the mass media can reach innumerable
people at an instant, which is difficult to be achieved by a medium that is personally
close to the target people. For effective science communication, we need to exploit
the strengths of all of these means including emerging technologies. It has been
observed that most people are interested in receiving useful information on hap-
penings in the field of science and technology, latest news on inventions and
discoveries, interesting facts, etc. They are also interested in getting scientific
answers and logical solutions to their day-to-day problems, needs and queries. It
is the responsibility of the science communicators to provide facilities to get the
required information as per the choice and convenience of the people. ICT and
mobile technologies have potential to provide individualised science information
anywhere and anytime.
Science Communication Through Mobile Devices                                     249


ICT-Enabled Science Communication

Traditionally, a variety of ways and means including print, electronic and folk
media were used for science communication. But advancements in ICT have
opened new dimensions in the field of science communication. Siddiqui, Siddiqui,
Bano, Khalid, and Butt (2012) report that ICT-enabled public communication of
science and technology is generating knowledge and a rapid awareness in a variety
of areas including rural, agro-based and agro-related disciplines in Pakistan. Fur-
ther, they say that the concept of science communication through electronic media,
particularly cable TV, the Internet and mobile phones, has emerged rapidly over a
short span of time. In fact, after print media, electronic media is the most powerful
and effective means of communication that human history has ever witnessed so
far. This medium has provided web-based science communication systems, ser-
vices and solutions. Now, it has become easy to make the latest research-based
scientific findings available to the common people right from the research labs and
institutes. Increasing access and affordability of Internet services has opened doors
for ICT-enabled science communication. A number of websites and portals giving
popular science content and the latest science news are now available at the click of
a mouse. In addition to it, web-based discussion forums, chat rooms, Google and
Yahoo groups, and other social groups on various topics related to popular science
are also playing an important role in ICT-enabled science communication. Mittal
and Tripathi (2009) say that telecommunication, especially mobile phones, has the
potential to provide solutions to the existing information asymmetry in the field of
agriculture and environment awareness. While discussing about the potential role
of science communication in Pakistan, Siddiqui et al. (2012) have also pointed out
that the adoption of ICT-enabled science communication is becoming a significant
success factor for protecting the economic and social viability of the agricultural
sector. The findings indicate that the idea of technology-based public science
communication has modernised the local rural community in terms of most recent
awareness regarding agricultural practices to increase their agro-field outputs. As a
result of awareness, farmers can handle their crops against deadly diseases in a
better way and can take proper measures for protection of their crops.
   Earlier, the study by Mohammad and Garfoth (1995) has also indicated that the
use of latest technologies and mass media like radio, television and print media,
including the Internet and mobile communication, plays an important role in
effective science communication. The findings of the study by Siddiqui
et al. (2012) show that in addition to other means of information sharing, the
Internet, cable TV, mobile phone and radio are the significant sources of commu-
nicating scientific information to farmers in an effective way. While ICT offers a
useful means to support accessibility, and the exchange and sharing of information,
the cognitive complexity of these communication processes often remain
undetermined (Alpay et al., 2004). However, because of its inherent features of
accessibility and affordability, ICT has great potential for effective healthcare
communication and creating environmental awareness in society.
250                                                                        O.P. Sharma


From Mobile Communication to Mobile Learning

The increasing number of mobile subscribers across the world indicates that the
mobile is not now the symbol of status or urbanisation. In fact, it has become a
necessity of the people. It did not remain the means of communication, but it is
proving to be an effective tool for teaching-learning all over the world. It is well
recognised that mobile phones have added a completely new dimension to the
teaching-learning process due to a wide range of attributes such as talk, text,
camera, video, radio, the Internet, etc. in an effective way.
    The International Telecommunication Union (2011) estimates that at the end of
2011, there were around 6 billion mobile subscribers, which is equivalent to 87 % of
the world population. Interestingly, there is a huge increase in the number of mobile
subscribers from 4.7 billion in 2009 to 5.4 billion in 2010. At the end of 2011, there
were 4.5 billion mobile subscriptions in the developing world, which is around
76 % of global subscriptions. According to this report, mobile penetration in the
developing world is now 79 %. Mobile subscription in developed countries has
reached its saturation point, with at least one cell phone subscription per person. But
in the developing countries, led by rapid mobile phone adoption in China and India,
the rate of annual subscription is increasing at a very fast rate. Another research
(Portio Research – in the excellent free Mobile Factbook 2012) predicts that mobile
subscribers worldwide will reach 6.5 billion by the end of 2012, 6.9 billion by the
end of 2013 and 8 billion by the end of 2016. In view of these estimates, it may not
be wrong to expect the subscription penetration to reach 100 % soon. The number
of mobile subscribers in India alone in September 2012 was 906.62 million.
However, active mobile subscriptions in September 2012 were 698.96 million, or
about 77.1 % of the total connections. Mobile connections per 100 people in India
were at 74.49 as of September 2012. Mobile tele-density in urban areas was 154.64,
as compared to 39.52 in rural areas.
    The figures indicate that mobile phones have penetrated into the remotest areas
and the number of users is increasing year after year. Moreover, because of its
value-added functions and widespread appeal, mobile phones have the potential to
be used for teaching-learning as well as for spreading knowledge of science in
society. There is increasing use of mobile phones, tablet PCs, iPads and other
mobile devices by the new generation and individuals in the field of business,
teaching-learning, industry and communication across the world. Mobile technol-
ogies are being used to provide learners with increased access to information and
learning materials and to support learning while working at anytime from anywhere
rather than from a specific location at a certain time (Ally and Laughtons, 2006).
Mobile technologies can not only provide unique learning opportunities, but they
can offer geographically dispersed people with new opportunities to connect and to
create (Downes, 2005).
    Because of its built-in learning strategies, mobile learning (m-learning) is
becoming a popular and convenient means for the delivery of electronic learning
materials on mobile devices. Unlike e-learning, m-learning allows teaching-
Science Communication Through Mobile Devices                                     251


learning to occur anywhere and at anytime. However, because of the small input
and output components of mobile technology, there are design implications for
learning materials for m-learning. Mobile learning has increasingly attracted the
interest of educationists, researchers and companies that develop learning systems
and publish instructional materials (Uzunboylu, Cavus, & Ercag, 2009). This
technology provides the potential for collaborative interaction and learning oppor-
tunities for geographically dispersed persons and groups (Bistrom, 2005; Edwards
et al., 2002). Although currently applied in small-scale projects, mobile learning is
potentially useful in more educational settings. Small and familiar to students,
mobile telephones do not require technological training, do not intimidate users
and remain unobtrusive in classrooms (Nyiri, 2003). Current research has
capitalised on these advantages with public discourse within disadvantaged com-
munities (Ananny, Strohecker, & Biddick, 2004), delivering content (Hoppe,
Joiner, Milrad, & Sharples, 2003) and supporting disadvantaged youth by teaching
literacy and numeracy skills (Mitchell & Doherty, 2003). Because of its value-
added functions and wider spread, the mobiles have the potential to be used for
teaching-learning purposes. Mobile technologies, if implemented thoroughly and
well, may potentially increase efficiency and productivity within the commercial
education sectors (Matesic, 2003).
    Recently, short message service (SMS) and wireless application protocols
(WAP), two types of wireless data communication, have gained increased global
popularity, although their use in online education has been limited (Motiwalla,
2007). The common use of mobile phones and messaging for facilitating friend-
ships and socialisation (Taylor & Harper, 2002) has established a role for the
mobile telephone as a means of collaborative learning and communicating basic
information to people. Mobile technologies potentially promote, facilitate and
enhance student collaboration and interaction, processes that serve as means for
accessing, discovering, discussing and sharing environmental concerns via multi-
media messaging services (MMS), SMS, electronic mail or MSN Messenger.
Students can converse with each other, question each other and share opinions
about environmental concerns. Mobile-enabled collaboration could also occur
outside the classroom, not limited by geography, space or time, although traditional
classroom instruction infrequently supports collaboration. Mobile phones with
cameras permit students to photograph interesting images having scientific values
and environmental problems and share them with friends. Based on such photo-
graphs, students can pose questions related to the environment, and thus, they can
collaborate with classmates, learn new knowledge and formulate plans to solve
such issues. Mobile-enabled learning potentially takes learning outside the class-
rooms and into students’ environments, both real and virtual, thus reconceptualising
learning as personal, situational, collaborative and lifelong. Mobile phones have
added a new dimension to science teaching and learning. Kukulska-Hulme and
Traxler (2005) suggest that this is due to their varied functions such as SMS, MMS,
video, camera, Internet, voice recorder, Bluetooth, etc. Students living in remote
locations can use mobile technologies with wireless capabilities to connect with
252                                                                        O.P. Sharma


their peers in other locations. As a result, remote students will feel less isolated,
which could result in more students completing their education (Ally, 2005).
   According to UNESCO’s report on ‘Mobile Learning for Teachers in Latin
America’, the use of mobile phones in education has the potential to make learning
more accessible, collaborative and relevant. Report says that, as a low-cost substi-
tute for computers, mobile phones can increase access to the Internet and digital
educational content, and because the devices are portable, they can facilitate
learning outside as well as inside schools. A study by UNESCO indicates that
mobile learning initiatives across the world included initiatives to enable teachers to
access multimedia resources and present them to students in the classroom, to
collect and share information and to allow students to participate in collaborative
activities, play educational games, reinforce key concepts and exchange informa-
tion with teachers and peers. The report further highlights that mobile technology
has tremendous potential for building ‘inclusiveness’ in educational participation.



Prospects of Mobile Phone-Enabled Science Communication

While the first decade of the twenty-first century witnessed ICT-enabled initiatives
for science communication, more recently some initiatives have been taken to
use mobile-enabled science communication in different parts of the world. It is a
fact that mobile technologies, including mobile phones, smart phones, PDAs,
pagers, personal navigation devices, etc., have immense potential for science
popularisation in society. The penetration rate of mobile phones throughout the
world is very high, and unlike computers, mobiles are affordable for the majority of
the population. Moreover, the standard mobile phones are particularly well suited
for use in rural areas, where educational resources are scarce and fixed broadband
connections are unavailable or unreliable. With around six billion mobile phone
subscriptions worldwide, these devices present an unprecedented opportunity to
spread minimum science literacy in society in a cheaper, ubiquitous and more
portable format than even low-cost computers. Mobile devices will not only help
in spreading scientific knowledge, including science news and updates, among the
people through mobile service, but they may help in creating awareness about
health, energy and environmental issues of common interest in society. Conse-
quently, mobile devices, particularly mobile phones, may help in creating interest
among people towards science and hence develop a scientifically empowered
society in India. The added advantage of science communication through mobile
phones is that it can target all the people having mobile phones, irrespective of
their age, gender, socioeconomic background and geographical location. It is
highly useful for people, particularly students, who live in rural and remote areas.
   Because of mobility and portability provided by the mobile devices, they can
play an important role in the field of science communication. Considering the
educational potential of mobile devices, Arrigo and Cipri (2010) state that these
Science Communication Through Mobile Devices                                       253


devices add new educational opportunities because of their unique features; they
are personal and portable, and permit new forms of interactions among all involved
in the learning process and respective surrounding environment. Further, according
to Markett, Arnedillo Sánchez, Weber, and Tangney (2006), mobile phone tech-
nology has been considered as the most successful technology for mobile-enabled
learning. In fact, because of the added features like camera, video camera, personal
organiser and web browsing facilities, mobile phones are also considered as
multimedia devices. Moreover, Markett et al. (2006) point out that mobile devices
seem to give their users a very strong sense of control and ownership which they
have highlighted in a research on motivation as a key factor. In this context, a study
on the use of Tablet PCs in schools (Twining, 2005) suggested that ownership was
important for learning in formal settings as in schools as well as in informal learning
outside the schools. Researchers (Jones et al., 2006; Twining, 2005) indicate that
learners often find their informal learning activities more motivating than learning
in formal settings. Delivering the information and instructions on mobile phones
will allow students and people to use their existing mobile technology to access
just-in-time information and learning (Ally and Laughton, 2006).
    In view of the above observations by various researchers, we can say that if
mobile devices are used for science communication in an informal way, they will
not only be motivating but will also be more effective tools of science
popularisation. As the mobile phone allows communication among learners, it
enables collaborative learning also. In fact, it encourages peer interaction and
learning from each other. Mobile devices can be used for science communication
mainly in two ways. The first way is just like mobile learning being practised all
over the world. In this case, the basic concepts of science can be communicated in
the form of interesting games, videos, voice mail or even images like cartoons, etc.
This format can make science communication through mobile a fun and joyful
experience. The latest mobile phones, having access to the Internet, can be used to
access web-based scientific information, and also it can help users to share ideas
and scientific experiences with others. Some interesting apps on basic concepts of
science can be developed and be made available for download on smart mobile
devices. But this kind of mobile-enabled science communication may not be
possible through standard mobile phones having no smart phone features. Thus, it
may not be accessible to all those who actually need to be communicated about
science.
    About the other way of using mobile phones for science communication is to use
the minimum available features in a normal mobile phone such as text messaging
and voice messaging. Using these facilities, people can receive SMS, listen to voice
mail and reply to SMS, making it interactive. With these minimum features, people
can converse with each other, question each other and share their opinions about
health, science and environmental concerns. It is not only cheaper, but it can reach
even in rural and remote parts of the world, that too with multiple language options,
enabling us to communicate any kind of information relevant to the users as per
254                                                                     O.P. Sharma


their choice and need. For example, with the help of normal mobile phones, the
latest research findings can be communicated to scientists and researchers; general
science information can be communicated to the general public; agriculture- and
weather-related information can be communicated to farmers, and so on.




Some Examples of Mobile Phone-Enabled Science
Communication

In view of the decreasing cost, increasing penetration and adding of new features,
mobile phones are being used for spreading scientific knowledge and cultivating
science temper among people. Review of literature (Abraham, 2007; Holzinger,
Dorner, Fodinger, Valdez, & Ziefle, 2010; Jensen, 2007; Mittal & Tripathi, 2009;
Sharma, 2012; Uzunboylu et al., 2009) reveals that a number of innovative exper-
iments are being done in different parts of the world, where mobile phones are
being used for science communication, creating environmental and health aware-
ness and imparting basic knowledge of agricultural practices and weather condi-
tions to the farmers. A few of such cases are described here:
1. Mobile-enabled weather information to farmers in India
      Jensen (2007) and Abraham (2007) reported that the introduction of mobile
   phones to Kerala fishermen could decrease price dispersion and wastage by
   facilitating the spread of information, which made the markets more efficient
   by decreasing risk and uncertainty. Mobile communication in India is being used
   to prepare farmers and fishermen for the increased severity of monsoons. They
   are able to access weather and crop forecasting through their mobile phones.
   According to a release by India Meteorological Department (IMD), mobile
   phone is one of the best ways of forecasting weather information to the farmers.
   Twice every week, the IMD prepares a weather package for farmers in each of
   the 600 districts across India and sends this package out through SMS text
   messages. These packages include a 5-day forecast, a weekly outlook, maximum
   and minimum temperatures, expected rainfall, cloud cover and surface wind
   humidity. Farmers can use this information when they plant, water, fertilise and
   harvest their crops, hence increase their yields. This innovative use of mobile
   phone for agricultural science communication to the farmers not only creates
   awareness about the basics of agricultural science but gives economic benefit to
   the farmers as well as to the nation.
      According to Mittal and Tripathi (2009), information-based decision-making
   agricultural system is designed to maximise agricultural production and is often
   described as the next great revolution in agriculture. They find that the use of
   mobile phone as a mode of providing agriculture-related information would
   depend on how far the mobile phone network has been able to market informa-
   tion – timely and accurately. The recent introduction of a number of mobile-
Science Communication Through Mobile Devices                                      255


   enabled information services suggests that it is the right time to take a look at
   their impact on the agriculture sector in India. These services deliver a wide
   range of information to the farmers. Their study reveals that almost all small
   farmers reported some increase in convenience and cost savings by using their
   mobile phones as basic communication devices to seek information like input
   availability or market prices.
2. Mobile phones for creating health awareness
      The use of mobile phones and wireless devices offers attractive low-cost, real-
   time ways to create health awareness in society. As mobile devices are
   miniaturised and require less energy to operate, they have the potential to
   bring the research laboratory to the patient in ways never before possible.
   Holzinger et al. (2010) report that the rapid improvement of mobile devices
   regarding their usability and technological abilities as well as their high perva-
   siveness and availability makes them a popular platform for health awareness
   and wellness-supporting applications. They advocate that the mobile phone can
   be an important means for supporting adolescents in leading a healthier lifestyle.
   It can be used for creating awareness about health and hygiene among people.
   Thus, this proactive way of health awareness and healthcare management can be
   an important factor in reducing future healthcare cost and keeping people
   healthy. Special health tips can be communicated to people through SMS and
   voice mail services.
3. Using mobile phones to increase environmental awareness
      Mobile technologies presently promote, facilitate and enhance student col-
   laboration and interaction processes that serve as a means for accessing, discov-
   ering and sharing environmental concerns via MMS, SMS, electronic mail or
   MSN Messenger (Uzunboylu et al., 2009). In their study, Uzunboylu et al. have
   used mobile technology to increase students’ awareness about technology and
   environmental concerns. Their findings indicate that mobile devices provided a
   means to engage both male and female students and also helped in developing
   positive attitude towards maintaining their environment.
4. Mobile-enabled science communication in China
      Mobiles are being used for science communication in China since 2006.
   Residents of the Chinese city of Shanghai are able to improve their knowledge
   by reading text messages sent to their mobile phones. The Shanghai Association
   of Science and Technology (SAST) is running the ‘Messaging Science’ initiative
   with technical support from the Shanghai Communications Administration and
   three major telecommunication companies in the city. According to Chen Jifang,
   SAST’s vice-president, the increasing popularity of text messages in China
   means that sending messages could have a much bigger impact than traditional
   methods of science communication such as fairs and exhibitions. In order to
   prepare content for SMS, SAST involved around 100,000 members in Shanghai
   to contribute interesting science facts based on published information. Scientists,
256                                                                        O.P. Sharma


   university students and others have provided nearly 2,000 messages under
   20 categories of SMS, including biology, aerospace, weather, health, etc.
   Professional science writers have been involved in editing the messages to
   make them more interesting, readable and, in some cases, humorous.
      Initially, the aim was to send 30 free messages on diet, nutrition and exercise
   to about 30,000 SAST members during the 2-week Spring Festival. In this
   context, it was also expected that the recipients would forward the information
   to their friends and family. It was also planned that the association would use a
   survey to assess the response to the first set of messages including how fre-
   quently they were forwarded. It was also aimed at encouraging Shanghai citizens
   to contribute new messages to the programme and had plans to award a prize for
   the best message at the city’s annual Science and Technology Festival.
5. Mobile weather alerts in Africa
      United Nations World Meteorological Organization in partnership with
   Ericson is enabling access to weather and climate services in Africa (Mobile
   Weather Alerts, 2011). During a pilot project in Uganda, weather forecasts and
   warnings were sent to fishermen to Lake Victoria by SMS text message. It is
   reported that every year, around 5,000 fishermen die on the lake as they face
   sudden winds and high waves that cause boats to capsize. By providing weather
   information and warning to the fishermen through mobile phones, they will be
   able to make informed decisions about when and where to fish and whether to go
   out on the lake or seek shelter in safe areas. This mobile-enabled weather
   communication will not only help to save many lives but also enhance the
   livelihoods of communities around the lake, as many fishermen are the sole
   providers of large families. In the long run, the scheme is likely to be extended to
   other communities around the lake, as well as farming communities who depend
   on weather information and warnings. This experiment of communicating
   weather-related scientific information is an excellent example of mobile-enabled
   science communication in Africa.


Science@Mobile

An Innovative Scheme of Science Communication in India

In order to exploit the potential of the fast spreading mobile technology in the
country, the National Centre for Innovations in Distance Education (NCIDE) at
Indira Gandhi National Open University (IGNOU) in collaboration with Vigyan
Prasar (Department of Science and Technology) Govt. of India has started
Science@Mobile – an innovative scheme of science popularisation through mobile.
The scheme was launched on 29 February 2012. The response of the people towards
the newly launched scheme of science popularisation through mobile was over-
whelming. Within 2 months of the launch of the scheme, around 60,000 people had
Science Communication Through Mobile Devices                                      257


subscribed, which included 37,850 through mobiles and around 21,300 through the
Internet. The Internet subscribers included 65 % students and 34 % others, which
indicate that the scheme is most liked by the school children. Such a big response is
highly encouraging, and it shows that the majority of the people want to gain
knowledge at all stages provided they get the right kind of information relevant
for them.
   While initiating the scheme, it was thought that in view of the decreasing cost,
increasing penetration and adding of new features, the mobile phone could be a very
effective means of spreading scientific knowledge and cultivating science temper
among the people. The basic objectives of the scheme included:
• Exploiting the potential of mobile phones for science popularisation in society
• Spreading scientific knowledge, including science news and updates among
  people through mobile devices
• Creating awareness about health, energy and environmental issues of common
  interest in society through mobile devices
• Creating interest among people towards science and hence developing a scien-
  tifically empowered society in India
    The target group for the Science@Mobile scheme basically includes all people
irrespective of age, gender, socioeconomic background and geographical location.
However, the content is categorised for three categories of people. The first
category includes the less educated common man who needs basic scientific
literacy. This category includes housewives, farmers and other employed or unem-
ployed people of any background. The second category is meant for educated
people having no science background. The third category is for educated people
having science background, and this may include the researchers and scientists also.
    The scheme of Science@Mobile has certain innovative features. One of the
important features of the scheme is that there is no fee for subscribing science SMS,
and it can be subscribed through mobile as well as through the Internet from
anywhere and anytime. Another unique feature is that people have the option to
select what type of information they would like to receive on their mobile as per
their choice and interest. Also they can select the frequency of receiving the SMS,
i.e. daily, weekly or twice a week. There is also provision for getting feedback from
subscribers. The subscriber has also the option to unsubscribe from the service
anytime from anywhere. The most important feature of the Science@Mobile
scheme is automation in terms of registration, choice of type of SMS, frequency
to receive SMS and 24  7 availability of the service. For this purpose, a specially
designed software has been developed by NCIDE.
    In the first phase of the scheme, eight different types of SMS items are being
delivered through mobiles, which include interesting science facts, science quota-
tions, latest science news, information about scientists, science humour, health tips,
green tips and events, and days of scientific importance. In the next phase, it is
planned to add more categories of SMS such as agriculture, energy, careers in
science, etc. In order to ensure quality and correctness of the SMS content, each and
every SMS item used for Science@Mobile is reviewed and edited by a group of
258                                                                       O.P. Sharma


experts. Efforts are made to make the SMS content interesting, informative and
useful to the subscribers in terms of imparting knowledge of science and inculcating
scientific temper among the people.
    In order to make it interactive, mobile-based science quiz is also planned for the
next phase, which is aimed at developing interest in science and its application in
day-to-day life. Presently, the scheme is available in English, but the Hindi version
of the SMS is also under development so as to reach out to a larger section of
society. It is also planned that with the support of state-level agencies, the scheme
may be extended in the regional languages also. Further, it will be made interactive,
i.e. the users can respond to the information received on their mobile.



Challenges in Mobile-Enabled Science Communication

No doubt, the mobile phone has the potential to be an effective means of science
communication. However, it has certain limitations to reach out to all the people,
particularly in the diversified Indian context. One of the challenges in science
communication through mobile is illiteracy. Many people in rural areas are not
able to read SMS texts. Though they are comfortable in opening and forwarding the
text messages, they are unable to read them because most of the SMSes are in
English. Even, if the SMS are delivered in Hindi, it may not be read and understood
by many people due to language barriers. India, being a multilingual country, has
several regional languages, and even the literate people, particularly in rural areas,
have problems in reading SMS in Hindi also. Therefore, it becomes imperative to
develop and deliver SMS in the local language of the users.
   Another challenge in mobile-enabled science communication is to reach the still
unreached population. Though the penetration of mobile phone is fast increasing
day by day, a large chunk of the population is still out of reach of the mobile
technologies. Availability, access and speed of connectivity to mobile phone
networks in different parts of the country are also big challenges. This limits the
opportunities for mobile-enabled science communication and mobile learning
programmes that rely on smart phones or require high-speed connectivity. The
problem of access and speed mainly affects isolated and rural areas.
   Development of user-friendly and interesting SMS items for mobile science
communication is also a very challenging task. These items have not only to be very
simple but also quite informative and be able to create interest in science. Normally,
there is a limit on the number of characters that can be included in an SMS item;
therefore, it becomes very difficult to prepare an SMS that conveys the meaning and
purpose of the message in a limited number of words.
   Conclusively, it can be said that because of its multiple utility, the mobile phone
has emerged as an all-purpose device having the potential to be used for entertain-
ment, information sharing, interaction, chatting, communication, etc. Moreover,
with the advancements in the field of technology, multimedia and voice-based
interfaces have been developed to increase access to information and knowledge.
Science Communication Through Mobile Devices                                                259


Aimed at illiterate people, the use of interactive voice response (IVR) systems can
be quite useful for communicating information related to science, health, agricul-
ture, environment, etc. IVR-based science communication can be a more effective
and efficient solution to deal with the barriers of illiteracy and regional language
disparities. In order to make mobile-based science communication more effective,
the language of the SMS or voice response should be local using colloquial phrases
and regional dialects which are easy to be understood by the user. Language of the
SMS text should be simple and clear, having no unexplained technical term, and the
text should be broken into smaller sentences for better comprehension.
   Despite the barriers and challenges as described above, experts are optimistic
about the potential of mobile-enabled science communication to improve basic
knowledge of the people and to motivate them towards science, especially in the
communities where educational opportunities are restricted. Mobile-enabled sci-
ence communication has the potential to address specific educational needs all over
the world from increasing basic science literacy and basic education skills for
vulnerable populations to improving their knowledge of science and developing a
scientifically empowered society.
